PREFACE 

This book is intended for use by both students and lawyers. The law of civil practice and procedure of the state of New York is outlined and discussed from the standpoint of the statutes; and interpretations and modifications of the statutes by judicial decisions are set forth as condensed annotations. The statutes include the 1930 amendments, and the decisions have been traced for appeals and reversals to the date of publication. 

The material is arranged in the order logically followed in the bringing of an action. Unlike other books on the subject, each proposition of law includes not only the supreme court practice, but any differences encountered in the city and municipal courts. It is hoped that the simple and concise presentation will be helpful to the student, and that the elucidation of many perplexing problems not treated in other works will be beneficial to the lawyer. 

August, 1930.

THE AUTHOR.

CHAPTER I INTRODUCTION §1. General Provisions. 

There are many topics of procedure which apply generally to the subjects to be considered in the following chapters. These topics include: civil statutes, classification of civil proceedings, representation by attorney, affidavits, motions, orders, stipulations and the service and filing of papers. 

§2. Sources of the Law of Civil Procedure. 

Almost all of the statutory law of civil procedure is found in the following acts of the legislature and amendments thereto: 

(1) The Civil Practice Act (L. 1920, Ch. 925, in effect 

October 1, 1921). 

(2) The Surrogate's Court Act (L. 1920, Ch. 928, in effect October 1, 1921). (3) The Court of Claims Act (L. 1920, Ch. 922, in effect 

April 15, 1921). 

(4) The Justice Court Act (L. 1920, Ch. 937, in effect 

October 1, 1921). 

(5) The New York City Court Act (L. 1926, Ch. 539, in effect January 1, 1927). 

(6) The New York City Municipal Court Code (L. 1915, 

Ch. 279, in effect September 1, 1915). (7) Other local court acts and city charters. 

In addition, there are: 

(1) The Rules of Civil Practice (adopted by a conven tion held pursuant to L. 1920, Ch. 902, in effect October 1, 1921). 1

(2) Rules of the various courts.

The Rules of Civil Practice must conform to the Civil 

Practice Act. O'Brien v. Lashar, 206 App. Div. 623, 199 

N. Y. S. 34, aff'd. 236 N. Y. 602, 142 N. E. 301. 

§3. Construction. 

The tendency in this state is toward a more and more liberal construction of laws relating to procedure. The Civil 

Practice Act specifically provides that the act shall be liberally construed, and also that the rule that a statute in derogation of the common law should be strictly construed does not apply. (C. P. A. §§2, 3.) The New York City Municipal 

Court Code states that its provisions must be liberally construed in the furtherance of justice. (Mun. Ct. Code §180.) "The statutes of reason are more permanent and immutable than those of the legislature." Benedict v. 

Seymour, 6 How. Pr. 298, 304. "All procedure is merely a methodical means whereby the court reaches out to restore rights and remedy wrongs; it must never become more important than the purpose which it seeks to accomplish. Unless some necessary requirement has been omitted, a wrong move or a mistake in the method of seeking relief from the courts ought not to furnish protection for a wrongful act." Clark v. Kirby, 243 N. Y. 295, 303, 153 N. E. 79. 

§4. Mistakes and Amendments. 

In order that a just cause of action or defense may not fail because of a mistake, omission, irregularity or defect, the court his discretion to allow a correction at any stage of the case or to order that the error be disregarded. (C. P. 

A. §§105-112; Mun. Ct. Code §93.) 85. Extension of Time. 

After the commencement of an action or proceeding the court or a judge may enlarge the time prescribed by statute, rule or order for the doing of any act therein upon just

INTRODUCTION 

3 terms, even though the specified period has expired. But a court or judge may not enlarge the time fixed within which to commence an action, take an appeal or to continue an action. (C. P. A. §§98, 99.) §6. Judicial Proceedings. 

The various proceedings which may be brought in a civil court for the adjustment of controversies are the following: (1) An action. (2) A special proceeding. 

(3) Arbitration. 

(4) Submission of the controversy to the court. (5) Provisional remedies and replevin. (6) State writs. 

87. An Action. 

An action is "an ordinary prosecution in a court of justice by a party against another party for the enforcement of a right, the redress or prevention of a wrong or the punishment of a public offense." (C. P. A. §4.) There are two kinds of actions, civil and criminal. A civil action is any action not prosecuted in the name of the people of the state against a party charged with a crime. (C. P. A. §§4; 6.) 

§8. Civil and Criminal Prosecutions. 

If the violation of a right is prohibited by penal law and also gives rise to a civil action for damages under the common or statutory law, the two prosecutions are not merged. 

Whether or not the wrong-doer is prosecuted criminally, the civil remedy remains. (C. P. A. §9; Penal Law §§23, 24.) 

An action for misappropriation of money may be brought in the civil courts after defendant's conviction for grand larceny of the same money and during his freedom on bail pending appeal. Shaul v. Fidelity & De posit Co. of Maryland, 131 Misc. 401, 227 N. Y. S. 163.

89. Law and Equity.

One of the chief reforms attempted by the various codifications of the law of procedure has been the abolition of the difference between actions at law and suits in equity. 

The Civil Practice Act states that there is only one form of civil action and the Constitution states that testimony in equity cases must be taken in the same manner as in cases at law. (C. P. A. §8; Const. Art. VI, §20.) Difficul ties have arisen in the application of these laws. Because of the almost overwhelming example of precedent, many of the distinctions still remain. 

"Section 3339 of the Code of Civil Procedure (now 

C. P. A. §8) . . . and others, clearly evince the legislative intent to strip our modern procedure of the cumbrous forms and distinctions which made the practice under the common law and the earlier statutes so burdensome in its details and so uncertain in its results." 

Hahl v. Sugo, 169 N. Y. 109, 113, 62 N. E. 135. "The names of actions no longer exist, but we retain in fact the action at law and the suit in equity. The pleader need not declare that his complaint is either; it is only necessary that it should contain facts constituting a cause of action, and if these facts are such as at common law his client would be entitled to judgment, he will under the Code obtain it. If on the other hand they establish a title to some equitable interposition or aid from the court, it will be given by judgment in the same manner as it would formerly have been granted by decree." Stevens v. The Mayor, etc., of New York, 84 N. Y. 

296, 304. 

§10. A Special Proceeding. 

A proceeding brought for any purpose specified in §4 of the Civil Practice Act,¹ which is not an action, is a "special proceeding." (C. P. A. § 5.) 1 See p. 3.

INTRODUCTION 

5 

§11. Arbitration. 

Arbitration is the voluntary submission to one or more arbitrators by two or more persons of any controversy existing between them which might be the subject of an action. 

(C. P. A. §1448.) §12. Submission of a Controversy. 

A submission of a controversy is the voluntary submission of facts to the court with a prayer for the appropriate judgment. This method is used when the parties are agreed as to the facts, but disagree as to the conclusion of law to be derived therefrom. (C. P. A. §§546-548.) §13. Provisional Remedies and Replevin. 

Provisional remedies are proceedings commenced concurrently with an action for the purpose of preventing a defendant from absconding or from disposing of or injuring his property or to obtain other temporary relief before a judgment can be obtained in an action. These remedies are: (1) An order of arrest, directing that the party be imprisoned pending the suit, unless bail be given for his appearance. (2) A warrant of attachment, directing that the property of the defendant be seized and held, pending the outcome of the suit. (3) An injunction order, directing the defendant to do or refrain from doing some act in relation to the property or question in suit. (4) The appointment of a receiver to take care of the property in dispute pending the suit.

Replevin, now called an action to recover a chattel, includes preliminary relief as does a provisional remedy. The action is begun in the regular way, but pending the suit a requisition may be given the sheriff requiring him to seize

the chattel in the possession of the defendant which the plaintiff alleges belongs to him. The chattel, or an undertaking given in its place, is held until the right to possession is determined. 

§14. State Writs. 

State writs are commands issuing from the state, under seal of the court, directed to some person, body, court or officer, commanding immediate obedience to the directions contained in the writ. Under the Civil Practice Act, there are only two state writs: (1) Habeas Corpus, to bring up a person and inquire into the cause of his detention. (2) Certiorari, to inquire into the cause of detention. 

Other proceedings formerly brought on by state writs but now brought on by order in the same manner are: (1) Certiorari to review. (2) Mandamus. (3) Prohibition. 

§15. Representation by Attorney.2 

Although most parties to litigation are represented by attorneys, such representation is not required. The statute specifically provides that a person of full age who has not been judicially declared to be incompetent to manage his affairs may prosecute or defend a civil action in person. As a general rule all the provisions relating to an attorney in an action apply to a person appearing on his own behalf. 

But if a party has retained an attorney to appear for him, he cannot act in person in the action without the consent of the court. (C. P. A. §236.)

In the event of the death, removal, suspension or other 

2 For proof of authority in real property actions, see p. 274.

INTRODUCTION 

7 circumstance which disables an attorney from acting at any time before judgment, no further proceeding may be taken in the action against the party represented by such attorney for 30 days after notice to appoint another attorney has been given to the party. (C. P. A. §240.)

Formerly a substitution of attorneys could be made only upon order. The attorney of record for an adult party or a corporation may now be changed by filing with the clerk of the court a written stipulation for the change, signed by the attorney and signed and acknowledged by the party. (Rule 

56, as amended by the appellate division.) 

§16. Contingent Fees. 

An agreement between an attorney and his client which specifies that the attorney's compensation is dependent upon the success in the prosecution or is to be a percentage of the monies recovered by judgment or settlement is not illegal, but is often looked upon with suspicion. When such contract is made between an attorney and a guardian ad litem of an infant, the contract and a petition for suitable compensation must be submitted to the court. After taking proof of the services rendered, the court fixes the fee to be paid out of the infant's estate. The attorney may recover only the amount fixed by the court. (Jud. L. §474.) In the first judicial department any agreement for the payment of a contingent fee after judgment or settlement of a claim for damages for personal injuries or for death or loss of services resulting from personal injuries, must be filed with the clerk of the appellate division within 10 days of the making of the agreement. The retainer filed must contain the date of the agreement, the name and address of the attorney and the client, the date and place of the occurrence of the personal injury, the terms of the compensation, and the signature of the attorney. (Special Rules, First Jud. 

Dept., 4-a.)

§17. Affidavits. 

An affidavit is a written statement sworn to by the person making it. A verified pleading and a verified petition are “affidavits." (C. P. A. §7.) Any proceeding which may be instituted by petition under the statutes or Rules of Civil Practice may be brought by affidavit and notice of application and vice versa. (C. P. A. §119.) 

§18. Motions. 

A motion is not an action or proceeding in itself but is a means of applying for a remedy which is incidental to and dependent on an action or proceeding. The statute defines a motion as an application for an order (C. P. A. §113) because, if the motion is granted, the court or justice orders that the relief applied for be given. 

§19. The Demurrer. 

The demurrer has been abolished, but an objection in point of law may be taken on motion. Sections 277 to 283 of the Civil Practice Act provide for the present motions, in lieu of demurrers. (Rothenberg v. Metzger, 227 App. 

Div. 444, 238 N. Y. S. 139.) 

§20. Notice. 

Ordinarily, notice of motion must be given the adverse party. Even though no provision for notice appears in the statute, in every case where the adverse party or his rights would be affected, notice must be given. Unless some other time is specified in the statute, notice in the supreme and county courts must be given at least 8 days before the date set for hearing, except where all the attorneys have their offices in the same city or village, where 5 days' notice may be given. In the city court 4 days' notice and in the municipal court 3 days' notice is sufficient. Where service of notice is made by mail, 3 days must be added to the

INTRODUCTION 

specified time. (C. P. A. §§114; 164; Rule 60; City Ct. Act 

§29; Mun. Ct. Rules XII.) §21. Order to Show Cause. 

The time of notice may be shortened by obtaining, upon affidavits showing sufficient reason and the stage of the action or proceeding, an order to show cause. (Rule 60; City 

Ct. Act §29.) An order to show cause is equivalent to a notice of motion and the subsequent proceedings are the same. (Jud. L. §761.) 

§22. Ex Parte Motions. 

Ex parte motions are made without notice. An affidavit must be submitted showing whether or not a previous application for the same relief has been made; and if so, to what court or judge, what determination was made, and what new facts exist to explain or justify the subsequent application. (Rule 61.) 

§23. Application for Relief. 

The moving party may specify, in his notice of motion, one or more kinds of relief in the alternative or otherwise. 

When 8 days' notice is given, the adverse party must serve copies of his affidavits and papers in opposition upon the attorney for the moving party at least one day before the date set for the hearing. At least 3 days before the hearing the adversary may serve upon the attorney for the moving party a demand for relief, whether or not such relief is responsive to the motion. (C. P. A. §117.) If the notice of motion was served at least 10 days before the hearing, it may require that the answering affidavits be served not less than 5 days before the hearing. When answering affidavits are served, replying affidavits may be served 2 days before the hearing. (Rule 64.)

§24. Hearing. 

A motion is usually made to the court or a justice thereof. If the defendant has defaulted in appearing, the application may be made to a judge out of court. In the first judicial district, any motion may be made to a judge out of court, except for a new trial on the merits. (C. P. A. §§115, 116.) After a hearing on a motion, any party may make a motion for reargument. If the application has been refused in whole or in part or granted on conditions or on terms, a new application may be made to the same judge only. (C. P. A. §118.) The moving party must furnish the papers necessary to the hearing unless they are in the possession of the adverse party, when a notice to produce is served with motion papers. On the hearing, the pleadings are before the court. (Rule 65.) $25. Default on Motion.

If the moving party defaults in appearance at the hearing, the motion is denied. Copies of the papers served must be filed. If the opposing party defaults in appearance or fails to serve the papers demanded in the notice, the motion is usually granted. In this case the papers served and proof of service must be furnished. (Rule 66.) 

§26. Orders. 

A direction of a court or judge in an action or special proceeding, unless contained in a judgment, is an order. 

Unless entered in the minutes on a trial, an order must be written. (C. P. A. §127.) §27. Court and Judge's Orders. 

The difference between a court order and a judge's order is now almost non-existent. An order which may be made by a judge in chambers, may be made by the court. In the supreme court an order authorized to be made by the court,

INTRODUCTION 

11 may be made out of court unless the application is made outside the county or judicial district. A judge's order in the county court may be made by a justice of the supreme court or by a county judge of any other county. Unless a particular judge is designated by law, an order in an action in any court other than the county court may be made by a judge of the court anywhere in the state; or by a justice of the supreme court or by the county judge of the county where the action is triable or the attorney for the applicant resides, except an order to stay proceedings after verdict, report or decision. (C. P. A. §§128, 129, 130.)

There is no longer any distinction between a judge's order and a court order in the supreme court. Rockwood & Co., Inc., v. Trop, 211 App. Div. 421, 207 N. Y. S. 507. 

§28. City Court Orders. 

When a statute states that an order in the city court of the city of New York should be made by the court, it may be made by a justice out of court, unless the order is made outside the county specified by the statute. (City Ct. 

Act §35.) 

$29. Form.

The order consists of a memorandum of the determination of the motion, a recital of the papers used and the judge's signature or initials. It may be endorsed upon or appended to the motion papers. (Rule 70.) 

§30. Settlement and Resettlement. 

If an opinion is handed down and a direction made to enter an order in accordance therewith, the formal order is drawn up, usually by the successful party, and submitted for "settlement" on 2 days' notice to the adversary. If the proposed order does not, in the view of the adverse party, conform to the opinion, the latter may submit a counter-

order at the time of settlement. If an order actually signed is alleged to be incorrect, either party may move for a resettlement on 2 days' notice. (Rule 70; Rules of the courts.) 

§31. Stipulations. 

Unless some other interest is affected, relief which may be obtained by order, may be had by agreement of the attorneys for the parties. The agreement is called a stipulation and must be in writing. (Rule 4.) Examples of the use of stipulations are: to extend the time to plead, to submit a controversy to the court, to take testimony by deposition and to settle and discontinue actions. 

§32. Service of Papers. 

When a party has appeared by attorney the ordinary papers in an action or proceeding must be served on the attorney. The papers which must be served on the party or other person include: the summons, a subpoena, an order directing a personal act, a paper to bring a person into contempt and an order in a provisional remedy. (C. P. A. §163; 

Rules 20, 21.) 

§33. Mode of Service. 

An ordinary paper in an action or proceeding may be served on an attorney as follows: (1) By delivering it to him personally. (2) By depositing it properly inclosed in a postpaid wrapper in a post-office or post-office box directed to the attorney. When service must be made within a specified time and service is made by mail, 3 days must be added to the time prescribed, except a notice of trial in the supreme court which must be made through the post-office not less than 14 days before trial, including the date of service. (3) By leaving it in the attorney's office with his partner or clerk or other person in charge. If no person is in

INTRODUCTION 

13 charge and the service is made between six o'clock in the morning and nine o'clock in the evening, the paper may be left in a conspicuous place in such office. (4) If the office is not open, by depositing it in a sealed wrapper directed to the attorney in his letter-drop or -box, accessible from outside the office; or by leaving it at his residence within the state with a person of suitable age and discretion. (5) If the office is not open and there is no letter-drop or -box and there is no person of suitable age and discretion at his residence, by leaving it with the clerk of the court in which the action or proceeding is pending. (Rules 20, 21; 

C. P. A. §164.) 834. Filing Papers. 

The summons and pleadings in an action must be filed with the clerk within 5 days after demand by the adverse party. Upon failure to comply, the court may direct that the paper be abandoned absolutely or on failure to file within a specified time. (C. P. A. §100.) A return or other paper in a special proceeding must be filed. (C. P. A. §101.)

CHAPTER II  Abatement §35. Purpose of the Statute of Limitations. 

The time within which an action may be brought has been limited by statute. The time of the courts cannot be wasted over stale claims, nor shall a party be compelled to search for evidence after a great lapse of time. (Conkling v. Weatherwax, 181 N. Y. 258, 73 N. E. 1028.) 

§36. Nature. 

The Statute of Limitations never pays nor discharges a debt, but affects the remedy only. If one remedy on the debt is barred by statute and another is not, a creditor may enforce his claim by means of the remedy which is not barred. (House v. Carr, 185 N. Y. 453, 78 N. E. 171.) "A lien on property, real or personal, given as security for a debt, is not impaired by the fact that the remedy at law for the recovery of the debt is barred by statute.

Moreover the pledgor cannot recover possession of the pledged property without paying the debt." Harvey v. Guaranty Trust Co., 134 Misc. 417, 434, 236 N. Y. S. 37, 60. See Hulbert v. Clark, 128 N. Y. 

295, 28 N. E. 638. §37. Waiver.

As a matter of public policy, the courts will not allow a party to waive his right under the Statute of Limitations by provision in the original contract. "A party may, undoubtedly, without trenching upon public policy, waive the defense of the statute of limitations, by omitting to set up the defense when sued . . . . 14

but no case has occurred to me in which a party can, in advance, make a valid promise that a statute founded in public policy shall be inoperative." Shapley v. Abbott, 42 N. Y. 443, 452. 

§38. The Statute and the United States. 

The New York Statute of Limitations cannot apply to an action brought by the United States.

A claim of the U. S. is not affected by the Statute of 

Limitations; and acts of the U. S. Shipping Board Emer gency Fleet Corporation were acts of the U. S., since the corporation was the agent of the government. U. S. v. 

Brown, 247 N. Y. 211, 160 N. E. 13. 

§39. An Action Not Limited. 

The only action not limited is one upon a bill or note or other evidence of debt issued or put in circulation as money. 

(C. P. A. §58.) §40. Within Forty Years. 

An action affecting real property, when the state or one claiming under it is plaintiff, must be brought within forty years after such cause of action accrued. (C. P. A. §31.) 

§41. Within Twenty Years. 

When a private person is plaintiff the action must be brought within twenty years. (C. P. A. §34.) A person can thus acquire a title to real property by adverse holding of the same for twenty years, since the courts will then refuse to help the true owner to regain possession, and even an actual entry by him upon the property would not be sufficient to revest the title. (C. P. A. §36.) 

If possession has been taken by one who founded his claim upon a written conveyance or upon the decree or judgment of a competent court, the actual possession of a part of the premises is deemed to have given constructive pos-

session of the remainder. But this is not true if the tract is divided into lots, for then the possession of one lot is not deemed the possession of any other lot. (C. P. A. §37.)

Where adverse possession is not founded upon a written instrument or a judgment or decree, the premises actually occupied and no others are deemed to be held. (C. P. A. §39.) 

Where title is founded upon a written instrument, judgment or decree, the land is deemed to have been possessed and occupied if it has been (1) usually cultivated or improved, (2) protected by substantial inclosure, or (3) used for the supply of fuel or fencing timber. (C. P. A. §38.)

Where there is no claim under written instrument, etc., continued occupation is necessary to adverse holding. Where land has been protected by a substantial inclosure or usually cultivated and improved, it is deemed to be so possessed and occupied. (C. P. A. §§39, 40.) 

Where the relationship has been that of landlord and tenant, the twenty year period begins to run at the termination of the tenancy, or, if there was no written lease, at the time of the last payment of rent, notwithstanding the tenant has acquired another title or has claimed to hold adversely to the landlord. (C. P. A. §41.)

When the entry upon land has been by permission or under some authority from the owner, adverse possession does not commence until such permission or authority has been repudiated, and the possessor thereafter has assumed an attitude of hostility to any right in the real owner. Hinkley v. State of New York, 234 

N. Y. 309, 137 N. E. 599.

Certain time is excluded from the computation of the period of limitation. If the party entitled to maintain an action to recover real property or to interpose a defense or counterclaim founded on title is, when his title first descends or his cause of action accrues, (1) within the age of twenty-one years, (2) insane, or (3) imprisoned on a crim-

inal charge or in execution upon conviction of a criminal offense for a term less than life, the time of such disability is not a part of the time limited, except that such time cannot be extended more than ten years after the disability ceases or after the death of the person so disabled. (C. P. 

A. §43.) 

In determining whether the limitation of the above section has run against an action to recover real property, the rule is: "take the date of the coming age of the infant as the starting point; to that date add twenty years, and from that ascertained date look back to the date when the cause of action accrued, and if said date was within the period of disability, and not more than ten years prior to the infant's coming of age and the suit is brought within twenty years after the coming of age, the statute has not run." Muller v. Manhattan Ry. 

Co., 124 App. Div. 295, 299, 108 N. Y. S. 852, 855. 

§42. Other Actions within Twenty Years. 

The other actions which must be brought within twenty years are: 

(1) Action upon a sealed instrument. (C. P. A. §47.) (2) To redeem from a mortgage. (C. P. A. §46.) 

(3) On a judgment of a court of record. (C. P. A. §44.) (4) On a judgment of a court of this state not of record when docketed in the county clerk's office. (C. P. A. §44.) 

(5) Action for dower. (R. P. L. §460.) 

To constitute an instrument under seal, there must be a seal or scroll affixed. Where one party affixes a seal and the other does not, it is not a sealed instrument with respect to the latter unless he adopts the other's seal as his own. Brooklyn Public Library v. 

City of N. Y., 222 App. Div. 422, 226 N. Y. S. 491, aff'd. 

250 N. Y. 495, 166 N. E. 179; Empire Trust Co. v. 

Heinze, 242 N. Y. 475, 152 N. E. 266. 

843. Within Ten Years.

Equity actions must be brought within ten years. (C. P. 

A. §53.) Ford v. Clendenin, 215 N. Y. 10, 109 N. E. 124;

Dodds v. McColgan, 229 App. Div. 273, 241 N. Y. S. 584. An action in equity may also be barred by laches (delay in bringing the action).

When a complaint states a cause of action at law and also one in equity, the statute of limitations applicable to the action at law governs. Keys v. Leopold, 241 N. Y. 189, 149 N. E. 828.

Where plaintiff's remedy at law was barred by the statute of limitations because of the fraudulent acts of the defendant, plaintiff may recover in equity under §53. Dodds v. McColgan, 134 Misc. 518, 235 N. Y. S. 492, aff'd. 229 App. Div. 273, 241 N. Y. S. 584. 

Where plaintiffs and defendant were co-adventurers, and the defendant bought in property of all of them unbeknown to the plaintiffs, thus defrauding them, an action for an accounting is within the ten-year period, and waiting two years after discovery of the fraud before suing does not bar the plaintiffs. Laches, to bar plaintiffs, must be more than a mere delay. There must be (in addition to delay) such a change in the situation as to make it inequitable to grant the relief sought. 

Seligson v. Weiss, 222 App. Div. 634, 227 N. Y. S. 338.

An action for separation for cruel and inhuman treatment is barred under this ten-year period where the acts upon which the plaintiff bases her claim occurred sixteen years before suit. To maintain her action the plaintiff must show that within the period she offered to resume her duties and demanded that the defendant provide for her. Guerin v. Guerin, 127 Misc. 

745, 217 N. Y. S. 1. 

844. Within Six Years.

Actions which must be brought within six years are the following: (1) Upon a contract obligation or liability, except a judgment or a sealed instrument. (2) Upon a liability created by statute, except a penalty or forfeiture. (3) To recover for an injury to property.

(4) To recover for personal injuries not expressly limited.¹ (5) To recover a chattel. (6) Upon a judgment of a court not of record, if not docketed. (7) To procure a judgment on the ground of fraud. (8) To establish a will. [In cases (7) and (8) the statute begins to run upon discovery.] (C. P. A. §48.) 

$45. Within Five Years. 

Actions which must be brought within five years are: (1) An action to annul a marriage on the ground of physical incapacity must be brought within five years of the marriage. (C. P. A. §1141.) 

(2) An action for divorce must be brought within five years after the discovery by the plaintiff of the defendant's adultery. (C. P. A. §1153.) 

These time provisions are not included in the Statute of Limitations and are considered facts which must be alleged and proved by the plaintiff as a part of his cause of action. 

However, the actions are barred unless brought within the time limit. 

§46. Within Three Years. 

The following actions must be brought within three years: (1) Against a sheriff or other officer for the non-payment of money collected upon an execution. (2) Against a constable for omission of official duty, except an escape. 

1 See p. 20.

(3) For a penalty or forfeiture to a private person aggrieved. (4) Against an executor, administrator, receiver or trustee, for detention or injury to property caused by defendant or the person whom he represents. (5) Upon a personal injury caused by negligence. (6) Against a director or stockholder of a moneyed corporation to recover a penalty or forfeiture or to enforce a statutory liability. (C. P. A. §49.) 

$47. Within Two Years. 

The following must be brought within two years: (1) An action for damages for libel, slander, assault, seduction, battery, false imprisonment, malicious prosecution or malpractice. (2) An action upon a statute for a forfeiture or penalty to the people of the state. (C. P. A. §50.) 

(3) Claims against the state. (Court of Claims Act §12.) 

The two-year limitation applies to an action against chiropractors for malpractice because the action is in tort and cannot be brought on contract. Monohan v. 

Devinney, 131 Misc. 248, 225 N. Y. S. 601. "Malpractice" may be used to indicate corrupt or culpably incompetent practice on the part of a lawyer or doctor. Ordinarily it means improper treatment or culpable neglect of a patient by a physician or surgeon.

It does not apply to the negligence of a nurse. Isenstein v. Malcomson, 227 App. Div. 66, 236 N. Y. S. 641.

The two-year limitation has been applied only to those cases of malpractice which involved injury to the person. Where injuries to property arise from malpractice in auditing accounts, the six-year period under Sec. 

48 applies. Am. Exchange Bank v. Touche, 131 Misc. 

236, 227 N. Y. S. 218. 

§48. Within One Year. 

The following must be brought within one year: (1) Against a sheriff for an omission of duty in his official capacity.

(2) Against any other officer for an escape of a civil prisoner. (C. P. A. §51.) 

(3) For a penalty or forfeiture to a common informer. 

If such a person neglects to sue within one year, the attorney-general may sue within two years. (C. P. A. §52.) (4) Actions or special proceedings against the city of

New York must be commenced within one year after the right of action accrues. (Greater N. Y. Charter §261.)

Although this provision is in the nature of a time limitation, it is construed to be one of the facts which must be proved in establishing a cause of action; and the claim must have been presented to and rejected by the proper officials. 

An action for personal injuries brought by a fifteen year old boy against the city of N. Y. is barred by Sec. 

261 of the Greater N. Y. Charter if not brought within the year. Infancy alone does not create a condition of mental disability so as to excuse noncompliance, and 

Sec. 60 of the C. P. A. does not apply. If the plaintiff does not prove an excuse he is precluded from maintaining the action. Yablonsky v. City of N. Y., 128 

Misc. 469, 219 N. Y. S. 121. 

849. Within Six Months. 

A claim against the state other than for the appropriation of land must be filed within six months. (Court of 

Claims Act §15.) 

850. Within Three Months.

When the claim of a creditor is rejected by an executor or administrator, the claimant must bring his action within three months of such rejection; or, if no part of the debt is due, within two months after a part becomes due. Otherwise the claimant is barred from such action and the claim must await judicial settlement. (Surr. Ct. Act §211.)

§51. Computation. 

It is a question of great importance to know when the time commences to run; and when, if at all, it ceases to run; and in what cases, if any, the time is extended. The following provisions have been made: (1) The period of the limitation must be computed from the time of the accruing of the right to relief by action, except as otherwise provided by statute. (C. P. A. §11.) 

In the case of a note payable on demand, the Statute of Limitations begins to run in favor of the maker from its date, and this is so whether the note be payable with or without interest. McMullen v. Rafferty, 89 N. Y. 456. (2) Where there have been mutual, open, and current accounts and reciprocal demands between the parties, the cause of action is deemed to have accrued from the time of the last item proved in the account on either side. (C. P. 

A. §56.) 

(3) Where an injury results from the act of an agent, the time within which the principal may bring an action against the agent commences from the time of the recovery of a judgment against the principal for the damage caused by the agent. (C. P. A. §14.) 

(4) Where a demand is necessary to entitle a person to maintain an action, the time begins to run as soon as the right to make the demand is complete. (C. P. A. §15.) (5) If, when the cause of action accrues against a person, he is without the state, the action may be commenced, within the time limited therefor, after his return into the state, unless he has designated someone upon whom summons may be served. (C. P. A. §19.)

Sec. 19 applies to nonresidents who have never been in this state. Backus v. Severn, 127 Misc. 776, 216 N. Y. 

S. 381.

Where a nonresident maintains an office in the state for the transaction of his business, which he visits regularly on each business day, his nonresidence does not constitute absence from the state under Sec. 19, and an action on a note brought more than seven years after it was made is barred under Sec. 48. Mack v. Mendels, 249 

N. Y. 356, 164 N. E. 248. (6) When a cause of action arises in another state, the limitation of that state governs except where the cause of action originally accrued in favor of a resident of this state. 

(C. P. A. §13.) 

"The obvious purpose of the provision as a whole is to prevent a non-resident claimant from coming into this State and prosecuting a claim whether against a resident or non-resident under our Statutes of Limitations if they are more favorable to him than the statutes prevailing in the State where the cause of action arose." National Surety Co. v. Ruffin, 242 N. Y. 413, 417, 152 N. E. 246.

Section 13 is of no value to a nonresident plaintiff if the New York period of limitation has run. In an action on contract, a 30-year period under a French statute is not available in our courts after the 6-year period has elapsed. Kahn v. Commercial Union of America, Inc., 227 App. Div. 82, 237 N. Y. S. 94.

Under C. P. A. §§13, 55, where the cause of action for personal injuries to a New York resident arose in 

Connecticut, since the Connecticut limitation (one year) was shorter than the New York limitation (three years), the New York statute should be applied. Backus v. 

Severn, 127 Misc. 776, 216 N. Y. S. 381. See Laurencelle v. Laurencelle, 217 App. Div. 159, 216 N. Y. S. 384, for similar construction in an action on a note. (7) A cause of action, not affecting real property within this state, against a nonresident is governed by the limitation of his state; unless the time limit is less in this state, when the limitation in this state applies. (C. P. A. §55.) 

In general the limitation of the state where the action is tried governs, rather than that of the state

where the contract was made. The policy of the C. P. A. 

Secs. 13, 55, recognizing foreign statutes of limitations, is held not to lengthen the time of the N. Y. Statute, but to provide additional limitations. Duggan v. Lubbin, 131 Misc. 154, 226 N. Y. S. 238.

Section 55 is modified by section 19, so that a nonresident who was without the state at the time the cause of action arose and always after that, can be sued in this state even though both the foreign and New 

York limitations have run. National Surety Co. v. Ruffin, 242 N. Y. 413, 152 N. E. 246. (8) Where the party entitled to maintain an action other than for the recovery of real property is under a disability such as infancy or insanity, or imprisonment for a term less than life, when the right to maintain the same accrued, the time of such disability is not part of the time so limited, except that the time cannot be extended more than five years by any such disability, except infancy, nor in any case more than one year after such disability ceases.

This does not apply, however, to actions for a penalty or a forfeiture, or against the sheriff for an escape. (C. P. A. $60.) Where two or more disabilities coexist when the right of action accrues, the limitation does not attach until all are removed. (C. P. A. §29.) §52. Suspension. 

After the time has commenced to run, it does not cease to run except in the following cases, and then only during the specified period: (1) Where the commencement of the action has been stayed by order of the court or by statutory prohibition. 

(C. P. A. §24.) 

(2) Where the parties, within the time limited, have stipulated in writing to submit the matter to arbitration and the submission has afterwards been revoked. (C. P. A. §25.) (3) Where the cause of action has been interposed as a counterclaim against the same defendant in some other ac-

tion and that action has been discontinued without a final decision on the merits. (C. P. A. §26.) (4) When the defendant has departed from the state and resided continuously out of the state for one year or more, or is within the state under a false name, and unknown to the plaintiff. (C. P. A. §19.) (5) Where either party is an alien subject or citizen of a country at war with the United States. (C. P. A. §27.) 

Sec. 27 is modified by Sec. 28, so that a person whose right of action accrued before the war with Germany cannot deduct the period of the war from the time provision in the statute. The general rule is that, when the statute of limitations once begins to run, it continues to run, notwithstanding any subsequent disability. Nathan v. Equitable Trust Co., 250 N. Y. 250, 165 

N. E. 282. (6) The Soviet Government of Russia has not been recognized by the United States and is, therefore, precluded from suing here. A Russian citizen, however, may bring an action in our courts. But if any plaintiff brings an action on a contract to be performed in Russia, the opposing party may, on application, obtain a stay; unless the plaintiff is the state of New York or its representative and the action is to take possession of the assets, etc., of a Russian company. 

(C. P. A. §§169-a, 169-b.) 

The Russian Republic has not been recognized by the U. S. and so cannot sue in our courts. However, a

Russian citizen may, even though the action is stayed under C. P. A. Sec. 169-a. Sliosberg v. N. Y. Life Ins. 

Co., 244 N. Y. 482, 155 N. E. 749. (7) Where the defendant dies: (a) if he dies without the state, the time does not begin to run until eighteen months after letters testamentary or letters of administration upon his estate have been issued within the state; (b) if he dies within the state, the time does not run during a

period of eighteen months after his death. (C. P. A. §§12; 21.) 

$53. Extension of Time. 

The time within which an action may be maintained may be extended in the following cases: (1) Where the action is regularly commenced, but afterwards abates under conditions not prohibiting the bringing of a new action, such new action may be brought within one year, notwithstanding the expiration of the time limit. The conditions which do not prohibit the commencement of the new action are: (I) a reversal on appeal which does not award a new trial, (II) or a termination of the action in any manner other than: (a) a voluntary discontinuance, (b) a dismissal of the complaint for neglect to prosecute, (c) or a final judgment on the merits. (C. P. A. §23.) (2) Where the person entitled to maintain such an action dies before the expiration of the time limited for the commencement of the action, and the cause of action survives, an action may be commenced by his representatives after the expiration of that time and within one year after his death. (C. P. A. §20.) (3) Where the defendant dies within the state, and letters testamentary or of administration are not issued upon his estate until some time during the six months immediately preceding the expiration of the limited time, such time is extended one year. (C. P. A. §§21, 22.) (4) Where the party to be charged has made and signed a written acknowledgment of or promise to pay a debt, an action, other than for the recovery of real property, is taken out of the operation of the statute. (C. P. A. §59.) 

See Conn. Trust & Safe Deposit Co. v. Wead, 172 N. 

Y. 497, 65 N. E. 261, and Laurencelle v. Laurencelle, 217 

App. Div. 159, 216 N. Y. S. 384, for sufficiency of the writing.

(5) A payment on account sometimes extends the time in which to sue. "Part payment of a debt is not of itself conclusive to take the case out of the statute. In order to have that effect it must not only appear that the payment was made on account of debt, but also on account of the debt for which action is brought, and that the payment was made as a part of a larger indebtedness, and under such circumstances as warrant a jury in finding an implied promise to pay the balance." Crow v. Gleason, 141 

N. Y. 489, 493, 150 N. E. 525.

Where nothing, either principal or interest, was paid on a note dated 1913 and action was commenced in 1922, recovery of both interest and principal due before 

1916 was barred by the 6 year limitation, since action should have been brought on each installment as it matured. True v. Brainard, 130 Misc. 191, 223 N. Y. S. 

672. But see Harmon v. Peats Co., 243 N. Y. 473, 154 

N. E. 314, for recovery of wages for work performed more than six years before action on a quantum meruit basis.

In order to save a cause of action against a jointdebtor, payment of interest or part of the principal by another joint-debtor must be made as the authorized agent of the former. But in all cases, there must be previous authorization or subsequent ratification of such payments by the person sought to be charged. Hoover v. Hubbard, 202 Ñ. Y. 289, 95 N. E. 702; McMullen v. 

Rafferty, 89 N. Y. 456.

The time cannot be extended by amending a complaint served within the limitation if an outlawed cause of action is thereby introduced. 

Where an amendment to the complaint introduces a new cause of action, barred by the Statute of Limitations since the beginning of the action, such amendment should not be allowed, except with leave to the defendant to plead the limitation. Dietz v. Harris, 221 

App. Div. 581, 224 N. Y. S. 491.

A court or judge may not extend the time fixed by law within which an action must be commenced. (C. P. A. §99.)

The courts construe statutes creating exceptions or interruptions to the running of the period of limitation; but the courts may not themselves create such exceptions. Mack v. Mendels, 249 N. Y. 356, 164 N. E. 248. 

§54. When an Action Is Deemed Commenced. 

An action is deemed commenced when the summons is served on a defendant or on a co-defendant who is a joint contractor or otherwise united in interest with him. (C. P. 

A. §16.) An action is properly commenced if the summons is delivered to the sheriff before the limitation has run and it is served within 60 days after the expiration of the limitation. (C. P. A. §17.) Von Wilmowsky v. Prindle, 225 

App. Div. 594, 234 N. Y. S. 15. In the municipal court, the action is commenced by (1) service of the summons, (2) voluntary joinder of issues by the parties, or (3) issuance of the summons by the clerk, when actual service thereof is made with due diligence. (Mun. Ct. Code §18.) $55. Application.

The provisions for bringing actions, other than for the recovery of real property, apply equally to summons and complaint, defenses and counterclaims. (C. P. A. §61.)

ABATEMENT §56. Abatement and Dismissal for Non-Prosecution. 

Sometimes the right to continue an action already commenced is extinguished. If a plaintiff unreasonably neglects to serve the summons on a necessary party or to proceed in an action, a motion may be made to dismiss the action. (C. P. A. §§180, 181.) After an issue of fact has been joined, if the plaintiff fails to bring the same to trial within a reasonable time and later issues have been tried in their regular order, the defendant may move for a dismissal of 2 

2 See p. 204.

ABATEMENT 

29 the prior complaint. (Rule 156.) Other circumstances may cause the action to abate. In the municipal court, an action which has been on the general calendar or on the calendar for actions reserved generally for more than six months may be dismissed on application of the defendant on notice. 

If the action has been on such calendar for more than one year, it may be dismissed by the court without application. 

(Mun. Ct. Code §126.) 

Where no excuse was offered for the delay, and plaintiff failed to proceed for ten years after joinder of issue, the defendant was entitled to dismissal. Raber v. 

Schweitzer, 214 App. Div. 759, 209 N. Y. S. 906. $57. Abatement and Continuance.

An action or special proceeding does not abate if the cause of action or the right to relief survives or continues. 

This is true even though a party transfers his interest or liability, becomes bankrupt or dies. The transferee or the decedent's representative may continue in the action, if the court so directs. (C. P. A. §§83-87; 1131.) Melnick v. Com mercial Casualty Ins. Co., 221 App. Div. 599, 224 N. Y. S. 

516. The death of one of two or more plaintiffs or one of two or more defendants does not abate the action, if the cause or part thereof survives. (C. P. A. §85.) 

When performance of a contract depends on the continued existence of a certain person or thing and such continued existence was the basis of the agreement, the obligation does not survive the death of the person or the destruction of the thing. Lorillard v. Clyde, 142 N. 

Y. 456, 37 N. E. 489.

The dissolution of a defendant corporation pending suit abates the action because the corporation becomes non-existent. The receiver of such corporation is not bound by the judgment in such action unless he was made a party. Rodgers v. Insurance Co., 148 N. Y. 34, 42 N. E. 515.

$58. Actions Which Do Not Survive. 

The following actions for personal injuries do not survive the death of either party: libel, slander, criminal conversation, seduction, malicious prosecution, assault, battery, false imprisonment or other actionable injury to the person of the plaintiff or another. (Dec. Est. Law §120; Gen. Con struction Law §37-a.) "Other actionable injury" has been construed to include: an action for loss of a wife's services, where the defendant dies (Gorlitzer v. Wolffberg, 208 N. Y. 

475, 102 N. E. 528); an action for breach of promise to marry, where defendant dies. (Wade v. Kalbfleisch, 58 N. Y. 

282. See also Hegerich v. Keddie, 99 N. Y. 258, 1 N. E. 787.)

But where an action is commenced by a father to recover damages for the seduction of his minor daughter and he dies, the mother may continue the action. After verdict, report or decision in an action to recover damages for personal injuries, the death of a party does not abate the action. If such judgment is reversed on questions of law only, the death of the party against whom it was rendered does not abate the action. (C. P. A. §89.) The death of a party before final judgment in an action to annul a marriage, for divorce or for separation, abates such action. (Grotsch v. Hassey, 133 Misc. 373, 231 N. Y. S. 469; White v. White, 224 App. Div. 355, 231 N. Y. S. 146.) 

§59. Disability of the Judge. 

The death, sickness, resignation, removal, absence or disability of the officer before whom the action or special proceeding has been brought does not abate the same, but it may be continued before a substituted officer. (C. P. A. 

§§93, 94; Mun. Ct. Code §9.) §60. Removal to Another Court. 

Removal of the action or special proceeding to another court does not abate the same or invalidate any process, proceeding or undertaking therein. (C. P. A. §95.)

§61. Order of Abatement. 

Upon application of the defendant or another person whose interest is affected, the court may direct that an action, commenced by a plaintiff who has died, abate unless it is continued by the proper parties within the time specified in an order, not less than six months nor more than one year after the granting of such order. (C. P. A. §88.) 

§62. Entry of Judgment. 

If either party dies after a verdict, report or decision has been rendered, but before final judgment is entered, the court must enter final judgment in the names of the original parties. (C. P. A. §478.)

§63. The Judiciary. 

CHAPTER III COURTS 1 

A list of the courts in which the judicial power of the state is vested is given below. Only those courts which have jurisdiction in civil cases will be considered in detail; and, if a particular court has both civil and criminal jurisdiction, only the civil side of that court will be discussed. 

The authority of the Board of Aldermen under the Charter of the City of New York to regulate the numbering of houses and lots in the streets and naming streets is legislative in character and the reasonableness thereof cannot be reviewed by the courts. Only the legality thereof is subject to judicial review. Bacon v. Miller, 247 N. Y. 311, 160 N. E. 381. 

§64. Courts of Record. 

The courts of record in New York State are the following: (1) Court for the trial of impeachments. (2) The court of appeals. (3) The appellate division of the supreme court. (4) The appellate term of the supreme court in the first and second departments. (5) The supreme court. (6) The court of claims. (7) A county court in each county, except New York County. (8) A surrogate's court in each county. (9) The court of general sessions of the peace in and for the city and county of New York. 

1 For jurisdiction over nonresidents, see Chapter IV, Parties. 32

COURTS 

33 (10) The city court of the city of New York. (11) The municipal court of the city of New York. (12) City court of Yonkers. (13) Municipal court of the city of Syracuse. (14) Such other local courts as are now constituted courts of record. 

865. Courts Not of Record. 

The courts not of record are the following: (1) Courts of the justices of the peace in each town and certain cities and villages. (2) Courts of special sessions of the peace in each town, and in certain cities and villages. (3) The police courts in certain cities and villages. (4) The city court of Rochester. (5) The city court of Buffalo. (6) Children's courts. (7) Domestic relations courts. (8) Such other local courts as are now constituted courts not of record. (Const. Art. VI; Jud. L. §§2, 3; Penal L. §487; C. P. A. §62.) 

866. Justice of the Peace.

The court of the justice of the peace is the lowest court in the state. One justice holds court in each town and in some cities. Over the actions within his jurisdiction, he has all the necessary powers possessed by the supreme court. 

(Jus. Ct. Act §18.) §67. Jurisdiction. 

The jurisdiction of a justice of the peace extends to the following: 

A. For any amount: (1) Dispossess proceedings, except in a city of more than one hundred thousand inhabitants adjoining a city of

one million or more inhabitants; and the amount of arrears may be included in the final judgment. (2) Special proceedings.

B. Where the sum claimed does not exceed $500, it extends to: (3) Judgment upon confession of the defendant. 

C. Where the sum claimed does not exceed $200, to an action: (4) On a contract, other than a contract to marry. (5) For damages for a personal injury caused by negligence. (6) For damages to property. (7) On a judgment of an inferior court not of record. (8) To recover a chattel, with or without damages. (9) On a bond. (10) For a fine or penalty.

D. Where the amount claimed does not exceed $50, to an action: (11) On a judgment of a court of record (12) For damages for escape from jail liberties. (13) On a claim rejected by an executor or administra tor. (Const. Art. VI, §§17, 18; C. P. A. §§1413; 1425; Jus. 

Ct. Act §§2, 3, 4, 5, 5-a, 7, 8; 18.) 

§68. Appeals. 

Appeals from the justice's court may be taken to the county court. (Jus. Ct. Act §427.) §69. The Municipal Court of the City of New York. 

The municipal court of the city of New York is a court of original civil jurisdiction. There are two districts of this 

2 "The municipal court" wherever mentioned in this volume refers to this court.

COURTS 

35 court in the borough of The Bronx, ten in Manhattan, eight in Brooklyn, five in Queens and two in Richmond. (Mun. 

Ct. Code §5.) The boundaries of each district are given in 

§5 of the Municipal Court Code. 

$70. Jurisdiction.

The municipal court has jurisdiction of the following: 

A. For any amount: (1) Dispossess proceedings, including a judgment for rent in arrears. (2) Action on a bastardy or abandonment bond. (3) On a marshal's bond. (4) Replevin. (5) Attachment. (6) Arrest. (7) Judgment on confession. 

(8)` Arbitration proceeding. 

B. When the amount claimed does not exceed $1000, exclusive of interest and costs, to an action: (9) On a contract, other than a contract to marry. (10) To establish a mechanic's lien on real property and a personal judgment for the amount due. (11) To foreclose a lien on a chattel. (12) For damages for fraud or deceit. (13) For a personal injury caused by negligence. (14) For an injury to property caused by negligence. (15) Accounting between partners. (16) To recover a fine, or penalty. (17) For damages for escape from jail liberties. (18) To recover chattels the value of which does not exceed $1000, with or without damages.

(19) On a counterclaim not exceeding $1000, exclusive of interest and costs. (C. P. A. §1425; Mun. Ct. Code §§6; 

8; 70; 86.)

In summary proceedings to dispossess, a judgment for rent in arrears may be entered whatever the amount and in any court whatsoever. Matter of Byrne v. Padden, 248 N. Y. 243, 162 N. E. 20. "The Municipal Court of the City of New York has jurisdiction of a complaint by five plaintiffs which separately states the injuries to each and specifically alleges the damages which each one suffered, and demands a separate and distinct judgment, notwithstanding the fact that the aggregate of all the amount claimed by the various plaintiffs is in excess of $1000, exclusive of interest and costs." Dilworth v. Yellow Taxi Corporation, 127 Misc. 543, 216 N. Y. S. 513.

The jurisdiction of the municipal court does not include the power to decree foreclosure and sale in enforcement of mechanics' liens. Deall v. Gordon, 51 Misc. 

618, 101 N. Y. S. 171. 

§71. Venue. 

In general, actions in the municipal court must be brought in a borough where one of the parties resides or has a place of business. If all parties reside outside the city of New York, the action may be brought in any borough.

Dispossess proceedings and actions for rent must be brought in the district where the premises or property is located. 

An action to recover a fine or penalty must be brought in the borough where the violation occurred. If an action is brought in the wrong borough the court may on its own motion and must on the motion of the defendant transfer the action to the proper borough. (Mun. Ct. Code §17.) 

Section 17, subd. 1, of the Municipal Court Code reads: An action must be brought in the borough in which either the plaintiff or defendant resides or has a place for the regular transaction of business, "unless all the plaintiffs or all of the defendants reside out of the city of New York, in which case the action may be

COURTS 

37 brought in any borough. 99 

The "or" italicized should be read "and." Grady v. McHugh, 131 Misc. 303, 226 N. Y. S. 608.

A demand for a change of venue in the municipal court must be made before or coincidental with the service of the answer. Chelsea Electric Corporation v.

Ornstein, 131 Misc. 849, 228 N. Y. S. 344. Section 187 

of the Civil Practice Act and Rule 146 apply to the removal of a municipal court case from the wrong district to a proper one. Grady v. Selden Truck Corp., 133 Misc. 

97, 231 N. Y. S. 255. See also Great Woodworking Co., Inc., v. Walley, 135 Misc. 213, 237 N. Y. S. 374. 

§72. Service of Process. 

The summons or any other process of the municipal court must be served or executed within the city of New 

York. (Mun. Ct. Code §14.) §73. Appeals. 

Appeals from orders or judgments in municipal court cases may be taken to the appellate term or, when the constitutionality of a statute is the only question involved, di rect to the court of appeals.³ (Const. Art. VI, §§3; 7; C. P. 

A. §627; Mun. Ct. Code §154.) 

$74. The County Courts.

A county court. is one step higher than a justices' court and may hear appeals therefrom. (Jus. Ct. Act §427.) One of these courts is situated in each county of the state except 

New York County. The county courts in the other counties of New York City, i. e., Kings, Bronx, Queens and Richmond, now have jurisdiction in criminal matters only; the jurisdiction they formerly had in civil matters is now vested in a city court in each county. (Const. Art. VI, §§11; 15; 

City Court Act §16.) 3 See Chapter XI.

$75. Jurisdiction and Venue. 

The jurisdiction of the county court extends to: (1) An action for the recovery of money only wherein the demand does not exceed $3000 and each defendant is a resident of the county or has a place of business therein. (2) An action to foreclose a lien on a chattel, under §206 of the Lien Law, where the lien does not exceed $1000 and the chattel is found within the county. (3) An action for money remaining due on a judgment of the same court. (4) Change of a person's name, where the petitioner resides in the county. (5) Proceedings for the custody of the person and the care of the property of an incompetent resident of the county. (6) Proceedings for the sale or other disposition of the real property situated within the county of an incompetent, wherever resident, of an infant, wherever resident, or of a domestic religious corporation. (7) Actions affecting real property situated within the county. This last group comprises: actions for partition; for dower; for the foreclosure, redemption or satisfaction of a mortgage on real property; for the foreclosure of a lien arising out of a contract for the sale of real property; for the enforcement or foreclosure of a mechanic's lien on real property; for specific performance of a contract relating to real property; and dispossess proceedings. (Const. 

Art. VI, §11; C. P. A. §§67; 1413; Civil Rights Law §60.) 

A domestic corporation or joint-stock association is deemed a resident of a county wherein it has a plant, shop, factory or office; or, in the case of a railroad corporation, where any portion of the road operated by it is within the county. (C. P. A. §68.)

If a counterclaim for more than $3000 is interposed in any action in the county court, the supreme court of the

COURTS 

39 district, on application of either party, may remove the cause to the supreme court. (Const. Art. VI, §11; C. P. 

A. §67.) 

When it has jurisdiction, the powers of the county court are co-extensive with those of the supreme court, and a county judge has the same power out of court as has a justice of the supreme court. (C. P. A. §§69; 74, 75; 77.)

The county court cannot decree specific performance of an agreement giving an option for the renewal of a lease. Armstrong v. Shapiro, 207 App. Div. 304, 202 N. Y. S. 305.

The provision which extends the jurisdiction to the "foreclosure, redemption and satisfaction of a mortgage upon real property," does not include, as incidental thereto, the power to reform a mortgage. The word "mortgage" means a written mortgage only. The provision giving jurisdiction of an action "to procure a judgment requiring the specific performance of a contract relating to real property," has reference to contracts which are complete, and does not extend to the reformation of an imperfect instrument, even if it is a lien upon land. A county court, therefore, has not jurisdiction of an action to reform a mortgage, although part of the relief asked is the foreclosure of the mortgage after it has been reformed; the remedies are independent, and may be attained by separate actions, open to separate and independent defenses. Thomas v. 

Harmon, 122 N. Y. 84, 25 N. E. 257.

The county court has jurisdiction of an action to compel satisfaction of a mortgage to be made in pursuance of written agreement. Mosher v. Campbell, 30 Hun 230. 

The county court has no jurisdiction of an action in which the complaint demands judgment for the maximum amount plus interest. Mansson v. Nostrand, 183 

App. Div. 371, 170 N. Y. S. 285. 

Where the amount demanded in a complaint, principal and interest, was within the jurisdictional limits of the county court, the court was not ousted of jurisdiction because subsequently accruing interest brought the sum recoverable above such limits, and it could proceed to render any judgment which the supreme court

might render in a like case. Tappin v. MacLean, 117 

Misc. 757, 192 N. Y. S. 196. 

§76. Service of Process. 

Any summons, process or mandate of the county court may be served anywhere within the state. (C. P. A. §72.) $77. Appeals.

Appeals from county court decisions may be taken to the appellate division of the supreme court. (C. P. A. §622.) §78. The City Court of the City of New York.¹ 

The city court of the city of New York is a court of limited original jurisdiction. There is one court in each county of the city. The forms and procedure are the same as in the supreme court. (City Ct. Act §65.) §79. Jurisdiction. 

The jurisdiction of the city court in New York City extends to: (1) An action for the recovery of money only in which the amount claimed does not exceed $3,000 and interest. (2) An action to recover chattels, with or without damages, in which the value of the chattels does not exceed $3,000. (3) Replevin, where the value of the property involved or the amount demanded does not exceed $3,000. (4) Judgment on confession where the amount confessed does not exceed $3,000 and interest. (5) An action for damages for breach of promise to marry for any amount. (6) An action on a bond of this court for any amount. (7) Change of the name of a person. 

4 "City court" wherever mentioned in this volume refers to the court in New York City.

COURTS 

41 

(8) Naturalization. 

(9) Judgment on a counterclaim for any amount. (10) Marine cases for any amount. The marine cases which may be brought in the city court are: (a) An action in favor of a person belonging to a vessel in the merchant service for the reasonable value of services or for breach of contract to pay for services rendered or to be rendered on board. (b) An action in favor of or against such person to recover damages for assault, battery or false imprisonment committed on board the vessel, upon the high seas, or in a place without the United States. (Const. Art. VI, §15; 

City Ct. Act §§16-19; 37-47; Civil Rights L. §60.) 

When the complaint demands judgment for more than the maximum, the City Court has no jurisdiction; and a motion to amend so as to reduce the demand to $3,000 will be denied. Central Pk. West-84th St. Corp. v. Cuscok, 130 Misc. 770, 224 N. Y. S. 414. But where the summons was served without the complaint and no sum was mentioned therein, an amendment of the complaint demanding more than $3,000 will be allowed. 

Dobrikin v. Union Ry. Co., 130 Misc. 796, 225 N. Y. S. 376. 

The joinder of two plaintiffs where a joint demand is made for $4,000 does not extend the jurisdiction nor obviate the necessity of bringing separate actions and consolidating them under C. P. A. §96. Weis v. Richartz, 130 Misc. 583, 224 N. Y. S. 416. But a wife's cause of action for injuries and a husband's action for loss of services and medical expenses may be joined in one city court action if each demands a separate judgment for $3,000. "Complaint" in §209 means a particular cause of action and demand for judgment of a particular plaintiff. Dobrikin v. Union Ry. Co., 130 Misc. 796, 225 

N. Y. S. 376; Spetler v. Jogel Realty Co., 224 App. Div. 

612, 231 N. Y. S. 517.

An action for replevin of a chattel valued at $2,052, and for damages for withholding same for $1,025, may be brought in the city court. The test is whether the action for replevin is more than $3,000 or not. Scarf-

man v. May-Claire Costume Co., Inc., 133 Misc. 482, 232 N. Y. S. 516.

In summary proceedings to dispossess a tenant and regain possession of real property, under C. P. A. §1425 any court may give judgment for rent in arrears for any amount due, and the city court of Long Beach is not limited as to the amount which may be awarded. 

Such construction is constitutional. Matter of Byrne v. 

Padden, 248 N. Y. 243, 162 N. E. 20. 

In an action to foreclose a mechanic's lien: (1) if the lien is on real property, the value of the property must not exceed $3,000; if the lien is on personalty, the lien must not exceed $3,000. Kaplan v. Antonelli, 132 

Misc. 572, 230 N. Y. S. 321.

The city court or a judge thereof has no power to stay an action until the subject matter has been decided by arbitration in compliance with a contract or submission under §5 of the Arbitration Law. Only the supreme court or a judge thereof has such power. Kipp v. Hamburg-American Line, 134 Misc. 481, 235 N. Y. 

S. 450.

That portion of the Constitution, Art. VI, §15, which states that the city court's jurisdiction to enter a counterclaim is unlimited must be read with the provisions which limit the causes of action which may be brought in this court. The "unlimited jurisdiction" as to counterclaims refers to amount only; and an equitable counterclaim cannot be interposed in a city court action. 

Schreiber v. Noe, 243 N. Y. S. 360. 

§80. Venue. 

An action in the city court must be brought in the division of the court located in the county where either party resides or has a place of business, unless all the plaintiffs or all the defendants reside outside the city of New York, or have no place of business therein, in which case the action may be brought in any division. If an action be brought in the wrong county, it may be tried there unless the defendant applies for a transfer. (City Ct. Act §48.) 

5 Quaere whether this "or" should not be construed as "and" as in a similar section in the Municipal Court, see p. 36.

COURTS 

43 Where the city court has jurisdiction of the subject matter, and has acquired jurisdiction of the parties thereto, the mere nonresidence of the parties in the territorial jurisdiction of the court is not a sufficient ground to warrant the removal of the case to the supreme court. 

Harris v. Hellyer, 210 App. Div. 399, 206 N. Y. S. 287. 

§81. Service of Process. 

The summons and mandates of the city court must be served within the city of New York. Section 27 of the City 

Court Act, which states that the process and mandates of this court may be executed in any part of the state is unconstitutional and void. "Original jurisdiction concurrent with the supreme court" means jurisdiction with respect to the cases specified and within its jurisdictional limits. (American Historical Society, Inc., v. Glenn, 248 N. Y. 445, 162 N. E. 481.) "It is a great and fundamental privilege of defendants to have small cases brought in local courts heard in their own territory." American Historical Society, Inc., v. Glenn, 248 N. Y. 445, 453, 162 N. E. 481. 

§82. Appeals. 

Appeals from orders or judgments in city court cases may be taken to the appellate term; or, when the constitutionality of a statute is the only question involved, direct to the court of appeals.' (Const. Art. VI, §§3; 7; C. P. A. §627; 

City Ct. Act, §§56; 61; 63.) 

§83. The Supreme Court. 

The supreme court is a higher court of general jurisdiction. For election purposes, the state is divided into nine judicial districts, the boundaries of which are given in §140 of the Judiciary Law. • But see p. 88. 

'See Chapter XI.

884. Jurisdiction. 

The jurisdiction of the supreme court extends to practically all cases at law and in equity. It has no jurisdiction of dispossess proceedings; and only in the city of New York does it have jurisdiction to change the name of a person. 

(Const. Art. VI, §1; C. P. A. §§64; 1413; Civil Rights L. 

§60.) Although virtually any action may be brought in the supreme court, the plaintiff is penalized by being deprived of costs if he might have brought the action in a lower court." (C. P. A. §1474.) 

The State Industrial Board is only a quasi judicial body with vast powers as to the disposition of claims under the Workmen's Compensation Law but without power to reform a policy of compensation insurance.

The supreme court has sole jurisdiction of such suits in equity. Allied Mutual L. Ins. Co. v. Interstate Cork 

Co., 134 Misc. 504, 235 N. Y. S. 541. 

§85. Venue. 

Real property actions must be brought in the county where the property is situated. (C. P. A. §183.) The following actions must be brought in the county where the cause of action arose: (1) To recover a fine or penalty imposed by statute. (2) Against a public officer for an act or omission in the line of his duty. (3) To recover a chattel distrained, or damages for distraining a chattel. Other actions must be brought in a county where one of the parties resides. An executor or administrator is deemed a resident of the county of his appointment as well as the county in which he resides. (C. P. A. §182.) 

§86. Change of Venue. 

Even though the county designated in the complaint is not the proper county the trial may be had therein, unless 

8 See Kipp v. Hamburg-American Line, p. 312. 

⁹ See p. 316.

COURTS 

45 the place of trial is changed to the proper county upon demand of the defendant. The plaintiff may consent to the change or an order may be obtained from the court. (C. P. 

A. §§186, 187; Rule 146.) In the discretion of the court or on stipulation by the parties an action triable by the court without a jury may be tried in any county within the judicial district. (C. P. A. §185.) The place of trial may be changed on order of the court where: (1) there is reason to believe that an impartial trial cannot be had in the proper county, or the convenience of material witnesses or the ends of justice will be promoted. (C. P. A. §187.) The supreme court may and in a proper case must transfer a case pending in any other court of record, except the city court of the city of New York or except a real property action in a county court, to the supreme court in another county. (C. P. 

A. §189.) If a county court judge is incapable of acting in a county court action or proceeding, the supreme court may; and if the special county judge is also incapable the action or proceeding must be removed to the supreme court. (C. P. 

A. §190.) §87. Service of Process. 

The summons or mandate in a supreme court action may be served or executed anywhere within the state. (C. P. A. 

Article 25.) 

§88. Appeals. 

Appeals from orders or judgments of the supreme court or a judge thereof may be taken to the appellate division. (C. P. A. §§608, 609, 610, 611; 631.) 

§89. Surrogate's Courts. 

A surrogate's court is situated in each county, and its jurisdiction extends to all matters affecting the estate of a decedent, from the probate of a will or the granting of ad-

ministration, to the final accounting of the executors or administrators and the distribution of the estate. All appeals from its decisions go to the appellate division of the supreme court. (Const. Art. VI, §§12, 13; Surr. Ct. Act §§40-47; 228.) 

$90. The Appellate Term.

The appellate term of the supreme court exists in the first and second districts. It is composed of three justices of the supreme court in each department designated by the appellate division of the department. Appeals from the city court or municipal court of the city of New York may be heard by the appellate term. This court or a justice of the appellate division may allow a further appeal to the appel late division. (Const. Art. VI, §3; C. P. A. §627.) 891. The Appellate Division. 

The appellate division of the supreme court is composed of seven justices in the first and second departments and five in the other two departments. Only five justices sit at one time; four constitute a quorum; and three are necessary for a decision. This court has little original jurisdiction, except with its consent; but hears appeals from the special, trial and appellate terms of the supreme court, from the surrogate's, county, and other courts of record. (Const. Art. 

VI, §§2, 3; C. P. A. §§618-622; 627. Matter of the Bar Ass'n. 

of the City of New York, 222 App. Div. 580, 227 N. Y. S. 1.)

Appeals from the appellate division are taken to the court of appeals. (C. P. A. §§588, 589, 590.) 

The appellate division has power to direct a general inquiry into the conduct of its officers, the members of the bar, and to compel such officer to testify in such inquiry as to his acts in his professional relations. The refusal to be sworn and to testify constitutes contempt and the officer so refusing may be committed to jail. A writ of habeas corpus for his release will not be granted. 

Peo. ex rel Karlin v. Culkin, 248 N. Y. 465, 162 N. E. 487.

COURTS 

47 

$92. The Court of Appeals. 

The court of appeals is the highest court of appellate jurisdiction in the state. It is composed of seven judges, five of whom must sit together to make a quorum to do business, and four must concur to render a decision. It is limited to review questions of law; except where the judgment appealed from is that of death or where the appellate division has made new findings of fact and entered final order or judgment thereon. (Const. Art. VI, §§5, 6, 7; C. P. 

A. §§588-607.) 

$93. The Court of Claims.

The court of claims is composed of three judges appointed by the governor, by and with the consent of the senate. This court has the powers and jurisdiction of the former board of claims, and hears and determines claims against the state. The state has recently waived its immunity from liability for the torts of its officers and employees. 

Claims for damages for injury to person or property caused by the misfeasance or negligence of state officers or employees are now determined by the court of claims in accordance with the rules of the supreme court. (Ct. of Claims Act.) $94. Appeal.

Either party may appeal from an order or judgment of the court of claims to the appellate division of the third department, except that where the claim arose in the fourth department the appeal must be taken to the appellate divi sion of the fourth department. (Const. Art. VI, §23; Ct. of 

Claims Act §§2; 12, 12-a; 29.) 

$95. The Court for the Trial of Impeachments. 

The court for the trial of impeachments is composed of the president of the senate, the senators, or major part of

them, and the judges of the court of appeals, or a major part of them. The assembly also has the power of impeachment. The powers of this court are limited to trial of the accused officer and removal from office and disqualification to hold any public office in the state. (Const. Art. VI, §10.)

CHAPTER IV PARTIES 

896. Plaintiff.

The party prosecuting a civil action is called the plaintiff. (C. P. A. §191.) Residents, whether natural persons or domestic corporations, may be plaintiffs. Under certain circumstances to be discussed later, nonresidents and foreign corporations may be plaintiffs. 

§97. Infancy. 

The infancy of a person does not disable him from suing. 

The action is brought in the infant's name by a guardian ad litem, who must be appointed by the court or a judge thereof and when the action is in the supreme court the appointment may be made by a county judge; except in an action for partition, when he must be appointed by the court. (C. P. A. §§201, 202.) 

"The original action was brought in the name of 'Michael Kaplan as guardian ad litem of Simon Kaplan' as plaintiff, instead of in the name of the infant by his guardian, but upon the trial of the action the court permitted the amendment of the summons and complaint so that the title should appear in proper form. Held, that the amendment was proper, as there could be no surprise upon the part of the defendant, and as the objection was not raised, either by answer or motion, it must be deemed to have been waived." Kaplan v. The 

N. Y. Biscuit Co., 5 App. Div. 60, 38 N. Y. S. 1049. See
