Spooner v. D., L. & W. R. R. Co., 115 N. Y. 22, 21 N. E. 696. 

§98. Application for Guardian Ad Litem. 

The guardian must be appointed on the application of the infant, if he is fourteen years of age or older; and, if he is under that age, upon the application of his general or 49

testamentary guardian, or a relative or next friend. If the application is made by a relative or next friend, notice thereof must be given to the infant's general or testamentary guardian, or if he has none, to the person with whom he lives. (C. P. A. §§203, 204.) §99. Time of Appointment. 

The guardian ad litem of an infant plaintiff should be appointed before the summons is served. In the supreme court, the guardian may be appointed nunc pro tunc. According to the Municipal Court Code, the guardian ad litem for an infant plaintiff must be appointed before the summons is served. (Mun. Ct. Code §26.) However, the ruling by the Court of Appeals in the following case probably applies to all courts. "We think that it should now be regarded as settled that the failure to appoint a guardian ad litem for an infant plaintiff affects the regularity of procedure but not the jurisdiction of the court." Rima v. R. I. 

Works, 120 N. Y. 433, 440, 24 N. E. 940. 

§100. Qualifications. 

The guardian ad litem: (1) Must be the general guardian of the infant or a person fully competent to understand and protect the rights of the infant. (2) Must have no interest adverse to that of the infant nor be connected in business with the attorney of any adverse party, nor shall he be nominated by any person having an adverse interest. (3) Must have sufficient ability to pay any damage sustained by his negligence or misconduct, and such ability must be shown by affidavit. (4) Any trust company authorized by New York laws to act as general guardian without giving security may be appointed.

COURTS 

51 (5) The written and duly acknowledged consent of the proposed guardian must be filed.¹ (6) It is the duty of every attorney or officer of the court to act as guardian of an infant defendant whenever appointed by order of the court. (Rules 40, 41, 42.)

Although an infant has a general guardian, an action to recover money or personal property of the infant may be brought by a different person as guardian ad litem. 

Segelken v. Meyer, 94 N. Y. 473. 

8101. A Person Judicially Declared Incompetent. 

The committee of a person judicially declared incompetent may maintain an action in his own name, adding his official title, in any case where the incompetent could have maintained an action if the committee had not been appointed. (C. P. A. §1377; Rule 44.) $102. Other Incompetents. 

An incompetent not judicially declared insane may be a plaintiff, but the court may appoint a guardian ad litem or a special guardian, at any stage in any action or proceeding, when it appears to the court necessary for the protection of the rights and interests of such incompetent. (C. P. 

A. §207.) The qualifications for a guardian ad litem for an incompetent are the same as for an infant. (Rules 40; 44.) $103. Poor Persons.

A poor person may sue as plaintiff who is worth less. than one hundred dollars exclusive of such right of action, without being liable for the costs or fees to his attorney, upon obtaining order from the court to that effect. (C. P. 

A. §§196-199; Rules 35, 36, 37; Mun. Ct. Code §28.) 

1 As to Costs, see p. 318.

122 

Where the petition of an infant over fourteen years of age, asking leave to sue as a poor person, complies with the requirements and is verified by a guardian ad litem, the application should not be denied simply because it appears that the guardian ad litem is a respon sible person. Feier v. Third Ave. R. R. Co., 9 App. Div. 

607, 41 N. Y. S. 821.

An order allowing the plaintiff in an action to sue in forma pauperis is fatally defective, unless it contains a provision that the attorney for the plaintiff shall act without compensation. The filing by the plaintiff's attorney of a consent to prosecute the action without compensation is not sufficient. Rutkowsky v. Cohen, 74 App. 

Div. 415, 77 N. Y. S. 546.

An application for an order to sue as a poor person, which showed counsel's advice that petitioner had a good cause of action, but did not set forth facts sufficient to satisfy the court thereon independent of this advice, should be denied. Weinstein v. Frank and 

Schnepp, 56 App. Div. 275, 67 N. Y. S. 746.

An order for leave to prosecute as a poor person is defective where it does not assign to that person an attorney to prosecute the action without compensation. 

Pankawicus v. Nichols Copper Co., 169 App. Div. 419, 155 N. Y. S. 123. $104. Husband or Wife.

A married woman may be a party in the same manner as if she were single. (C. P. A. §200.) A husband or wife may maintain an action against the other on a contract, but neither can maintain an action against the other for personal injuries. (Dom. Rel. L. §§51; 56, 57; 60.)

A married woman has the power to make contracts with all persons, including her husband, as if she were single, and such contracts may be enforced by or against her. Werner v. Werner, 169 App. Div. 9, 154 N. Y. S. 

570; Winter v. Winter, 191 N. Y. 462, 84 N. E. 382.

A wife may not maintain an action against her husband, nor a husband against his wife for personal injuries, whether negligent or wilful. Schultz v. Schultz,

COURTS 

53 

89 N. Y. 644; Allen v. Allen, 246 N. Y. 571, 159 N. E. 656. 

But a wife may recover against a defendant for injuries sustained through the negligence of her husband, who was employed by the defendant. The disability does not extend to the employer. Schubert v. Schubert, 249 N. Y. 253, 164 N. E. 42.

A husband or wife may sue the other for injury to property. Berdell v. Berdell, 19 Hun 358. 

A wife as well as a husband may maintain an action for alienation of affections or criminal conversation. 

Oppenheim v. Kridel, 236 N. Y. 156, 140 N. E. 227. $105. Receiver.

Unless restricted by the special order of the court, every receiver of property of a debtor may sue for and collect all the debts, demands and rents belonging to such debtor. He may also sue in the name of the debtor, where it is necessary and proper for him to do so. (Rule 175.) 

§106. Foreign Corporations. 

A foreign corporation, other than a moneyed corporation, doing business in this state may not maintain an action in this state upon a contract made in the state, unless it obtained a certificate of authority before making the contract. (Gen. Corp. L. §218.) In order to obtain the certificate of authority to do business in this state a foreign corporation must file, among other things, a designation of the secretary of the state as its agent upon whom process may be served. (Gen. Corp. Law §210.) Thirteen months after beginning business in the state, a foreign corporation, other than a banking, building and loan or insurance corporation, must pay a license tax and it cannot maintain an action here unless such tax is paid. (Tax Law §181.)

The certificate of authority to do business in this state is a condition precedent and must be pleaded and proved by the plaintiff foreign corporation. Compliance

B44 

with the Tax Law is a condition subsequent and need not be pleaded in the complaint. Non-compliance is a defense to be pleaded in the answer. Wood & Selick v. 

Ball, 190 N. Y. 217, 83 N. E. 21. 

§107. Surrender of Authority. 

Such foreign corporation may surrender its right to do business within the state by filing a certificate of surrender with the secretary of state. Thereafter only actions upon liabilities or obligations incurred within the state before such filing may be brought by or against such corporation. 

(Gen. Corp. L. §216.) 

Broadly speaking, submission to jurisdiction depends upon presence of the foreign corporation within the state, and where the corporation has withdrawn and no longer acts as a corporation within the state, it cannot be sued here. Gaboury v. Central Vermont 

Ry. Co., 250 N. Y. 233, 165 N. E. 275. §108. Restrictions as to Nonresidents and Foreign Corporations.

A nonresident or a foreign corporation may not maintain an action against a foreign corporation unless (a) the action is brought to recover damages on a contract made in New York relating to property in New York when the contract was made; (b) an action to recover real property in 

New York or a chattel replevied in New York; (c) where the cause of action arose in New York, except where the action affects title to real property outside of New York; or (d) where one of the foreign corporations is doing business in the state. (Gen. Corp. L. §225.) Whether a foreign corporation is "doing business" within the state can be determined only by reference to the decisions, since there are no precise rules. For the sake of conciseness, a foreign corporation as defendant in all possible cases is discussed at this point.

PARTIES 

55 Even though, in a particular case, a foreign corporation may not sue another foreign corporation in New York where the cause of action arose outside the state, if the same claim is assigned to a bona fide resident of the state, the action may be maintained here. McCauley v. Georgia Railroad Bank, 122 Misc. 632, 203 N. Y. S. 

550, aff'd. 209 App. Div. 886, 205 N. Y. S. 935, aff'd. 239 

N. Y. 504, 147 N. E. 171. The same rule applies when the assignment is made by a nonresident. Application of Hamburg-American Line, 135 Misc. 715, 238 N. Y. S. 331. 

An action against a foreign corporation may be maintained by another foreign corporation where the cause of action arose within the state, and it is local in its nature. Gen'l. Corp. Law, §225. However, a judgment recovered by one foreign corporation against another in the state of their domicile which has failed of enforcement in the jurisdiction where rendered, cannot be regarded, when sued upon within this state, as a cause of action arising therein, upon which an action may be maintained. Anglo-American Provision Co. v. 

Davis Provision Co., 169 N. Y. 506, 62 N. E. 587, aff'd. 

191 U. S. 373. "The jurisdiction taken of foreign corporations, in the absence of statutory requirement or express consent, does not rest upon a fiction of constructive presence, It flows from the fact that the corporation itself does business in the state or district in such a manner and to such an extent that its actual presence there is established." Bank of America v. Whitney 

Bank, 261 U. S. 171, 173. 

What constitutes "doing business in this state" as will subject a foreign corporation to service of process depends upon the facts in each case. All that is requisite is that enough be done to enable the court to say that the corporation is here, and, if it is here, it may be served. And, if in fact the corporation is here, not occasionally or casually, but with a fair measure of permanence and continuity, then, whether its business is interstate or local, it is within the jurisdiction of the court. Tauza v. Susquehanna Coal Co., 220 N. Y. 259, 115 N. E. 915. 

Where a foreign corporation has filed a certificate of doing business in the state, it is held to be present

here and is answerable to the due service of process. 

Hartshein v. Seidenbach's, Inc., 129 Misc. 687, 222 N. 

Y. S. 404.

If a foreign corporation doing business in this state has designated an agent who may accept service, a resident plaintiff may sue such corporation and serve such agent, even though the cause of action arose outside the state. Bagdon v. Phil. & Reading C. & I. Co., 217 

N. Y. 432, 111 N. E. 1075. 

Where the defendant has not taken out a certificate to do business and has no property within the state, but its selling agent maintains an office in New York with defendant's name on the door and has listed defendant's name in the telephone directory, defendant is not doing business in the state. Hamlin v. Barrett & Co., Inc., 246 

N. Y. 554, 159 N. E. 648.

Buying is doing business as much as is selling. Nat'l. 

Furniture Co. v. Speigelman & Co., 198 App. Div. 672, 190 N. Y. S. 831.

Where a foreign corporation through its agents solicits orders and enters into contracts for the delivery of its goods within the state, and such agents manage its business within the state, service of summons upon such agents as representing the corporation is valid, notwithstanding such orders and contracts were made subject to the approval of the corporation. Bersin v. 

John Boath, Jr., & Co., 198 App. Div. 344, 190 N. Y. S. 398.

A foreign corporation, which recognizes orders for goods given by its treasurer in this state, is doing business here. Nat'l Furniture Co. v. Spiegelman & Co., Inc., 198 App. Div. 672, 190 N. Y. S. 831.

A Michigan corporation, having no permit to do business in New York, manufacturing automobiles in

Michigan and selling them there at wholesale to dealers in New York, who were not its agents, and having representatives in New York for the purpose of looking after its export business, who had no power to negotiate sales or collect money, was not doing business here. 

Holzer v. Dodge Brothers, 233 N. Y. 216, 135 N. E. 268.

Where the board of directors and executive committee of the defendant foreign corporation met in New

PARTIES 

57 York and exercised supervision and management of the business; and the secretary resided and had an office for the transaction of the defendant's business; and the treasurer here paid obligations and dividends of the corporation for the purpose of carrying on the business for which it was organized; the corporation was doing business within the state and service of the summons on the said secretary was valid. Pomeroy v. Hocking 

Valley Ry. Co., 218 N. Y. 530, 113 N. E. 504.

The fact that oil produced by defendants was sold in the state through defendant's parent corporation's subsidiary marketing corporation did not constitute "doing business" in the state, where defendants did no business here through officers, directors, or employees. Compania 

M. R. I. v. Compania M. DeO., S. A., 250 N. Y. 203, 164 

N. E. 907.

Where a corporation has avowedly and notoriously held itself out as maintaining an office in the state, and has repeatedly and continuously made substantial purchases in that office, where it has kept books, etc., incidental to the business, it is doing business in the state. 

Hartshein v. Seidenbach's, Inc., 129 Misc. 687, 222 N. 

Y. S. 404. 

Where the defendant foreign corporation maintains an office in New York under the direction of a sales agent with numerous salesmen and clerical assistants, and orders were systematically and regularly solicited in New York and filled in Pennsylvania as a part of an established course of business, the defendant was doing business in the state, and could be sued here; and the validity of the service of the summons is independent of the origin of the cause of action. Tauza v. Susquehanna Coal Co., 220 N. Y. 259, 115 N. E. 915. 

Where brokers in the state take orders subject to approval and acceptance by a foreign corporation for goods manufactured without the state, the foreign corporation is not doing business in the state. Clift & 

Goodrich v. Collier Mills, Inc., 204 App. Div. 539, 198 

N. Y. S. 407.

A foreign insurance corporation that maintains a representative in the state for the purpose of adjusting claims of loss, is doing business in the state. Henriques v. Gauthiod Marine Ins. Co., 205 App. Div. 8, 199 N. Y. S. 131.

Maintaining a resident buyer and office with facilities here is doing business within the state. Merchan dise Reporting Co. v. Oransky & Sons, 133 Misc. 890, 

234 N. Y. S. 83. 

§109. A Foreign Executor or Administrator. 

A foreign executor or administrator may sue in the courts of New York. "Foreign administrators and executors may sue in the same manner as non-residents, for comity may enlarge the measure of their rights as plaintiffs without encroaching upon the jurisdiction of other courts or overstepping the limits of their own." Helme v. Buckelew, 229 N. Y. 363, 372, 128 N. E. 216. 

§110. Unincorporated Associations. 

Any special proceeding or action on any cause of action owned by an unincorporated association, consisting of seven or more members, may be brought by its president or treasurer. (Gen❜l. Ass'ns. L. §12.) §111. Real Party in Interest.

The real party in interest must prosecute the action, except that (a) an executor or administrator, or (b) a trustee of an express trust, or (c) one expressly authorized by statute, may sue without joining with him the person for whose benefit the action is prosecuted. (C. P. A. §210.) A receiver may sue for and collect all the debts, demands and rents belonging to the debtor. In a proper case, the receiver may sue in the debtor's name. (Rule 175.)

It is sufficient that assignee has a valid transfer as against the assignor and holds legal title to the demand. 

Defendant has no legal interest to inquire whether the transfer was actual sale or merely colorable or whether a consideration was paid, since a discharge by the assignee will protect him. Sheridan v. Mayor, 68 N. Y. 30.

An assignee of choses in action by a written assignment, valid upon its face, is the real party in interest,

PARTIES 

59 although others may have an ultimate beneficial interest in the proceeds. Allen v. Brown, 44 N. Y. 228.

One empowered by instrument to prosecute suits for recovery of debts due the maker, in the name of either, held not trustee of express trust, nor assignee of legal title or equitable interest, but mere attorney in fact, not authorized to sue in his own name as real party in interest. Spencer v. Standard C. & M. Corp., 237 N. Y. 

479, 143 N. E. 651.

This section is permissive only, for it does not forbid an action by the beneficiary or by the trustee with the beneficiary. Hubbell v. Medbury, 53 N. Y. 98.

A person who pledges the money of his partner as security for an obligation may sue for its recovery in his own name. Meinhardt v. Excelsior Brewing Co., 98 

App. Div. 308, 90 N. Y. S. 642. 

An exécutor may sue in his own name for the price of property of the estate, which he has sold. Thompson v. Whitmarsh, 100 N. Y. 35, 2 N. E. 273. 

Sec. 67 of the Negotiable Instruments Law, which provides that the agent of the indorser may sue in his own name for the benefit of the indorser, makes such agent a trustee. Mechanics & Metal Nat. Bank v. Termini, 117 Misc. 309, 191 N. Y. S. 334.

Although a foreign corporation may not sue another foreign corporation in New York where the cause of action arose outside the state, if the same claim is assigned to a bona fide resident of the state, the action may be maintained here. McCauley v. Georgia Railroad 

Bank, 122 Misc. 632, 203 N. Y. S. 550, aff'd. 209 App. 

Div. 886, 205 N. Y. S. 935, aff'd. 239 N. Y. 504, 147 

N. E. 171. 

§112. Nonassignable Claims. 

Claims which cannot be assigned include: (1) a cause of action to recover for a personal injury or for a breach of promise to marry; (2) where the claim is based on a grant, which is made void by a state statute; or upon a claim to or interest in real property, a grant of which, by the transferror, would be void by such statute; (3) where a transfer

of the claim is expressly forbidden by a state or federal statute, or would contravene public policy. (Per. Prop. L. 

§41.) §113. Many Persons. 

Where the question is one of common or general interest of many persons or where the persons who might be made parties are very numerous and it may be impracticable to bring them all before the court, one or more may sue or defend for the benefit of all. (C. P. A. §195.)

Four persons are sufficient to constitute "Many per sons" under section 195. Climax Specialty Co. v. Seneca 

Button Co., 54 Misc. 152, 103 N. Y. S. 822.

Section 195 authorizes one person to sue on behalf of himself and others similarly situated where their interest is common; and section 193 provides that where a person not a party to the action who is interested in the subject thereof, or in real property, the title to which may in any manner be affected by the judgment, makes application to be made a party, the court must direct him to be brought in by proper amendment. A suit was instituted by certain stockholders of a corporation, on behalf of themselves and others similarly situated, to set aside the foreclosure of a mortgage on the corporate property as fraudulent, and was continued on the calendar for 10 years without being brought to trial. Held, that stockholders who had acquiesced in plaintiff's conduct of the suit until after their right to maintain an independent action was barred by the statute of limitations were not entitled to be joined as plaintiffs, though they might have the right to share in plaintiffs' recovery in case they obtained judgment. 

MacArdell v. Olcott, 62 App. Div. 127, 70 N. Y. S. 930. 

§114. Joinder of Parties Plaintiff. 

All persons may be joined in one action as plaintiffs in whom any right to relief in respect of or arising out of the same transaction or series of transactions is alleged to exist whether jointly, severally or in the alternative, where if

PARTIES 

61 they brought separate actions, a common question of law or fact would arise, unless it is shown, on application of a party, that such joinder may embarrass or delay the trial.² 

(C. P. A. §209.) 

Where a cause of action was alleged to exist in favor of 193 plaintiffs, each on a separate state of facts, against the underwriters of stock which the plaintiffs had been induced to purchase by a false prospectus issued by the defendants, the court held that the plaintiffs were properly joined in one action, since their causes of action involved common questions of law and fact, arose out of the same transaction, and the joinder, instead of embarrassing or delaying the trial of the action, would facilitate and advance it. Akely v. Kinnicutt, 208 App. 

Div. 487, 203 N. Y. S. 741.

This section does not authorize the joinder as parties plaintiff of the maker and the payee of a certified check, paid by the defendant bank on a forged indorsement, their claims not being co-ordinate, alternative nor complementary and not joint, several nor common but mutually destructive. Olsen v. Bankers Trust Co., 205 

App. Div. 669, 199 N. Y. S. 700. 

§115. Parties United in Interest. 

When parties having a cause of action are united in interest they must be joined as plaintiffs. If one who should be a plaintiff refuses to be joined as such, he may be made a defendant. (C. P. A. §194.)

Where two persons are jointly and severally interested in a contract, they should both be plaintiffs when their interest is sued upon. Natter v. Blanchard Co., 153 App. Div. 814, 138 N. Y. S. 969.

The mortgagor and mortgagee must be joined in an action on a fire insurance policy, and if the mortgagee refuses to be made a plaintiff, he may be made a de fendant. Lewis v. Guardian Fire & Life Assur. Co., 

181 N. Y. 392, 74 N. E. 224. 

2 See Peacock v. Tata Sons, 206 App. Div. 145, 200 N. Y. S. 656, p. 119.

The general rule is that one legatee may sue alone for his legacy, but if the fund will not suffice to pay all legatees interested, they should all be made parties by an action in the names of all, or by one for all. Mc- 

Kenzie v. L'Amoureux, 11 Barb. 516. 

§116. Nonjoinder and Misjoinder. 

Nonjoinder and misjoinder of parties cannot defeat an action or special proceeding. Parties may be added, substituted or dropped, by order of the court at any stage of the cause as the ends of justice may require. (C. P. A. §192; 

Mun. Ct. Code §27.) A motion to correct a defect of parties plaintiff should be made within 20 days after service of the complaint. (Rules 102; 105.) 

§117. New Party. 

When the plaintiff dies and the cause of action survives, the proper person may be made a party upon application.³ (C. P. A. §§83, 84, 85, 86, 87.) $118. Defendants.

The party adverse to the plaintiff is called the defendant. (C. P. A. §191.) Natural persons, domestic corporations, foreign corporations, assignees, poor persons and married women may be defendants. See provisions governing plaintiffs. 

§119. Infancy. 

An infant may defend an action through a guardian ad litem appointed by the court to protect his interests, after the summons has been served on the infant. (C. P. A. 

§§202-204.) The provisions for appointment, notice and qualifications are the same as in the case of the appointment of a guardian ad litem for an infant plaintiff. When no guardian ad litem has been appointed on application, the 

3 See p. 29.

PARTIES 

63 court may do so at any stage of any action or proceeding. (C. P. A. §§206, 207.) If no application for the appointment is made on behalf of the infant within 20 days after service on him, the application may be made by any other party to the action, but the guardian ad litem can not be nominated by such adverse party. (Rules 39, 40.) The court may appoint a guardian ad litem for a nonresident infant defendant or a resident infant defendant who is absent from the state. (C. P. A. §206.)* 

$120. In the Municipal Court. 

After filing proof of service of the summons against an infant defendant, no other proceeding in the action can be taken until a guardian ad litem has been appointed. (Mun. 

Ct. Code §26.) 

A guardian at litem can only be regularly appointed for an infant defendant after personal or substituted service. Ingersoll v. Mangam, 84 N. Y. 622. 

Where an infant defendant in an action for foreclosure of a mortgage is served with the summons, but a guardian ad litem for him is not appointed, the judgment is voidable, but not absolutely void. McMurray v. McMurray, 66 N. Y. 175.

The appointment of a guardian ad litem cannot be attacked collaterally upon the ground that he was unfit because adversely interested, where the appointment was made upon the application of the ward or his parents and they did not thereafter seek to vacate the appointment. Parish v. Parish, 175 N. Y. 181, 67 N. 

E. 298. 

§121. A Judicially Declared Incompetent. 

A person judicially declared to be incompetent may be made a party defendant by the plaintiff's applying to the court for permission to sue his committee. Where the interest of the committee is adverse to that of the incompe- 

4 See p. 49.

tent, the court may appoint a special guardian ad litem.

(C. P. A. §208.) 

The consent of the court must be obtained before an action can be brought against a person adjudged an incompetent in another state of which he was a resident, where a committee was appointed in this state. 

Dean v. Halliburton, 241 N. Y. 354, 148 N. E. 548. 

§122. Other Incompetents. 

An incompetent not judicially declared to be such may be made a defendant, but the supreme court may appoint a guardian ad litem or a special guardian at any stage of any action or proceeding, when it appears to the court necessary for the protection of the rights and interests of such incompetent. (C. P. A. §207.) The qualifications for such guardian ad litem are the same as for a guardian ad litem for an infant. (Rule 40.)

The proper practice, in a case where the defendant is insane, but never judicially declared incompetent, is for the prospective plaintiff to apply to the court for an order designating some person upon whom the summons shall be served. Without such an order and compliance therewith, and without also delivering the summons to the defendant personally, the service of the summons is not complete. Wurlitzer Co. v. Williams, 123 Misc. 777, 206 N. Y. S. 145.

Where it is alleged that one defendant in a partition action is incompetent, although he has never been judicially declared to be incompetent, this question should be determined first, and, if such defendant is found to be incompetent, the court should designate someone to receive the summons on the incompetent's behalf before other proceedings are taken in the action. Bernstein v. Bernstein, 188 App. Div. 277, 176 N. Y. S. 820. $123. Husband or Wife.

The provisions for making a husband or wife a party defendant are discussed under parties plaintiff." 

See p. 52.

PARTIES 

65 

§124. Receivers. 

Before suing a receiver appointed by the court, permission should be obtained from the court. 

Leave of the court to sue a receiver should be obtained before suit, otherwise plaintiff may be in contempt of court. Chautauqua County Bank v. Risley, 19 

N. Y. 369; Di Chiara v. Sutherland, 62 Misc. 555, 115 

N. Y. S. 622. $125. The State.

Ordinarily the state may be made a defendant only with its consent. Certain claims may be presented to the Court of Claims. 

§126. Torts of State Officers. 

The state has waived its immunity and has consented to have its liability for the torts of its officers and employees determined by the court of claims in accordance with the rules that apply to an action in the supreme court against an individual or a corporation. (Court of Claims Act, §12-a.) 

§127. The State and Real Property Actions. 

In an action affecting real property upon which the people of the state have a claim or lien under the Tax Law, the people of the state may be made a defendant in the same manner as a private person. (C. P. A. §214.) 

§128. A City or Other Municipal Corporation. 

The general rule is that a city or other municipal corporation may be sued if the cause of action arose out of some transaction wherein such municipal corporation was acting in a private and not in a governmental capacity. (Cooley, Municipal Corporations, p. 471.) An unusual exception to the general rule that a city will not recompense for injury inflicted by a governmental agency is found in

Local Laws of the City of New York, 1927, No. 13. The 

Board of Estimate and Apportionment has discretion to make an award to a person injured by a police officer while such officer is engaged in arresting any person or retaking any person who has escaped from legal custody or in executing legal process. The law was enacted in order to give some remedy to innocent passersby. 

§129. Domestic Corporations. 

For purposes of jurisdiction and service of summons in an action in the county court, a domestic corporation or joint-stock association is deemed a resident of a county wherein it has a plant, shop, factory or office; or, in the case of railroad corporation, where any portion of the road operated by it is within the county. (C. P. A. §68.) 

§130. Foreign Corporations. 

An action against a foreign corporation may be maintained by a resident or a domestic corporation for any cause of action. (Gen'l. Corp. L. §224.) However, certain jurisdictional facts must be present. See pp. 54-58. §131. Nonresidents.

A nonresident may be sued by a resident for any cause of action and the situs of the cause of action is immaterial." 

In an action ex contractu, the courts of this state will assume jurisdiction even though both parties are nonresidents and the contract was made without the state. (Reeve v. 

Cromwell, 227 App. Div. 32, 237 N. Y. S. 20.) The courts have discretion to refuse to take jurisdiction in cases arising from torts committed outside the state when both plaintiff and defendant are nonresidents. (Gregonis v. P. & R. 

Coal & Iron Co., 235 N. Y. 152, 139 N. E. 223; Murnan v. 

Wabash R. R. Co., 246 N. Y. 244, 158 N. E. 508; Douglas v. • See p. 55.

PARTIES 

67 

N. Y., N. H. & H. R. R. Co., 248 N. Y. 580, 162 N. E. 532; 

Banco de la Lagune v. Escobar, 135 Misc. 165, 237 N. Y. S. 267.) $132. Nonresident Motorists.

The mere operation by a nonresident of a motor vehicle or motorcycle on a public highway in this state, or such operation of a motor vehicle or motorcycle owned by a nonresident if operated with his express or implied consent, is deemed an appointment of the secretary of state as attorney to receive the summons in any action against him growing out of an accident or collision in which such motor vehicle may be involved while being operated on a state highway. (Vehicle and Traffic L. §52.) 

The date of the accident is of vital importance when an action is brought to charge the nonresident owner with the negligence of a driver other than the owner. The present law was effective March 3, 1930. The previous wording of §52 (L. 1929) and the corresponding section of the Highway Law, §285-a (L. 1928), omitted the provision for making a nonresident owner a defendant if his motor vehicle was operated by another with his consent. Section 285-a of the Highway Law, which contained this provision (L. 1929), was repealed by §52 of the Vehicle and Traffic Law before its effective date. In brief, the provision for making a nonresident owner a party defendant when the accident occurred while another was driving his motor vehicle with his consent, has been effectively in the statute only since 

March 3, 1930. $133. A Foreign Executor or Administrator.

An action in personam can not be brought against a foreign executor or administrator. The remedy is under the 

7 See p. 87 for service of the summons.

8889 

Surrogate's Court Act by way of ancillary letters. (Rogers v. Gould, 210 App. Div. 15, 205 N. Y. S. 755.) "Foreign administrators and executors may be sued in the same manner as non-residents, but only when the subject matter subjects them to the jurisdiction, for comity, though it may enlarge their rights, cannot, unless it is also the comity of the domicile, enlarge their liabilities." Helme v. Buckelew, 229 N. Y. 363, 372, 128 N. E. 216. $134. Unincorporated Associations.

When a person has a cause of action against an unincorporated association, he must sue the president or treasurer thereof. Any partnership or other company of persons which has a president or treasurer is deemed such associa tion. (Gen'l. Ass'ns. L. §13.) 

$135. Unknown Defendant.

Where the plaintiff is ignorant of the name or part of the name of the defendant he may designate that defendant in the summons and in any other process or proceeding in the action, by a fictitious name or by as much of his name as is known, adding a description identifying the person intended. Where the plaintiff demands judgment against an unknown person, he may designate that person as unknown, adding a description tending to identify him. When the name or the remainder of the name of the person becomes known, an order must be made by the court that the proceedings already taken be deemed amended by substi tuting the true name. (C. P. A. §215; Mun. Ct. Code §19.) §136. Joinder of Defendants. 

All persons may be joined as defendants against whom the right to relief is alleged to exist, whether jointly, severally or in the alternative; and judgment may be taken against such one or more of the defendants as may be found

PARTIES 

69 liable, according to their respective liabilities. (C. P. A. §211; Mun. Ct. Code §27.) It is not necessary that each defendant be interested as to all the relief prayed for, or as to every cause of action; but the court may relieve any defendant from attending proceedings in which he may have no interest. (C. P. A. §212.) A motion to correct a defect of parties defendant should be made within 20 days of the service of the pleading joining such defendants. (Rules 102; 105.)

Notwithstanding the discretion of the court, it is improper to strike out the name of one defendant, unless the party objecting to the joinder shows undue prejudice. Bossak v. National Surety Co., 205 App. Div. 

707, 200 N. Y. S. 148.

Where the defendants have lived in the same house owned by the plaintiff and it is sought to hold each liable for reasonable rental value of the portion occupied by him, they may be joined as defendants, for it is not necessary that all causes in the complaint should affect all parties. S. L. & Co., Inc., v. Block, 118 Misc. 

756, 194 N. Y. S. 773.

It is proper to join as defendants several physicians who, the plaintiff claims, were negligent in setting his arm. However, the causes of action should be separately stated and numbered. Sherlock v. Manwaren, 208 App. 

Div. 538, 203 N. Y. S. 709.

An administrator bringing an action to recover damages for wrongful death of his intestate cannot join as defendants two or more parties who are alleged to have been responsible for such death, but whose alleged wrongful acts, neglect or breaches of duty are separate and distinct. Ader v. Blau, 241 N. Y. 7, 148 N. E. 771. 

8137. Persons Severally Liable.

Two or more persons severally liable on the same written instrument, including the parties to a bill of exchange or a promissory note, whether the action is brought upon the instrument, or by a party thereto to recover against other

parties liable over to him, may all or any of them be defendants in the same action at the option of the plaintiff. (C. P. 

A. $216.)

A defendant in a pending action of ejectment or to recover a chattel, against whom a person not a party makes demand for the same debt or property, may apply for an order joining such claimant as co-defendant. (C. P. A. §287.) 

§138. Alternate Defendants. 

Where the plaintiff is in doubt as to who is liable he may join two or more defendants to determine, as between the parties, which and to what extent any of the defendants is liable. (C. P. A. §213.) §139. Necessary Parties. 

The court may determine the controversy as between the parties before it, where it can do so without prejudice to the rights of others or by saving their rights, but where a complete determination cannot be had without the presence of other parties, they must be brought in. And where a party to an action shows that some third person is or will be liable to such party for the claim made against such party, the court may order such person to be brought in as a party to the action. And where one not a party to an action has an interest in the subject thereof or in real property the title to which may be affected by the judgment, or in real property for injury to which the complaint demands relief, and makes application, the court must direct him to be brought in. However, the controversy between the defendants shall not delay a judgment to which the plaintiff is entitled, unless the court otherwise directs. (C. P. A. $193.)

An application to have a third person made a party under Sec. 193 should be granted, (1) where the third 

8 For "unknown defendants," see p. 68.

PARTIES 

71 person is liable to the plaintiff jointly or severally with the defendant, or (2) where, irrespective of the third person's liability to the plaintiff for the claim sued upon, he is liable to indemnify the defendant thereon. May 

Co. v. Mott Ave. Corp., 121 Misc. 398, 201 N. Y. S. 189.

The provisions of section 193 were intended to permit a party who is charged with some sort of liability to bring in another party liable to contribute or indemnify the party charged. It is improper to permit plaintiff to bring in as defendants, parties liable under different contracts and circumstances concerning which the facts involved were separate, distinct and unrelated as to time and subject matter. Therefore, a plaintiff is not permitted to bring in as a party defendant a person against whom the plaintiff might have another and distinct cause of action of an entirely different nature and which would be governed by different rules. And he cannot bring in as party defendant any one who might ultimately be liable to the plaintiff in the event that the original defendant successfully defended the action. 

Stern v. George P. Ide & Co., 212 App. Div. 714, 209 

N. Y. S. 473. 

The defendant in an action for negligence in placing marine insurance was permitted to bring in the party who had acted as defendant's agent in procuring said insurance. The court said: "The purpose of this provision (§193) of the Civil Practice Act was to avoid circuity and multiplicity of actions, and to encourage and authorize determination of damages and liability in one suit." Travlos v. Commercial Union of America, Inc., 217 App. Div. 352, 359, 217 N. Y. S. 459, 466.

In a suit upon a special agreement of the defendants to forward and deliver merchandise at a certain time, the defendants may under section 193 have the railroad shipping the goods made a party defendant as having delayed the shipment. Wichert, Inc. v. Gallagher & Ascher, 200 N. Y. S. 957, 201 N. Y. S. 186.

In an action by plaintiff for breach of contract consisting of the alleged failure of the defendant to return to the plaintiff skins delivered to the defendant to be dressed and dyed, the defendant is not entitled, under section 193, to an order directing that certain other parties to whom the defendant claims to have delivered the skins be brought in as parties defendant, as the third

parties were liable to defendant regardless of plaintiff's ownership of the skins. New Netherland Bank of N. Y. v. Goodman, 201 N. Y. S. 188.

In an action brought by taxpayers against the board of supervisors and town auditors to vacate certain audits of town accounts and to restrain the board from levying a tax, the persons in whose favor the audits were made were necessary parties. Osterhoudt v. Bd. of Supervisors, 98 N. Y. 239.

In an action by a mortgagor to recover upon a fire insurance policy, the mortgagee to whom the loss is payable to the extent of his interest is a necessary party, and if such mortgagee refuses to join as a plaintiff he may be made a party defendant. Lewis v. Guardian Fire & Life Assur. Co., 181 N. Y. 392, 74 N. E. 224.

Where plaintiff sued a town and the receiver of a railroad company to recover for injuries received when his automobile, which became stalled on a railroad crossing because the highway was being repaired, was hit by a train operated by defendant receiver, the court may direct that the contractor, who was repairing the highway, be made a party defendant on application of the defendant receiver. Fisher v. Bullock, 204 App. Div. 

523, 198 N. Y. S. 538.

In an action brought by plaintiffs to recover for injuries sustained while riding in defendant's taxicab, a motion by defendant to bring in a street railway company as party defendant should not be granted. Although it might be shown that the street railway company was concurrently liable, the plaintiffs chose to sue the party who contracted to carry them safely and they should not be compelled to sue the other. In addition, the plaintiff's rights would be prejudiced by the delay occasioned by bringing in the additional party. Greenhouse v. Rochester Taxicab Co., 218 App. Div. 228, 218 

N. Y. S. 167, aff'd. 244 N. Y. 559, 155 N. E. 896.

In an action on promissory notes, parties alleged by defendants to be eventually liable to pay the same, held properly brought in as defendants. Williams v. Tompkins, Inc., 211 App. Div. 17, 206 N. Y. S. 637. "The plaintiff in an action at law who seeks nothing but a money judgment, cannot be compelled to bring in other parties than those he has chosen to make defend-

PARTIES 

73 133 ants." Chapman v. Forbes, 123 N. Y. 532, 26 N. E. 3.

And the supreme court has no authority to compel the plaintiff to bring in as a defendant a third party upon the third party's application where only a money judgment is sought and no specific property is involved. 

Bauer v. Dewey, 166 N. Y. 402, 60 N. E. 30. In these two cases the theory is that if a third person were permitted to become a party, issues would be introduced other than those tendered by the plaintiff. In the latter case the plaintiff's title was not assailed. However, where a third person claims to be the owner of a pending cause of action and the plaintiff therein is a pretender, the third party may, under section 193, become a party to the action and litigate the basic issue of plaintiff's right to maintain the action. Bulova v. Barnet, Inc., 194 App. Div. 418, 185 N. Y. S. 920.

The divorced wife of an insured, who was the original beneficiary under the policy, held neither a necessary nor a proper party to a proceeding by the insured's children, who had procured their substitution as beneficiaries after the divorce was granted. Wilcox v. Mutual 

Life Ins. Co., 235 N. Y. 590, 139 N. E. 746. 

Where the purchaser, in the contract of sale, represented there was no broker in the transaction, a broker cannot bring in the purchaser as a party defendant, in an action for commission on such sale, on the ground that the purchaser would be liable to the seller if the seller was found liable to the plaintiff. May Co. v. Mott 

Ave. Corp., 121 Misc. 398, 201 N. Y. S. 189.

In an action for damages caused by impurities in beer coloring sold, the seller was not entitled to have the party who sold him the coloring brought in as a defendant. Neuss, Hesslein & Co. v. National A. & C. 

Co., Inc., 120 Misc. 164, 197 N. Y. S. 808.

Section 211-a of the Civil Practice Act, which per mits contribution between joint tort-feasors does not authorize a defendant in a personal injury action to bring in a joint tort-feasor as party defendant under §193, subd. 2. Rowe v. Denler, 135 Misc. 286, 238 N. 

Y. S. 9. 

§140. New Parties. 

Where a defendant sets up a counterclaim which raises. questions between him and the plaintiff along with any

other persons, he must set forth the names of all persons who might be defendants in a cross action. If such person is not already a party, he must be served with a copy of the answer and thereby becomes a defendant in the action. (C. P. A. §271.) When the defendant dies and the cause of action survives, the proper person may be made a party upon application. (C. P. A. §§83, 84, 85, 86, 87.) Where a defendant has been served by substituted service in any manner other than personally without the state and he does not appear, he or his representative, on application and sufficient cause shown, at any time before final judgment must be allowed to defend the action. In any action other than for divorce or where the contrary is specified by law, such person must be allowed to defend after final judgment and within one year of personal written notice thereof. (C. P. 

A. §217.) §141. Interpleader. 

When a sum of money exceeding fifty dollars is due and payable on a contract and is claimed or demanded by adverse claimants, the debtor may bring suit demanding judgment of interpleader and that the debtor be permitted to pay the amount due into court and that he thereupon be discharged from further liability to any party to the action (C. P. A. §285.) This action is sometimes called dep sit.

The same general procedure may be followed by a defen ant in a pending action of ejectment or to recover a ch ttel when a person not a party makes a demand against him for the same debt or property. Such claimant may be substituted in his place and the petitioner discharged. (. P. 

A. §287.) An order of interpleader joining adverse cimants as parties may be had in the municipal court pon application of the defendant in an action to recover up on a contract or to recover a chattel. (Mun. Ct. Code, §27.) 

⁹ See p. 29.

CHAPTER V  §142. Commencement of the Action. 

1 A civil action is commenced by service of a summons. 

The court acquires jurisdiction of the defendant by such service or by his voluntary appearance. (C. P. A. §§218; 

237.) Although a summons is a mandate of the court, it is issued by the plaintiff or his attorney. In the municipal court the clerk may also issue the summons and, when he does, the action is deemed commenced, within the meaning of the statute of limitations. (Mun. Ct. Code §18.) 

Even in courts of equity, a plaintiff must have a cause of action for some relief at the commencement of his suit. If he has such right, the extent of the relief will be adapted to the situation at the time of the decree. Wappler v. Woodbury Co., 246 N. Y. 152, 158 

N. E. 56. 

§143. Prerequisites. 

Where the plaintiff is an infant, the guardian ad litem should be appointed before the summons is issued.2 According to the language of the statute, in municipal court actions the guardian ad litem must be appointed before the summons is issued. (Mun. Ct. Code §26, subd. 1.) Application for the appointment of a guardian at litem for a defendant who is an incompetent but not judicially declared to be such should be made. Permission of the court must be obtained before suing a judicially declared incompetent for whom a committee has been appointed; and also before suing a receiver appointed by the court." 

1 For taking depositions before action is commenced, see p. 183. 

2 See p. 50. 

3 See p. 64. 

See p. 63 and p. 65. 75

$144. Contents of the Summons. 

The summons must contain: 5 6 

(1) The name of the court and, if the action is brought in the supreme or city court, the name of the county. (2) The title (the names and designations of the parties). (3) The name and office and post-office address of the plaintiff's attorney, or of the plaintiff, if he is not represented by an attorney. (4) The body of the summons informing the defendant that he must answer the complaint or appear, if the summons is served without a complaint or notice, within the time limited by statute, and if he fails to answer or appear judgment will be taken against him for relief demanded in the complaint. 

(5) The date. (Rule 45; C. P. A. §236; City Ct. Act §49.) 

Where the action is brought for breach of an express contract to pay absolutely or on a contingency a sum of money fixed by the contract or capable of being ascertained therefrom by computation; or on an express or implied contract to pay money received or disbursed, or for value of property delivered, or for services rendered by, to or for the use of the defendant or a third person; and if the complaint is not served with the summons, the plaintiff may serve with the summons a notice stating the sum of money for which judgment will be taken in case of default. (Rule 46.) 

Such notice obviates the necessity of serving a complaint if the defendant defaults. (Rule 46.)

An action by a copartnership must be brought in the individual names of the partners, and a motion on the ground that it appears upon the face of the com- 

5 For the form, see Rule 45 and Mun. Ct. Code §20. 

6 For necessary endorsement in matrimonial actions, see p. 281.

plaint that the plaintiff has not legal capacity to sue will be sustained if the action is brought in some adopted name. Union Wine Co. v. Green, 62 Misc. 551, 115 N. Y. S. 921.

Designating the christian names of the parties in the summons by their initials is an irregularity which may be waived or amended. Grant v. Birdsall, 2 Civ. 

Proc. R. 422, 16 J. & S. 427.

The court may on the trial allow pleadings to be amended by striking out the words "and son" and inserting in place the name of the son. Bannerman v. 

Quackenbush, 11 Daly 529.

If the defendant is known by two names, he may be sued in either. Anderson v. Horn, 23 Abb. N. C. 475, 10 N. Y. S. 8.

If the person served has been incorrectly named in the summons, but the complaint served with the summons contains statements regarding him, so that he must have been apprised that he was the person intended to be served, he has been brought into court and must defend. Stuyvesant v. Weil, 167 N. Y. 421, 60 N. E. 738. 

Where the averments of a complaint show that the plaintiff sues only as executor, the omission in the title to the action of the word "as" is immaterial. Beers v. 

Shannon, 73 N. Y. 292.

When a complaint shows a cause of action in favor of plaintiff, not in a representative but in his individual capacity, the addition of the words "executor," etc., in the title, and an allegation in the complaint that he is executor of the will of a deceased person named, the descriptive words may be rejected, leaving the action to stand as one in the individual capacity of plaintiff. 

Litchfield v. Flint, 104 N. Y. 543, 11 N. E. 58.

Where it appears that in the summons and in the title of the complaint the defendant is named only in his individual capacity and that in the complaint no cause of action is stated against him in that capacity, but only in his representative capacity as trustee under the will of a certain decedent, there is a defect of parties defendant. Leonard v. Pierce, 182 N. Y. 431, 75 

N. E. 313.

A motion to vacate a service of summons on the ground that it was signed by plaintiff personally, and not by his attorney, was properly denied, in view of §236. O'Brien v. Lashar, 206 App. Div. 623, 199 N. Y. 

S. 34, aff'd. 236 N. Y. 602, 142 N. E. 301.

The supreme court has power under C. P. A. §105 to permit the amendment of the summons and complaint by changing the designation of the defendant from trustee to that of an individual and the effect is not to bring in a new defendant but to change the capacity in which he is sued. Boyd v. U. S. Mortgage & 

Trust Co., 187 N. Y. 262, 79 N. E. 999.

Under C. P. A. §215, providing that, where plaintiff is ignorant of the name or names of the defendants, he may designate them in the summons by fictitious names, a summons in an action for partition may recite the names of the defendants who have not been heard from for over 30 years, each name being followed by fictitious names designated as the widow and heirs of such persons, deceased. Snyder v. Parezo, 151 App. Div. 110, 135 N. Y. S. 960, aff'd. 206 N. Y. 689, 99 N. E. 1118. $145. In the Municipal Court.

In the municipal court the summons must give, in addition to the above: (1) The plaintiff's address. (2) The borough, district and address of the court. (3) The amount of the judgment which will be taken if defendant defaults. (4) The signature of the clerk, if it is issued by him. (5) A brief statement of the nature and substance of the cause of action which must be endorsed on or attached to the summons, if a complaint is not served therewith. (Mun. Ct. Code §§19, 20; 78.) (6) If no verified complaint is served with the summons in an action where execution against the person may issue upon the judgment, the summons and the copy delivered to the defendant must bear the endorsement substantially in the following language: "Plaintiff claims defendant is liable

to arrest and imprisonment in this case." (Mun. Ct. Code $25.) (7) The provisions are the same in an action for penalty or forfeiture except that the endorsement must describe the statute or ordinance. (Mun. Ct. Code §24.) 

A demand for a jury trial may be endorsed on the summons. (Mun. Ct. Code §118.) The form for a municipal court summons is given in §20 of the Municipal Court Code. 

The defendant is required to answer, not answer or appear. 

(Mun. Ct. Code §§19, 20.) 

If no complaint is served with the summons and a statement of the nature and substance of the cause of action is not endorsed on or attached to the summons in a municipal court action, the summons is void and cannot be amended. Steffens v. Martin, 100 Misc. 263, 165 N. Y. S. 445.

If the defendant was not misled by the omission of the plaintiff's address, such defect is only an irregularity and does not divest the court of jurisdiction. Hober v. Reikert, 97 Misc. 637, 162 N. Y. S. 328.

The omission of the date on the summons is a mere clerical error, and does not prejudice the rights of the defendant, nor is it a jurisdictional defect. Mayerson v. 

Cohen, 123 App. Div. 646, 108 N. Y. S. 59. $146. Supplemental Summons.

When a new party is brought into the action by direction of the court, whether upon application or not, he must be served with a supplemental summons in the same form as the original. (C. P. A. §§193; 219; Rule 48.) If a person not a party is affected by a counterclaim, he may be made a party defendant by serving him with a copy of the answer. 

(C. P. A. §271.) §147. Service. 

A summons is served upon a defendant: (1) personally; (2) by substituted service; or (3) by publication.

$148. Personal Service.

Personal service upon a natural person must be made by delivering a copy of the summons to the defendant in person, if he is twenty-one years of age or over and not judicially declared to be incompetent. (C. P. A. §225.) 

Where the process server tendered the summons to the defendant's mother-in-law when told the defendant was not at home and upon her refusal to receive them dropped them on the floor and left, service was not made. The fact that the mother-in-law gave the summons to the defendant did not make the service good, because a casual delivery of the summons to the defendant by a person not acting under the authority of the plaintiff, nor at his request, does not affect the commencement of an action, nor does it constitute service, within the meaning of the C. P. A. Service must be proved, either by the admission of the defendant in writing or by affidavit of the server. Mecca v. Young, 133 Misc. 540, 233 N. Y. S. 169. See also O'Connell v. 

Gallagher, 104 App. Div. 492, 93 N. Y. S. 643. 

Where the process server went to the home of the defendant and was met at the door by the defendant's husband, to whom he stated that he had a summons and complaint to serve upon the defendant, and her husband called to her in an adjoining room, stating the object of the process server's call, and she replied telling her husband to take the papers for her, whereupon the process server handed them to defendant's husband, held an insufficient service upon the defendant. Ives v. Darling, 210 App. Div. 521, 206 N. Y. S. 493.

It is not a good service of a summons to deposit it on a chair in a room in which defendant is, without asking for defendant by name, or stating the nature of the paper, and without offering to deliver it into defendant's hands. Correll v. Granget, 12 Misc. 209, 34 

N. Y. S. 25. 

Where the summons and complaint were delivered to the defendant by one of her employees who found them on the floor of a room where the process server dropped them, proper service was not made and a judgment entered against the defendant upon her alleged

default is void. O'Connell v. Gallagher, 104 App. Div. 

492, 93 N. Y. S. 643.

Service of the summons must be made in an orderly manner. "A person may not assault another to effect service, and papers violently thrust upon the person, even though the act may convey some information to the party intended to be served, is not good service, and will be held void." Anderson v. Abeel, 96 App. Div. 

370, 374, 89 N. Y. S. 254, 256.

Service of the summons and complaint made by a process server who gained access forcibly to the room where defendant was at the time, after having obtained an entrance into defendant's residence upon the pretext that she wanted to see one of the servants, is illegal and will be set aside. Olson v. McConihe, 54 Misc. 

48, 105 N. Y. S. 386.

Any trick or device which deprives a defendant of a fair notice that an action has been commenced is fraud. Putting defendant in the unknown possession of a summons, disguised, so as to conceal from her its nature, just as she is embarking upon a sea voyage, is not good service; nor does the subsequent discovery of the contents, when she is beyond the limits of the state, make it good. Bulkley v. Bulkley, 6 Abb. Pr. 307. 

Where there has been any deceit for the purpose of bringing a person within the jurisdiction in order that he may be served with a summons, the service will be set aside. Baker v. Wales, 45 How. Pr. 137.

Where the defendant was enticed into the jurisdiction by fraud and deceit, and the summons was served on him, a motion to vacate the service will be granted. 

Garabettian v. Garabettian, 206 App. Div. 502, 201 N. 

Y. S. 548. 

8149. Designation of Another Person. 

A resident of the state, of full age, may designate another resident as a person upon whom to serve a summons or paper for the commencement of a special proceeding during the absence from the state of the person making the designation. Process may then be served on the person designated as if he were the person who made the designa-

tion. The designation and the consent of the designated person must both be executed and acknowledged like a deed and filed in the office of the clerk of the county where the person making the designation resides. The designation remains in force for the period specified therein or, if no period is specified, for three years after filing. Such designation may be revoked. (C. P. A. §227.) $150. Partners and Joint Debtors.

When suing on a partnership debt or on a joint debt, the plaintiff may serve the summons on only one of the partners or joint debtors." (C. P. A. §1197.)

In an action against joint debtors, service of the summons on one authorizes judgment against all, which may be enforced by execution against the joint property, although the other defendants were not served and did not appear in the action. Sternberger v. Bernheimer, 121 N. Y. 194, 24 N. E. 311. The same rule applies where property of nonresidents has been attached and levied upon. Yerkes v. McFadden, 141 N. Y. 136, 36 N. E. 7. $151. An Infant. If the defendant is an infant, a copy of the summons must be delivered to his father, mother or guardian or, if there is none of these within the state, to the person having the care and control of him, or with whom he resides, or his employer. If the infant is fourteen years of age or over, a copy must be also delivered to the infant in person. The court, in its discretion, may make an order requiring a copy to be also delivered in behalf of the infant to a designated person. When such order is made, service is not complete until such delivery is made. (C. P. A. §225.) Service may be made on the guardian ad litem of a nonresident or absent resident infant defendant. (C. P. A. §224.) 

7 For judgment and execution in such case, see pp. 230, 242, 249. 

8 For guardian ad litem of infant defendant, see p. 62.

Where the summons in an action against an infant aged 18 years was served upon him personally, but no service was had against the other requisite persons, the court acquired no jurisdiction; nor was the defect in service cured by the filing of an answer. McGrath v. 

Roefs, 119 Misc. 641, 196 N. Y. S. 891.

A judgment is not binding upon a defendant who at the time of its recovery was an infant, and who was not served with the summons, and never made any application to appear, by guardian or otherwise, notwithstanding the summons was served upon a guardian ad litem appointed on the application of plaintiff, where it was not shown on such application that the infant was not temporarily absent from the state. Smith v. Reid, 134 N. Y. 568, 31 N. E. 1082. 

§152. A Judicially Declared Incompetent. 

If the defendant has been judicially declared to be incompetent to manage his affairs and for whom a committee has been appointed, a copy of the summons must be delivered to the committee and also to the defendant; but the court, in its discretion, may make an order dispensing with the delivery to the incompetent. Where the court has reason to believe that the interest of the committee is adverse to that of the incompetent, or for any reason he is unfit to protect the rights of the defendant, the court, in its discretion, with or without application, may make an order requiring a copy to be also delivered to a designated person. 

When such order is made, service is not complete until such delivery is made. (C. P. A. §§225, 226.) §153. Other Incompetents. 

The court in its discretion, and with or without application, may make an order requiring a copy of the summons to be also delivered to a designated person, where the court has reasonable ground to believe that the defendant is mentally incapable adequately to protect his rights because of habitual drunkenness, or any other cause, although he has not

been judicially declared incompetent to manage his affairs. 

Where the order is made, service is not complete until such delivery is made. (C. P. A. §226.) $154. A Domestic Corporation. 

Personal service on a domestic corporation must be made by delivering a copy to the president or other head of the corporation, the secretary or assistant secretary, or clerk to the corporation, the cashier, the treasurer or assistant treasurer, or a director or managing agent. (C. P. A. §228.)

Service upon the general superintendent of a telephone company is sufficient. Barret v. A. T. & T. Co., 138 N. Y. 491, 34 N. E. 289.

An assistant superintendent of a corporation who has no general supervision over its affairs, and no authority to manage its affairs as agent, but whose duties are really those of a foreman, is not a “managing agent." Kramer v. Buffalo Union Furnace Co., 132 App. 

Div. 415, 116 N. Y. S. 1101.

A person in charge of a branch office, who works under general rules of the company and specific instructions of the company's superintendent, is not a "managing agent" upon whom service may be made.

Kiely v. Utica Gas & Electric Co., 134 Misc. 258, 235 

N. Y. S. 288. 

§155. A Foreign Corporation.10 

Personal service of a summons on a foreign corporation must be made by delivering a copy: (1) To the president, vice-president, treasurer, assistant treasurer, secretary or assistant secretary; or, if the corporation lack any of those officers, to the officer performing corresponding functions. 

⁹ See p. 64. 

10 For the necessary jurisdictional facts which must be present before a foreign corporation can be sued in New York, see pp. 54-58.

(2) To a person or public officer legally designated for the purpose, or, if the designation no longer remains in force, to the secretary of state. (3) To the cashier, a director or managing agent of the corporation, if service cannot be effected under (2), or an officer specified in (1), with due diligence, cannot be found within the state. (C. P. A. §229.) 

Section 229 provides for service on a foreign corporation where the jurisdictional facts appear and is not unconstitutional because it does not specify that service can be made only on corporations doing business in the state. Robert Dollar Co. v. Canadian Car & 

Foundry Co., 220 N. Y. 270, 115 N. E. 711. 

Service cannot be had on an officer only temporarily in the state where the corporation is not doing business in the state. Riverside Mills v. Menefee, 237 U. S. 189; 

Robert Dollar Co. v. Canadian Car & Foundry Co., 220 

N. Y. 270, 115 N. E. 711.

In order that service on the president of a foreign corporation may be good, he must be here officially, representing the corporation in its business. Bagdon v. Phil. & Reading C. & I. Co., 217 N. Y. 432, 111 N. 

E. 1075.

A sales agent of a foreign corporation doing business in the state, who has charge of salesmen and clerical assistants, is a proper person upon whom to make service. Tauza v. Susquehanna Coal Co., 220 N. Y. 259, 115 N. E. 915. 

Before the service can be made on the managing agent of a foreign corporation, diligent effort must be made to serve its officers or its designated agent. Gursky v. Blair, 218 N. Y. 41, 112 N. E. 431; Karosas v. Susquehanna Coal Co., 172 App. Div. 873, 158 N. Y. S. 1021.

Where a receiver of a foreign corporation has been appointed and the officers have been enjoined from interfering with him, the summons in an action cannot be served on such an officer. After the appointment, the receiver was liable for any wrong done in the trans action of the business. Gaboury v. Central Vermont 

Ry. Co., 250 N. Y. 233, 165 N. E. 275.

In so far as §48 of the Municipal Court Code authorizes service of the summons on a foreign corporate defendant by delivering it to the person, in possession of defendant's attached property, it is unconstitutional. 

Drew v. Northwestern Corporation, 133 Misc. 706, 233 

N. Y. S. 292; Nerenberg v. Keith, 101 Misc. 551, 167 

N. Y. S. 612. $156. The State.

Where the people of the state are by law a proper party defendant, the summons must be served on the attorney general or a deputy. (C. P. A. §221.) §157. The City of New York. 

Personal service on the city of New York is made by delivering a copy to the mayor, comptroller or corporation counsel. (C. P. A. §228.) §158. Any Other City. 

Personal service on any other city is made by delivering a copy to the mayor, comptroller, treasurer, counsel, attorney or clerk; or, if the city lack any of those officers, to the officer performing corresponding functions. (C. P. 

A. §228.) 

$159. Nonresident Motorists.

The operation by a nonresident of a motor vehicle or motor cycle on a public highway in this state, or the operation on such highway of a motor vehicle or motor cycle owned by a nonresident with his express or implied consent, is equivalent to the appointment by such nonresident of the secretary of state as his attorney upon whom may be served the summons in any action growing out of any accident or collision in which such nonresident may be involved while operating a motor vehicle on such highway or in which his motor vehicle or motor cycle may be involved while it is being operated on such highway with his con-

sent. Such operation is deemed the consent of such nonresident that such service of the summons shall have the same legal force and validity as personal service within the state. (Vehicle and Traffic Law §52.) 

$160. Service.

Service of the summons on such nonresident may be made in two ways: (1) By delivering a copy of the summons, with a fee of two dollars, to the secretary of state, or to someone in his office, and sending forthwith a notice of such service and a copy of the summons to the defendant by registered mail. 

The defendant's return receipt, the plaintiff's affidavit of service of compliance with these provisions, and a copy of the summons and complaint must be filed immediately with the clerk of the court in which the action is pending. (2) By leaving a copy of the summons and a fee of two dollars with the secretary of state, or in his office, and by delivering a duplicate copy with the complaint annexed, to the defendant personally without the state. Such personal service may be made by a resident or citizen of New York State, or a sheriff, under-sheriff, deputy sheriff or constable of the county or other political subdivision in which service is made, or by an officer authorized by New York law to take acknowledgments of deeds to be recorded in this state, or an attorney or counselor at law duly qualified to practice in the state where service is made, or by a 

United States Marshal. Personal service without the state is complete 10 days after proof thereof is filed. 

and Traffic Law §52.) §161. History of this Law. 

(Vehicle 

A provision similar to the present §52 of the Vehicle and Traffic Law first appeared in the statutes as §285-a of the Highway Law. (L. 1928, July 1.) The original word-

ing provided for the designation of the secretary of state by a nonresident operator only. It did not include the provision for such designation by a nonresident owner when his motor vehicle or motor cycle was operated on a public highway of this state with his consent. Service could be made only by the method outlined under (1) above. The original law was in force until March 3, 1930, when the present §52 of the Vehicle and Traffic Law became effective. 

The original §285-a of the Highway Law had been repealed and its provisions taken over into the Vehicle and Traffic 

Law as §52, by Laws, 1929, Ch. 54. The 1929 Legislature enacted a new §285-a of the Highway Law (L. 1929, Ch. 10) 

which was the same as the present §52 of the Vehicle and Traffic Law except that provision for service in accordance with (2) above was omitted; but before its effective date (July 1, 1929) the same legislature passed the Vehicle and 

Traffic Law (effective date, March 6, 1929) which repealed $285-a of the Highway Law. In brief, the designation of the secretary of state by a nonresident owner where the operation was by another with his consent, and the method of service outlined under (2) above, have been in effect only since March 3, 1930.

A foreign corporation may be served with a summons in accordance with §52 of the Vehicle and Traffic 

Law. Bessen v. Public Service Co-Ordinated Transport, 

135 Misc. 368, 237 N. Y. S. 689. Likewise an infant. 

Gesell v. Wells, 229 App. Div. 11, 240 N. Y. S. 628.

The application of §52 is not restricted to actions in the supreme court. Mailing the summons to the secretary of state is service within the jurisdiction of the city court. Bessen v. Public Service Co-Ordinated Trans port, 135 Misc. 368, 237 N. Y. S. 689.11 Contra as to an action in the municipal court. Osterhaudt v. Horowitz, 135 Misc. 744, 240 N. Y. S. 683. 

11 But see American Historical Society v. Glenn, p. 43.

$162. Time of Service. 

Service of a summons on Sunday is absolutely void. 

(Penal L. §2148.) Service on other legal holidays is valid. 

Anyone who maliciously causes a summons to be served on Saturday on a person who observes Saturday as a holy day, is guilty of a misdemeanor. (Penal L. §2150.)

A service of a summons and complaint made on 

Sunday is absolutely void. Scott Shoe Machinery Co. v. 

Dancel, 63 App. Div. 172, 71 N. Y. S. 263. But a service of a summons and complaint on a legal holiday is valid. 

Flynn v. Union Surety & Guaranty Co., 170 N. Y. 145, 63 N. E. 61. 

§163. Place of Service. 

For the territorial limits within which a summons of a particular court may be served personally, see that court under Chapter III. 

§164. Who May Serve a Summons. 

Personal service may be made by any person eighteen years of age or over who is not a party to the action. The necessity of having the summons served by a competent and conscientious person cannot be overestimated. If the summons is not properly served on the defendant, jurisdiction of the defendant is not obtained, and all proceedings and any judgment in the action may be declared void.12 The summons in an action in the supreme court may be served by the sheriff; in the city court by the sheriff or marshal; and in the municipal court by a marshal. In an action for a penalty or forfeiture to a common informer, the summons must be served by such officer. (C. P. A. §§220, 221; City 

Ct. Act §28; Mun. Ct. Code §§22; 151.) 

It is a mere irregularity for the plaintiff to serve the summons; if the defendant wishes to take advantage 

12 See pp. 80, 259.

of it, he must move at the first opportunity. He may make the motion before but not after judgment. Myers v. Overton, 2 Abb. Pr. 344. 

§165. Proof of Service. 

Proof of personal service is made: (1) By affidavit of the process server. (2) By sheriff's or marshal's certificate. (3) If the defendant be an adult not judicially declared incompetent, by his written admission (acknowledged and certified or accompanied by an affidavit of some one other than the plaintiff that the signature is genuine). (Rule 53;

City Ct. Act §28; Mun. Ct. Code §22.) 

The statements necessary to a proper affidavit or admission are specified in Rule 53. 

§166. Filing. 

Ordinarily the summons and affidavit, certificate or admission of service in supreme and city court actions need not be filed. They must be filed 5 days after notice of the adverse party requiring such filing. (C. P. A. §100.) In the municipal court, these papers must be filed within 3 days after service. (Mun. Ct. Code §22.) Where the demand is for wages for less than $100, and is meritorious, no fees are charged in the municipal court. (Mun. Ct. 

Code §174.) 

Failure to file the summons and complaint within the three days required by §22 of the Municipal Court Code is not a jurisdictional defect requiring vacation of a judgment entered on defendant's default. Abo v. Panish, 135 Misc. 792, 239 N. Y. S. 669. $167. Privilege from Service.

A foreign resident is exempt from service of a summons while attending a hearing or trial in this state as a volun-

tary witness. A resident witness is not exempt from service of a summons, but is exempt from civil arrest. (Frisbie v. Young, 11 Hun 474.)

A resident of another state who has in good faith come into this state to give evidence as a witness in a cause here, is exempt from service of a summons in a civil action against him while in attendance, and for a reasonable time thereafter in which to return. Person v. Grier, 66 N. Y. 124.

The immunity of a nonresident witness extends to a witness who has come within the state for the purpose of testifying at an official inquiry such as the Moreland Commission. In re Ferrari's Ex'rs., 134 Misc. 728, 236 

N. Y. S. 406.

When the defendant has voluntarily come into the state to testify in a proceeding, the subsequent service on him of a subpoena in such proceeding does not destroy his voluntary appearance and does not open the way for service of a summons. Bunce v. Humphrey, 214 N. Y. 

21, 108 N. E. 95.

If a nonresident witness comes into the state for the double purpose of attending court and attending to business which has no connection with the trial, he is not immune and the presumption is that service is regular. 

Finucane v. Warner, 194 N. Y. 160, 86 N. E. 1118. 

A foreign resident, arrested upon a criminal charge in this state and released on bail, is not exempt from service of process in a civil suit. Netograph Manufacturing Co. v. Scrugham, 197 N. Y. 377, 90 N. E. 962. 

§168. Substituted Service. 

If the plaintiff is unable with due diligence to make personal service of the summons on a resident defendant (natural person, domestic corporation other than a municipal corporation, a joint-stock or other unincorporated association having a president or treasurer residing within the state) he may obtain a court or judge's order for a substituted service. (C. P. A. §230; City Ct. Act §51; Mun.

Ct. Code §23.) This method of service is used when the

defendant cannot be found, when his place of sojourn cannot be ascertained, or when he avoids service. It may not be used in a partition proceeding. (C. P. A. §1033; Toole v. 

Larkin, 206 App. Div. 809, 201 N. Y. S. 952.)

The provisions for substituted service of the summons do not violate the constitutional provision that a person shall not be deprived of his property without due process of law. Continental Nat'l Bank v. Thurber, 74 

Hun 632, 26 N. Y. S. 956, aff'd 143 N. Y. 648, 37 N. E. 828. 

Defendant moved to vacate an order authorizing substituted service of summons and service made thereunder. It did not appear from the papers on which the order was founded, that defendant was within the state when the order was granted, or had avoided service, or that proper effort has been made to serve her personally. 

Held, that the motion would be granted. Nichols v. 

Emmett, 56 Misc. 321, 107 N. Y. S. 663.

The summons may be served by substituted service only on a resident. Lynch v. Eustis, 85 N. Y. S. 1063.

Infants may be served by substituted service, and acts of the mother in preventing service may be regarded as the acts of the infants. Steinhardt v. Baker, 163 

N. Y. 410, 57 N. E. 629. 

§169. Manner of Making Substituted Service. 

The order, which is granted only on satisfactory affidavits, must direct that service be made: (1) By leaving copies of the summons and order at the residence (office, in the case of an unincorporated association) of the defendant with a person of suitable age and discretion. (2) Or, if admittance cannot be gained nor such person found who will receive the papers, by affixing the same to the outer or other door of the office or residence and by depositing in a post-office other copies, properly enclosed in a post-paid wrapper, addressed to the defendant at its office or place of business or his residence.
