(3) Or, in the case of a natural person, upon proof by affidavit that no residence can be found, service may be in such manner as the court may direct. 

City Ct. Act §51; Mun. Ct. Code §23.) (C. P. A. §231; 

The order must be filed and service made within 10 days after the order is granted except in the Municipal court, when the time is 5 days. On filing proof of such service the summons is deemed served. (Rule 49; City Ct. Act 

§51; Mun. Ct. Code §25.) 

Substituted service on a natural person must be made at defendant's residence and service at any other place is fatally defective. Fisk v. Bennett, 69 Hun 272, 23 N. Y. S. 471. (Unless the court otherwise directs, see §231, subd. 3.)

The date of filing proof of service fixes the date from which time to answer runs; but delay in filing does not invalidate service. Dealers' Lumber Corp. v. 

Stauffer, 128 Misc. 358, 218 N. Y. S. 464. $170. Proof.

Proof of substituted service consists of the affidavit of the server or the certificate of the sheriff or marshal showing compliance with the order. (Rule 53, subd. 5;

City Ct. Act §28; Mun. Ct. Code §22.) §171. Service by Publication. 

An order for service of the summons by publication may be had: (1) Where the defendant is a foreign corporation; 13 or an unincorporated association whose president and treasurer are both nonresidents; or a domestic corporation, if, after diligent effort, service cannot be made within the state upon the president or other head, the secretary or clerk, the cashier, the treasurer or a director or managing agent; 14 

13 See pp. 54-58. 

14 NOTE List of officers under §232 differs from that under §228.

or a nonresident; or where, after diligent inquiry, the defendant remains unknown to the plaintiff or the plaintiff is unable to ascertain whether or not the defendant is a resident. (2) Where the defendant is a resident and has departed from the state with intent to defraud his creditors or to avoid service, or keeps himself concealed in the state with like intent. (3) Where the defendant is an adult resident and has been continuously without the state more than six months before the granting of the order and has not designated a person on whom summons may be served; or such designation no longer remains in force; or service on the designated person cannot be made within the state after diligent effort. (4) Where complete personal service cannot be made within the state, after due diligence, upon an infant or incompetent; whether a resident or nonresident, under $225. (5) In an action to annul a marriage, for divorce, or for separation. (6) Where the complaint demands judgment affecting defendant's interest in or lien upon specific, real or personal property within the state; or where it appears by affidavit that a warrant of attachment granted in the action has been levied upon property within the state. (7) Where the defendant is a resident or domestic corporation and an attempt was made to commence the action before the expiration of the limitation thereon and the limitation would have expired within 60 days next preceding the application if time had not been extended by the attempt to commence action. (8) Where the action is against the stockholders of a corporation or joint stock company and is authorized by state law and the defendant is such stockholder. (C. P. A. $232.)

A municipal court summons may not be served by pub lication. (Mun. Ct. Code §21.) 

$172. Prerequisites to Order for Publication. 

Before an order to serve the summons on a foreign corporation or nonresident by publication can be obtained in an action in personam, it must appear by satisfactory affidavit that a warrant of attachment has been granted in the action and that levy has been made upon property of the defendant within the state. Otherwise jurisdiction is not obtained. (C. P. A. §232.) 

Except in cases affecting the personal status of the plaintiff, and cases in which that mode of service may be considered to have been assented to in advance, the substituted service by publication against nonresidents is effectual only where property in the state is brought under the control of the court; in other words, where the action is in the nature of a proceeding in rem. In this case the process being in personam, all subsequent proceedings in the case, up to the termination of it in the judgment, were of the same character, and gave the court no jurisdiction over the res. Pennoyer v. Neff, 95 U. S. 714.

In a judgment-creditor's action, the complaint alleged that an assignment of a copyright to a nonresident defendant was made with intent to defraud plaintiff, and that the collection of royalties thereon by the debtor and payment thereof to said assignee was with like intent. 

Held, that a denial of a motion to vacate an order for the service of the summons on the nonresident defendant by publication was error, because the court had no jurisdiction over the res or person. Bryan v. University Pub. Co., 112 N. Y. 382, 19 N. E. 825.

Proof is required of the issuance of a warrant of attachment against the property of a nonresident defendant and the levy thereunder upon his property within the state, as a condition precedent to the granting of an order for service of the summons upon him by publication in an action in personam. Dimmerling v. 

Andrews, 236 N. Y. 43, 139 N. E. 774, and 236 N. Y. 

510, 142 N. E. 263.

In a suit against nonresidents to declare an assignment to them of a bond and mortgage upon property located within the state to be fraudulent, the action is in personam, and the court can only acquire jurisdiction over the defendants by their voluntary appearance, and not by substituted service. McLaughlin v. McLaughlin 

Real Estate Co., 162 App. Div. 644, 147 N. Y. S. 959.

No personal judgment against the mortgagor for a deficiency in an action for the foreclosure of a mortgage can be rendered where the mortgagor is a nonresident, and has neither appeared nor been served with process within the state. Schwinger v. Hickok, 53 N. Y. 280. $173. Application for the Order.

Application for the order to serve by publication must be made: (1) Upon a verified complaint showing a sufficient cause of action against the defendant. (2) Upon a verified complaint to recover a sum of money only and proof by affidavit that a warrant of attachment granted in the action has been levied upon property of the defendant within the state (not applicable where defendant is a domestic corporation and the action is brought by the attorney general under §219-f of the Tax Law) and proof by affidavit of the additional facts required by this section. (3) And also, where the application is made on the ground that the defendant is a foreign corporation or a nonresident under subds. 5, 6, 8 above, the affidavit must show that the plaintiff, with due diligence, has been or will be unable to make personal service. If the defendant is an infant, the affidavit must also set forth the name and last known residence of the father, mother or guardian, or person having control of him, or with whom he resides, or by whom he is employed, or that the name and residence, or either, of such person is unknown to the applicant. (C. P. 

A. §232.)

The order may be made by the court or a judge. (C. P.

A. §234.) §174. Manner of Serving by Publication. 

The order must direct that service be made by: (1) Publication of the summons and notice in two newspapers, in English, designated in the order not less than once in each of six consecutive weeks; and either (2) Depositing on or before the first publication day in a post-office or post-office box one or more copies of the summons, complaint, order and notice under Rule 52, each set properly enclosed in a postpaid wrapper addressed to the defendant (and other proper person in case of an infant); or (3) That such deposit may be dispensed with because the court or judge is satisfied by the affidavits on which the order was granted that with reasonable diligence the plaintiff cannot ascertain a place where such defendant (or any proper person, in the case of an infant) probably would receive matter transmitted through the post-office. (Rules 

50; 52.) 

Where the summons through mistake was published in a newspaper other than the one designated by the order, the defect may be cured by an ex parte order amending the original order nunc pro tunc and substituting the name of the paper in which publication was made for the one designated. Such service was irregular and the defendant could move to vacate, but such a motion is directed to the discretion of the court and where it appears the defendants had formal notice of their right to defend, the court may cure the defect by belated approval of the paper in which publication was made. Valz v. Sheepshead Bay Bungalow Corp., 249 

N. Y. 122, 163 N. E. 124.

An affidavit in support of a motion for leave to serve summons by publication stated that defendant was a nonresident of the state, and recited her place of residence, and that she "is of full age, and that plaintiff will be unable to make personal service of summons

upon said defendant; that the sources of my information and the grounds of my belief are correspondence had with defendant from her said residence in New Jersey, and from conversations had with the son and representative of defendant." Held insufficient, as it neither stated that "due diligence had been used," or set forth the correspondence to prove that fact. Orr v. 

Currie, 14 Misc. 74, 35 N. Y. S. 198. 

Sec. 232 authorizing an order for service of summons by publication where it appears by affidavit that plaintiff has been or will be unable with due diligence to make personal service, requires that the judge, and not the affiant, shall be satisfied that the defendant is a nonresident and that personal service cannot be made. 

Evans v. Weinstein, 124 App. Div. 316, 108 N. Y. S. 

753, aff'd. 195 N. Y. 549, 88 N. E. 1119. 

Personal service of a summons and complaint upon a director and managing agent of a foreign corporation without the state is sufficient, there being no express statutory provision governing the mode of service in such a case. McCoy v. Erie Forge & Steel Co., 118 

Misc. 851, 194 N. Y. S. 695. 

A personal service of summons and complaint on defendant outside of the state, without service of a copy of the order for publication is insufficient. Ludden v. 

Degener, 14 App. Div. 397, 43 N. Y. S. 908. 

§175. Time of Publication. 

The first publication of the summons and notice in each designated newspaper must be made within three months after the order is granted. Service is complete on the fortysecond day after the first publication. (Rules 51, 52.) $176. Filing Papers.

The summons, complaint, notice, order and the papers on which the order was made must be filed with the clerk on or before the first day of publication. (Rule 52.) $177. Proof.

Proof of publication of a summons in a newspaper must be made by the affidavit of the printer or publisher or his

foreman or principal clerk. Proof of deposit of the summons in a post-office or post-office box must be made by the affidavit of the person who deposited it. (Rule 53.) 

§178. Service without the State in Lieu of Publication. 

In all cases where publication is ordered, service of the summons, complaint and notice required (see Rule 52) may be made personally without the state. Such service is equivalent to publication and mailing. The summons, complaint, order and papers on which the order was made must be filed before service is made. Service must be made within three months after the order is granted and is deemed complete 

10 days after proof thereof is filed. (C. P. A. §233; Rules 

51, 52.)

In real property actions under §232, subd. 6 and in matrimonial actions; or, where it appears by affidavit filed as a part of the judgment-roll in such action, that a warrant of attachment, granted in the action has been levied upon property of the defendant in the state, the summons, complaint and notice may be served outside the state without an order. 

Service is complete 10 days after proof thereof is filed. (C. 

P. A. §235; Rules 51, 52.) Summons in an action for specific performance of contract to convey real property may be served outside of the state under C. P. A. §§232 and 235. 

(Garfein v. McInnis, 248 N. Y. 261, 162 N. E. 73.) $179. The Process Server.

Whether such service is made with or without an order, it may be made by the following officers only: a resident or citizen of New York State, a sheriff, under sheriff, or deputy or constable of the county or other political subdivision in which service is made, an officer authorized by New York law to take acknowledgments of deeds, an attorney of the state where service is made, or a United States marshal. 

(C. P. A. §§233; 235.)

$180. Proof.

Proof of service without the state must be made by the affidavit of the person making the service and must show his authority. (Rule 53, subd. 6.) 

§181. Vacating Service. 

When service of the summons is absolutely void, any judgment taken in the action is also void and it is unnecessary for the defendant so served to raise objection until an attempt is made to enforce judgment.15 However, what appears to be void service may be only irregular service, which may become good service unless timely objection is raised. The best practice is to appear specially and move to vacate the service. (See Special Appearance.) 

§182. Appearance. 

If a defendant wishes to contest plaintiff's claim, he must appear generally in the action. If only the summons was served, he "appears"; if the summons and complaint were served, he "appears" and "answers." 16 The defendant's general appearance must be made by serving on the plaintiff's attorney a notice of appearance, a copy of an answer or a notice of motion raising an objection to the complaint in point of law."7 A voluntary general appearance is equivalent to personal service of the summons. 

(C. P. A. §237.) 

"Appearance" means the voluntary submission to the jurisdiction in whatever form manifested. One who confesses a judgment appears by his voluntary consent to the entry of judgment. People v. Cowan, 146 N. Y. 

348, 41 N. E. 26. "Defendant, a foreign insurance company, took the proceedings to the Federal Court, and subsequently the 

15 See pp. 259, 260. 

10 See p. 128. 

17 See Rothenberg v. Metzger, p. 123.

action was remanded to the state court. Thereafter the defendant moved to set aside the service of the summons and complaint on the ground that there had been no legal service thereof, and that an admission of service signed by the superintendent of insurance as its attorney did not comply with the provision of Rule 53. 

Held, that the motion was properly denied; that defendant necessarily submitted itself to the jurisdiction of the state court by the proceedings for removal, as there could be no transfer unless there was an action pending; and that the legal effect of this acknowledgment of jurisdiction was not avoided by the declaration of defendant's attorney in appearing in the proceeding that his appearance was special and only for the purpose of removal." Farmer v. The National Life Ass'n., 138 N. Y. 265, 33 N. E. 1075.

Where defendant comes into court and opposes an injunction on its merits, he submits himself to the jurisdiction of the court, although having made no "appearance." Metal Shelter Co. v. Fosdick, 172 N. Y. S. 273, aff'd. 188 App. Div. 916, 175 N. Y. S. 912.

A judge of the court of general sessions is not an officer within the meaning of §256 of the Greater N. Y.

Charter, which provides for the appearance by the corporation counsel on behalf of city officers. But where the corporation counsel did appear on request of such defendant judge, the appearance is not entirely void, since the corporation counsel may appear as an attorney for a defendant other than the city. The court will not vacate such appearance but will direct the defendant to substitute another attorney. Ledwith v. Rosalsky, 244 N. Y. 406, 155 N. E. 688.

Announcing in court that the appearance is special combined with conduct indicating the contrary, such as participating in the merits, has the effect of a general appearance. Henderson v. Henderson, 247 N. Y. 428, 160 N. E. 775. 

A general appearance by an attorney cannot be withdrawn if it was authorized when made. Equitable 

Trust Co. v. Halim, 133 Misc. 678, 234 N. Y. S. 37.

If at the commencement of the action the defendant was deemed to be a corporation, which in fact was a

partnership, the answer of the alleged corporation did not amount to an appearance by the partnership. Gray v. Vought & Co., 216 App. Div. 230, 214 N. Y. S. 765. 

A general appearance is not effected by: (1) A motion to make the complaint more definite and certain. (Valentine v. Myers' Sanitary Depot, 36 Hun 201; 

225 Fifth Ave. Corp. v. Freeman, 120 Misc. 472, 199 N. Y. 

S. 519.) (2) The signing by an attorney of a stipulation accepting an extension of time to answer. (Paine Lumber Co. v. 

Galbraith, 38 App. Div. 68, 55 N. Y. S. 971; Couch v. Mulhane, 63 How. Pr. 79; Littauer v. Stern, 177 N. Y. 233, 69 

N. E. 538.) (3) A clerk of an attorney signing a stipulation prepared by the plaintiff's attorney, restoring a case to the calendar, where a partner had not been served. (Duimo v. 

Arbuckle, 166 App. Div. 86, 151 N. Y. S. 669.) (4) A motion to set aside an attachment. (Wood v. Furtick, 17 Misc. 561, 40 N. Y. S. 687; Zabriskie v. Second 

National Bank, 204 App. Div. 428, 198 N. Y. S. 482.) (5) An attorney indorsing a bond vacating an attachment. (Engels Express Co. v. Ferguson, 79 Misc. 40, 138 

N. Y. S. 1086.) (6) A motion to vacate an injunction. (Regelman v. 

South Shore Traction Co., 67 Misc. 590, 123 N. Y. S. 353.) (7) A motion to open a default. (Couch v. Mulhane, 63 

How. Pr. 79.) (8) A motion to vacate a judgment. (Noble v. Crandall, 49 Hun 474, 2 N. Y. S. 265.) (9) An appearance in a contempt proceeding. (Robinson v. Robinson, 123 Misc. 80, 204 N. Y. S. 329; Weiss v. 

Weiss, 227 App. Div. 757, 237 N. Y. S. 56.)

§183. Special Appearance. 

A defendant may appear specially for the purpose of objecting to the jurisdiction of the court. (C. P. A. §237; 

Mun. Ct. Code §§78; 88.) 

"The special appearance is recognized only for the purpose of raising the question whether the court has obtained jurisdiction over the defendant personally or through his property." Muslusky v. Lehigh Valley Coal 

Co., 225 N. Y. 584, 587, 122 N. E. 461.

When a nonresident does not intend to submit himself to the jurisdiction of the court, he may either appear specially for the purpose of raising the question of the jurisdiction by motion, or he may allow plaintiff to take judgment by default. The question of jurisdiction will be available if he has not waived it by his own act. Reed v. Chilson, 142 N. Y. 152, 36 N. E. 884.

A motion to vacate the service of the summons is a special appearance. Bernstein v. Hakim, 126 Misc. 582, 214 N. Y. S. 82.

While a defendant is given the right to make a motion to dismiss a complaint for failure to serve the same, the remedy was intended and should be given only in cases where the defendant has entered a general appearance, and not where defendant appeared specially for the purpose of obtaining a copy of the complaint. Muslusky v. Lehigh Valley Coal Co., 225 N. Y. 584, 122 N. 

E. 461. §184. Time within Which to Appear.

In supreme court and county court actions, the defendant must appear within 20 days after service of the summons, exclusive of the day of service. In city court actions the time is 6 days, except when service is made by publication or without the state, when the time is 10 days, exclusive of the day of service. In municipal court actions, the time is 5 days, exclusive of the day of service, unless the return day falls on Saturday, when the defendant may answer on the next business day. (C. P. A. 

§237; City Ct. Act §49; Mun. Ct. Code §§19, 20; 78.) When

service is made by publication, the time begins to run the forty-second day after the first publication. Service without the state in lieu of publication is complete 10 days after proof is filed. (Rule 51; C. P. A. §235.)

An order extending the time of a defendant to answer for the period of twenty days extends defendant's time to appear in the action for that period. Littauer v. Stern, 177 N. Y. 233, 69 N. E. 538.

A motion to make the complaint more definite and certain does not extend defendant's time to answer or appear. 225 Fifth Ave. Corp. v. Freeman, 120 Misc. 472, 199 N. Y. S. 519.

CHAPTER VI PLEADINGS §185. Nature of Pleadings. 

The "pleadings" are the statements regarding the controversy made by the parties before trial. The plaintiff sets forth his claim in a "complaint." The defendant defends himself against this claim and sets forth counterclaims against the plaintiff in an “answer." The plaintiff answers a counterclaim, and sometimes the affirmative defenses in a "reply." 

§186. Written and Oral Pleadings. 

In the supreme and city courts the pleadings are written. 

They must be served on the adversary and may be filed with the clerk of the court. In the municipal court, the pleadings may be written or "oral." If written, the pleadings in a municipal court action are likewise served on the adversary or his attorney, and must be filed. If the complaint is oral, the clerk writes the nature and substance of the action on the summons. By way of an oral answer, the defendant states his defense and counterclaim to the clerk, who endorses them on the summons and notifies the plaintiff.

When the complaint is written, the answer must be written; except where the claim is for less than $100 and the defendant appears in person, in which case the defendant may answer orally. (Mun. Ct. Code §78.) 

$187. Pleadings Should Contain Facts. 

Each pleading must state plainly and concisely the material facts upon which the party relies, without unnecessary repetition, and must not contain the evidence by which such facts are to be proved. It is not proper to plead mere 105

conclusions; the facts must be pleaded. (C. P. A. §241;

Mun. Ct. Code §§82, 83.) 

A pleader may not aver a legal conclusion as an equivalent for the group of separate facts from which it is inferred. The allegations should be such and so stated, as to permit a distinct traverse and evolve a definite issue. Cook v. Warren, 88 N. Y. 37.

Plaintiff should state facts which constitute the cause of action and not the evidential facts from which the trier of the facts is authorized to infer or deduce a liability. DeCordova v. Sanville, 214 N. Y. 662, 108

N. E. 1088. 

A complaint in an action to recover damages for the loss of goods alleged to have been covered by a policy of insurance issued by the defendant, which fails to set out either the policy itself or the material provisions thereof, although alleging that the policy itself is in defendant's possession, was held not to state the facts upon which the party pleading relies. Bandler v. Globe, etc., Ins. Co., 205 App. Div. 515, 199 N. Y. S. 707.

A complaint which in substance merely alleges illegality or fraudulent conduct on the part of the defendant is insufficient. It is necessary to set out facts showing the illegality or fraudulent character of the claim. 

Daly v. Haight, 163 App. Div. 239, 148 N. Y. S. 46.

In pleading fraud, the plaintiff must set forth the facts and intentions to enable the court to decide whether or not they were fraudulent. Plaintiff's characterization of them is insufficient. Knowles v. City of 

N. Y., 176 N. Y. 430, 68 N. E. 860.

In an action based on fraud, sufficient facts must be alleged to set forth the claim. Allegations of fraud without facts are mere conclusions. Swift v. Chester 

Carbon Co., Inc., 223 App. Div. 808, 228 N. Y. S. 475.

When matter which is well pleaded is so commingled with matter that is badly pleaded that redrafting of the answer is necessary, the whole will be stricken out with leave to plead over. Walsh v. Judge, 223 App. 

Div. 423, 228 N. Y. S. 497; Burnham v. Hornaday, 223 

App. Div. 218, 228 N. Y. S. 246; Merchants' National 

Bank v. R. Prescott & Son, Inc., 223 App. Div. 194, 228 

N. Y. S. 483.

PLEADINGS 

107 

§188. The Complaint. 

1 The complaint, which is usually served with the summons, is the first pleading in an action. If the complaint is not served with the summons, the defendant must demand a copy of the complaint before or at the time he serves his notice of appearance. Otherwise he is entitled to only notice of the proceedings. The complaint must be served within 20 days after demand, if the action is brought in the supreme or county court, and within 6 days in a city court action. If the plaintiff does not comply, his action may be dismissed on motion. (C. P. A. §§254; 257; 238; City Ct. 

Act $50.) 

$189. The Contents of a Complaint. 

The complaint must contain: 

(1) The title of the action. 

(2) The name of the court. (3) In supreme and city court actions, the name of the county. (4) In municipal court actions, the borough and the number of the district. (5) A statement of each cause of action. (6) A demand for the judgment to which the plaintiff supposes himself entitled. (7) The name of the plaintiff's attorney or the plaintiff's name, if he appears in person. (8) The address of such attorney or of the party appearing in person. (C. P. A. §255; Rules 11; 91; City Ct.

Act §65; Mun. Ct. Code §§15; 19.) 

The service of a complaint, subsequent to the service of the summons, stating a different place of trial from that stated in the summons, operates, particularly 

1 For examination before framing the complaint, see p. 183.

where the defendant's attorney admits service of such complaint and retains it without objection, to change the place of trial to the county named in the complaint. Tolhurst v. Howard, 94 App. Div. 439, 88 N. Y. 

S. 235. "A complaint states a cause of action if, under its allegations, evidence may be admitted which will prove a cause of action; and the plaintiff is entitled to all the facts that may be fairly implied from the allegations by reasonable and fair intendment." Rockefeller v. Kellas, 222 App. Div. 368, 370, 226 N. Y. S. 325, 328. See also 

Sage v. Culver, 147 N. Y. 241, 41 N. E. 513; Abbey v. 

Wheeler, 170 N. Y. 122, 62 N. E. 1074.

The relief prayed for, or to which the plaintiff may be entitled, is no part of his cause of action. Hahl v. 

Sugo, 169 N. Y. 109, 62 N. E. 135. "The court looks to substance and not to form. It determines from the allegations of the complaint and answer the issue to be tried. The form of the action, however it may be disguised by words, allegations or the prayer for judgment, will not be permitted to mislead the court or divert its attention from the main issue to be determined. The prayer for relief is not decisive and does not control the nature of the action." City of Syracuse v. Hogan, 234 

N. Y. 457, 461, 138 N. E. 406.

When the complaint states a cause of action at law, a dismissal is precluded even though the prayer for relief is equitable in character. The nature of the complaint is not determined by the prayer for relief. Bloom v. Gelb, 227 App. Div. 619, 235 N. Y. S. 730.

If the complaint states a sufficient cause of action in equity, the form of relief demanded is immaterial. 

The decree will be made to fit the proof. Dodds v. Mc- 

Colgan, 134 Misc. 518, 235 N. Y. S. 492; Thompson v. 

Thompson, 134 Misc. 614, 235 N. Y. S. 617; Lightfoot v. Davis, 198 N. Y. 261, 91 N. E. 582. 

Under a general allegation in the complaint that damages were sustained because of the personal injury set forth, the plaintiff may recover all damages which necessarily, usually and immediately result from the alleged injury. Other damages are special and must be alleged. Kurak v. Traiche, 226 N. Y. 266, 123 N. E. 377; 

Keefe v. Lee, 197 N. Y. 68, 90 N. E. 344.

PLEADINGS 

109 In an action to recover damages for wrongful discharge before the expiration of the employment contract, it is unnecessary for the plaintiff to plead and prove that the discharge was without cause. The employer must plead and prove facts in justification of the dismissal. He is not permitted to prove such facts under a denial, but must allege them in his answer. Linton v. 

Unexcelled Fire-Works Co., 124 N. Y. 533, 27 N. E. 406. "For a valuable consideration" is sufficient allegation of consideration in an action on a non-negotiable draft. California Packing Corp. v. Kelly S. & D. Co., 228 N. Y. 49, 126 N. E. 269.

It is not necessary to allege lack of contributory negligence in the complaint. That allegation is involved in the usual statement that the injury was caused by the defendant's negligence, and in proving the statement the plaintiff must show that his own negligence did not contribute to the injury. Lee v. Troy Citizens' Gas- 

Light Co., 98 N. Y. 115; Klein v. Burleson, 138 App. 

Div. 405, 122 N. Y. S. 752; Bogardus v. Metropolitan 

Street R. Co., 62 App. Div. 376, 70 N. Y. S. 1094.

A complaint which alleges delivery of goods and the loan of money without stating the agreed price or reasonable value of the goods or the amount of money advanced is fatally defective and should be dismissed on motion. Sparks v. Ducas, 123 App. Div. 507, 108 N. Y. 

S. 546. 

8190. Special Provisions. Accounts. 

There are certain special provisions affecting the form of the pleading in particular cases. 

If the action is brought on an account, it is not necessary to set forth the items. However, within 10 days after a written demand, a copy of the account must be delivered to the adverse party. (C. P. A. §246.) 

§191. Sale and Delivery of Goods or the Rendering of 

Services.

In an action involving the sale and delivery of goods, or rendering of work, labor or services, or the furnishing of

materials, the plaintiff may set forth and number the items of his claim and the reasonable value or agreed price of each in a schedule attached to and made a part of his verified complaint. In his verified answer the defendant must indicate the items which he disputes in respect of delivery, performance, reasonable value or agreed price. (C. P. A. 

§255-a.) $192. Performance of a Condition Precedent. 

Performance of a condition precedent in a contract is sufficiently pleaded by an allegation that the party duly performed all the conditions of the contract on his part. (Rule 92.)

If he is to take advantage of this provision, the pleader must, in relation to the performance of a condition precedent, allege that he "duly" performed. 

Clemens v. American Fire Ins. Co., 70 App. Div. 435, 75 N. Y. S. 484.

In an action to recover on a contract of employment, held that an allegation in the complaint of due performance of the "terms and obligations" of the contract was not synonymous with a plea of due performance of all the "conditions" of the contract, so as to entitle plaintiff to the benefit of this rule. Berger v. Urban Motion

Picture Industries, 206 App. Div. 379, 201 N. Y. S. 489. 

General Corporation Law §218 provides that a foreign corporation, other than a monied corporation, may not maintain an action on a contract made in this state unless it had a certificate of authority to do business in the state at the time the contract was made. Obtaining the certificate is a condition precedent and must be pleaded and proved by the plaintiff foreign corporation. 

If the allegation is not made in the complaint, the objection that the complaint does not state facts sufficient to constitute a cause of action may be raised at the trial and is not waived by failure to raise it on motion or answer. On the other hand, payment of the license tax under §181 of the Tax Law is a condition_subsequent and need not be pleaded in the complaint. Non-compli-

PLEADINGS 

111 ance is a defense to be pleaded in the answer. Wood & 

Selick v. Ball, 190 N. Y. 217, 83 N. E. 21.2 $193. Instrument for Payment of Money.

Where a cause of action is founded upon an instrument for the payment of money only, the party may set forth a copy of the instrument and state that a specified sum is due. (Rule 94.)

The simple requirements of Rule 94 do not apply where the liability of the party, by the terms of the instrument, was conditional and depended on outside facts. In such case the facts as to the performance of conditions precedent must be averred. Tooker v. Arnoux, 76 N. Y. 397. $194. Judgment or Order.

In pleading a judgment or other determination of a court or officer of special jurisdiction, it is sufficient to allege that it was duly given or made. It is not necessary to state the facts conferring jurisdiction. (Rule 95.)

The complaint stated that the judgment of the proceedings had before a justice's court "was entered in said action." Held, not equivalent to the words "duly given or made," as used in this section. To say that a judgment is entered, is merely to allege the single fact of the entry of the judgment, without including an averment that it was properly or lawfully done. Hunt v. 

Dutcher, 13 How. Prac. 538. 

§195. Libel and Slander. 

In libel and slander actions, the plaintiff may allege that defamatory matter was published or spoken concerning him without setting forth any extrinsic fact from which its application to him would appear. (Rule 96.)

If the words are not libelous per se, the complaint must state in what way they were damaging and must 

2 See p. 53.

also claim special damages. Burnham v. Hornaday, 130 

Misc. 207, 223 N. Y. S. 750. 

§196. Slander of a Woman. 

In an action brought by a woman for words imputing unchastity to her, she need not allege nor prove special damages. (Rule 97.) 

$197. Real Property Actions. 

In an action to recover title or possession of real property, the complaint must describe the property with reasonable certainty. (Rule 240.) The particulars which must be stated in the complaint when the people of the state of New York are a party defendant are given in §259 of the Civil 

Practice Act. 

In a real property action, the complaint must show title or right to possession as the case may be, but it is neither necessary nor permissible to set forth the entire chain of title. Crook v. Licourt, 216 App. Div. 

237, 214 N. Y. S. 774. 

§198. Statutes. 

A private statute is pleaded sufficiently if its chapter number, year of passage and title are designated; or if it is designated by some other definite description. (Rule 98.) 

If a party wishes to prove a foreign statute on the trial, he must plead it. "The laws of the country to whose courts a party appeals for redress, furnish, in all cases, prima facie, the rule of decision; and, if either party wishes the benefit of a different rule of law, he must aver and prove it." Monroe v. Douglas, 5 N. Y. 447, 451.

The laws of another state or nation must be pleaded, or evidence thereof will be excluded. Lemyé v. Sirker, 130 Misc. 605, 224 N. Y. S. 130.

If a party claims that the statute of another state governs his cause of action or defense, he must plead

PLEADINGS 

113 the foreign statute. Stricks v. Siegel, 135 Misc. 608, 238 N. Y. S. 154. $199. Pleading by or against a Corporation. 

The complaint must state that the plaintiff or defendant is a domestic corporation, if such is the case. If either is a foreign corporation, the complaint must so state and set forth the state, country or government by or under whose laws it was created. Upon the trial, it is unnecessary for the plaintiff to prove the existence of the corporation unless the verified answer specifically alleges its non-existence. A mistake in the corporate name is waived unless the misnomer is pleaded. (Rule 93.) 

§200. In an Infant's Case. 

The complaint in an infant plaintiff's case must allege the time, the place and power of the appointment of the guardian ad litem. 

§201. Election of Remedies. 

In some cases the plaintiff has a choice between several remedies for one wrong suffered. If the various possible remedies are inconsistent, he must choose between them and, once his choice has been made, it is final. 

If, with full knowledge of the facts, plaintiff brings an action for the purchase price of property against a particular defendant, he cannot bring a subsequent action for conversion of the same property against another defendant. When election between two inconsistent rights and remedies has been made, it is final. Terry v. 

Munger, 121 N. Y. 161, 24 N. E. 272.

Plaintiff rescinded a purchase of stock on the ground of fraud and misrepresentation, tendered back the stock and sued to recover the purchase price. Thereafter he retained monies paid him as dividends on the stock without disclosing the fact that he applied such monies on account of the purchase price. Retention of the dividends constituted a repudiation of the rescission and

the complaint may be dismissed. Brennan v. Nat. Equitable Investment Co., 247 N. Y. 486, 160 N. E. 381. "Where a party, knowing all the facts, elects to sue in rescission instead of for damages, he must pursue the course he has taken. Even then, if the remedy be insufficient or inadequate or useless, the rule has not barred the plaintiff from taking other timely methods to obtain his rights." Clark v. Kirby, 243 N. Y. 295, 303, 153 N. E. 79.

A previous action for conversion against one defendant does not preclude an action to replevin the same chattel against another defendant, unless a judgment in first action has been satisfied. As against joint tortfeasors, the plaintiff is not subject to the doctrine of election. Cramer v. Brownell, 166 App. Div. 456, 151 N. 

Y. S. 1001, aff'd. 222 N. Y. 705, 119 N. E. 1037.

Where a complaint attempts to state a cause of action based on a breach of warranty and another cause of action based on negligence, and it is apparent on the face thereof that the plaintiff has no cause of action on warranty, he will not be required to elect on motion before answer. Turner v. Edison Storage Battery Co., 248 N. Y. 73, 161 N. E. 423. 

Where the complaint sets forth a cause of action for services at an agreed price and in another count sets forth an action for the reasonable value of the same services, the plaintiff should not be required to elect between the two counts before trial, since it is permissible to sue on a special contract for services and, if that is not proved, to recover on quantum meruit. 

Rubin v. Cohen, 129 App. Div. 395, 113 N. Y. S. 843.

It is proper to plead in the alternative under §§211 and 213 of the Civil Practice Act, and judgment may be granted against one or more defendants found liable. If the causes of action are inconsistent, the most that can be required of the plaintiff is an election upon which he would proceed at the trial. Reeve v. Cromwell, 227 App. 

Div. 32, 237 N. Y. S. 20.

The plaintiff cannot be compelled, before trial, to elect between the theory of negligence and the theory of nuisance. Smith v. Earle, 202 App. Div. 305, 195 N. 

Y. S. 342; Schoenfeld v. Mott Avenue Realty Co., 168 

App. Div. 91, 153 N. Y. S. 745.

PLEADINGS 

115 

§202. Uniting Causes of Action. 

Two or more causes of action may be united in one complaint, if all causes appear upon the face of the complaint: (a) to be consistent with each other; (b) to require the same place of trial; and (c) all causes belong to one of the following subdivisions: (1) Upon a contract, express or implied. (2) For personal injuries, except libel, slander, criminal conversation or seduction. (3) For libel or slander. (4) For injuries to real property. (5) For ejectment, with or without damages for withholding the property. (6) For injuries to personal property. (7) Replevin, with or without damages. (8) Against a trustee upon a contract or by operation of law. (9) Upon claims arising out of the same transaction, or transactions connected with the same subject of action, whether or not they are included within one or more of the other subdivisions of this section. (10) For penalties incurred under the conservation law. (11) For penalties incurred under the agricultural law. (12) For penalties incurred under the public health law. 

It is possible to join a legal and an equitable cause of action in the same complaint. But all the causes of action so joined must belong to one of the foregoing subdivisions; except under subd. 9, where it is possible to join a cause of action on contract with one in tort or two or more causes in tort, each of which comes under a different subdivision, if all arise out of the same transaction or are connected with the same subject of action. (C. P. A. §258.) If causes of action have been improperly united, the defendant may

make a motion to correct the complaint. The motion should be made within 20 days after service of the complaint. An extension of time will not be granted except on 2 days' notice to the plaintiff. (Rules 102; 105.)

The test for determining when more than one cause of action is stated in a complaint: Where a recovery upon one cause of action would not bar a subsequent recovery upon the others, and the evidence to establish each would be different from the evidence required to establish the others, separate causes of action are stated. Raftery v. Carter, 162 App. Div. 17, 147 N. Y. 

S. 271.

The fact that two causes of action stated in a complaint arose out of the same transaction is not sufficient against a motion for misjoinder unless it also appears on the face of the complaint that they do not require different places of trial. Jacobus v. Colgate, 217 N. Y. 

235, 111 N. E. 1075.

Two causes of action arising at the same time do not, as a matter of law, arise out of the same transaction. Unity of time is not a "transaction." Slander and false imprisonment may not be joined in the same complaint even though the two causes of action arose at the same time. De Wolfe v. Abraham, 151 N. Y. 186, 45 N. E. 455.

A cause of action for fraud inducing the making of a contract may be united in the same complaint with causes of action for breach of such contract. France 

& Canada S. S. Corp. v. Berwind-White Coal Mining 

Co., 229 N. Y. 89, 127 N. E. 893.

Injuries to the person and to the property of the plaintiff arising out of a single negligent act on the part of the defendant, may be joined in one complaint since they arise out of the same transaction. McInerney v. Main, 82 App. Div. 543, 81 N. Y. S. 539. 

Where the facts stated in the complaint in an action against several physicians for malpractice were so related that in the event of separate trials they would necessarily have to be proved on each trial, and where the substantive law applicable to each cause of action was the same, held that the parties and causes of action

PLEADINGS 

117 were properly joined in one action and one complaint. 

Sherlock v. Manwaren, 208 App. Div. 538, 203 N. Y. 

S. 709. But a plaintiff may not join two defendants who are alleged to have been responsible for the death of a child, one for maintaining an attractive nuisance on which the child was injured and the other a physician for negligently treating the child for such injuries. 

Ader v. Blau, 241 N. Y. 7, 148 N. E. 771.

A cause of action for goods sold and delivered and one on the guaranty of payment may be joined in one complaint, but they should be stated separately and numbered. Winter v. Maple City Mfg. Co., 132 Misc. 

631, 230 N. Y. S. 458.

Negligence and nuisance are alternative remedies and may be joined in one complaint if they are stated separately and numbered. Smith v. Earle, 202 App. 

Div. 305, 195 N. Y. S. 342. 

An action for an injunction restraining the unlawful maintenance and operation of a railroad in a street in front of plaintiff's premises and for special damages to real property occasioned by the nuisance may be united with an action for a personal injury suffered on a particular occasion from the same wrongful appropriation and use of the highway. While the injuries are distinct in character, they arise out of the same "transaction" within the meaning of subdivision nine. Laming v. Galusha, 135 N. Y. 239, 31 N. E. 1024.

An action for assault and an action for malicious prosecution are not inconsistent with each other. If the defendant in each is the same, they may be joined in a single complaint under $258. Schecher v. Wittner, 130 Misc. 424, 224 N. Y. S. 66.

An action on a contract against an attorney for negligently conducting a case and an action under the penal law, cannot be joined in the same complaint. Barkley v. Williams, 30 Misc. 607, 64 N. Y. S. 318.

An action for the conversion of an old automobile and an action based on a promise to pay the value thereof are inconsistent and cannot be joined. Drexel v. Hollander, 112 App. Div. 25, 98 N. Y. S. 104. 

A complaint, which alleges a cause of action in tort for selling an unsafe gun and one for breach of war-

ranty as to quality of the gun, misjoins causes of action, even though they are not separately stated and numbered. Reed v. Livermore, 101 App. Div. 254, 91 

N. Y. S. 986.

A complaint alleging that the plaintiff was assaulted by the defendant's servants, who spoke falsely concerning plaintiff, is bad. Causes of action for assault and slander cannot be united in one complaint because they are not actions arising out of the same transaction. 

Paul v. Ford, 117 App. Div. 151, 91 N. Y. S. 986.

A complaint against a bank may not contain a cause of action to recover a balance of various deposits made at different times, a cause of action for conversion of a check, and a cause of action for the negligence of an unfit vice-president in converting the check. Salter v. 

Bronx Nat. Bank, 150 App. Div. 639, 135 N. Y. S. 702.

A complaint which states the two inconsistent actions, rescission and damages for the vendor's misrepresentation, may be amended so as to set forth a cause of action in equity to have the court decree a rescission. 

In the action on the amended complaint all incidental rights arising from the rescission may be determined. 

Castiglia v. Lucas, 132 Misc. 480, 230 N. Y. S. 116. See also Houstan Mercantile Co. v. Powell and King, 72 

Misc. 358, 130 N. Y. S. 274. 

§203. Stating Separately and Numbering. 

Each separate cause of action must be stated separately and numbered, and divided into paragraphs numbered consecutively. As far as possible, each paragraph should contain but a single allegation, but an allegation contained in one paragraph of one cause of action may be incorporated as a whole in another cause of action by reference. (Rule 90.)

In an action for libel, a complaint alleging publication of the libel to an agent and employee of plaintiff and to other persons, stated only one cause of action; and not separate causes of action, which plaintiff could be required to separately state and number. Fried, Mendelson & Co. v. Halstead, 203 App. Div. 113, 196 

N. Y. S. 285.

PLEADINGS 

119 When it was alleged in a complaint that defendant had converted 50 boxes of gloves pledged to him by a third party, and two plaintiffs each claimed 7 boxes and a third plaintiff claimed the remaining 36 boxes, the causes of action for conversion might be joined, but they should have been stated separately and numbered. 

Fleitman & Co. v. Colonial Finance Corp., 203 App. Div. 

827, 197 N. Y. S. 125. 

A complaint which alleges breach of contract and payment of money for work done under the contract, and which seeks to recover both the money paid and damages, states two causes of action, one for breach of contract, the other for money had and received. A motion to compel plaintiff to state separately and number the causes of action under these facts will be granted. 

Astoria Silk Works v. Plymouth Rubber Co., 126 App. 

Div. 18, 110 N. Y. S. 175. 

A complaint sought to recover a sum of money claimed to be due under a contract for sale and delivery to defendant of cotton yarn, shipments to be made monthly, and also a sum of money for sale and delivery to defendant of yarn. The court held that the complaint set up two distinct causes of action, first, for the purchase price of the goods sold and delivered, for which defendant had failed to pay as provided; and, second, for anticipatory breach of agreement as to the remaining goods still undelivered by plaintiffs. The defendant's motion to compel plaintiff to state separately and number such causes of action should be granted. Sampson v. Frank F. Pels Co., 195 App. Div. 487, 186 N. Y.

S. 815. 

If plaintiff wishes to join a cause of action for nuisance and one for negligence in the same complaint, he must state separately the facts constituting each cause of action and number each. Smith v. Earle, 202 App. 

Div. 305, 195 N. Y. S. 342.

A corporation and the two organizers thereof sued for damages for a libel as one cause of action. Since it is only when the defamatory words were published of partners in the way of their business that two persons may join in a single action for the same words, the causes of action should have been stated separately and numbered. Peacock v. Tata Sons, 206 App. Div. 145, 200 

N. Y. S. 656.

A complaint which demands an injunction against two railroads to restrain them from continuing acts of nuisance consisting of noises, smoke and soot from the operation of their engines states a single action in equity and the plaintiff should not be compelled to state separately and number the cause of action against each defendant. Schenectady Holding Co., Inc., v. N. Y. 

Central R. Co., 225 App. Div. 479, 233 N. Y. S. 495. See also Warren v. Parkhurst, 186 N. Y. 45, 75 N. E. 579.

There is no time limit within which a motion to compel the plaintiff to state separately and number his causes of action under Rule 90 must be made. However, it should be made before answer, since the purpose of this motion is to enable the defendant to answer or to make a motion for judgment separately on the various causes of action in the complaint. Brown-Duffy Goatskin Corp. v. Henkel, 211 App. Div. 342, 207 N. Y. S. 357. $204. In the Municipal Court.

In the municipal court the plaintiff may include in one complaint all the causes of action of which the court has jurisdiction; but the court may order that the causes so joined be tried or disposed of separately or that the actions be severed. (Mun. Ct. Code $79.) $205. Defects Waived.

Unless the defendant takes an objection by motion, he waives his right to object on the following grounds, if they appear on the face of the complaint: (1) That the court has not jurisdiction of the person of the defendant in a case where jurisdiction may be acquired by his consent. 3 (2) That the plaintiff has not legal capacity to sue. (3) That another cause of action is pending between the same parties for the same cause. (4) That there is a misjoinder of parties plaintiff. 

3 But see p. 259.

PLEADINGS 

121 (5) That there is a defect of parties, plaintiff or defendant. (6) That causes of action have been improperly united. 

(C. P. A. §278.) 

Dilatory pleas under Rules 106 and 109 must be taken before trial or they are waived under $278. Dilatory pleas under Rules 107 and 110 may be taken before trial but are not waived if not so taken. Where the defect of another action pending did not appear on the face of the complaint, the objection could be taken in the answer and raised on the trial. Gentilala v. Fay 

Taxicabs, 214 App. Div. 255, 212 N. Y. S. 101, ruling approved 243 N. Y. 397, 153 N. E. 848.* $206. Defects Not Waived. 

The following objections are not waived if not taken before trial: (1) To the jurisdiction of the court, except as specified under §278. (2) That the complaint, or a separate statement therein of a cause of action, does not state facts sufficient to consti tute a cause of action. (C. P. A. §279.) 

If the defendant takes an objection under §278 and §279, he must specify in his notice of motion the particular defect upon which he relies. The following objections may be stated without particulars: (1) That the complaint, or a separate statement therein of a cause of action, does not state facts sufficient to constitute a cause of action. (2) That the court has not jurisdiction of the subject of the action. (3) That the court has not jurisdiction of the person of the defendant. 

4 For Rule 106, see p. 123; Rule 109, p. 145; Rule 107, p. 124; 

Rule 110, p. 145.

(4) That another action is pending between the same parties for the same cause. (C. P. A. §280.) 

Since it would be impossible to obtain jurisdiction of the Government of Portugal, an objection to the jurisdiction of the court is not waived by the general appearance of the defendant, a department of such government. De Simone v. Transportes Maritimas Do Estado, 200 App. Div. 82, 191 N. Y. Supp. 864. 

§207. Objections to Part of the Complaint. 

The defendant may object on motion to a specified cause of action in the complaint and plead to another. If the defendant pleads to a complaint or to a separate cause of action to which a motion has been addressed, he abandons the motion. If his objections on motion are not sustained, the defendant may serve an answer, or an amended answer where he has pleaded to a separate cause of action not affected by the motion, as a matter of right, within 10 days after the service of notice of the entry of the order deciding the motion, unless the court stated in the order that the objections were frivolous. Upon the decision of a point of law, the court, in its discretion, may allow the party in fault to plead anew or amend, upon just terms. (C. P. A. §§281, 282, 283.)

Affidavits may not be used to prove the objections made under Rule 106. Deutsch v. Textile Waste Merchandising Co., 212 App. Div. 681, 209 N. Y. S. 388; 

Cole v. Levy, 212 App. Div. 84, 208 N. Y. S. 481; Bron son v. Board of Education of Binghamton, 136 Misc. 

76, 240 N. Y. S. 291. 

§208. Special Rules. 

Certain of the Rules of Civil Practice also pertain to much of the subject matter discussed under Sections 278 

and 279 of the Civil Practice Act.

PLEADINGS 

123 $209. Defects on the Face of the Complaint. 

The defendant has 20 days," after service of the complaint, in which to serve notice of motion for judgment dismissing the complaint, or one or more causes of action therein, where it appears on the face of the complaint: (1) That the court does not have jurisdiction of the person of the defendant. (2) That the court does not have jurisdiction of the subject of the action. sue. (3) That the plaintiff does not have legal capacity to (4) That there is another action pending between the same parties for the same cause. (5) That the complaint does not state facts sufficient to constitute a cause of action. (Rule 106.)

The defendant waives the objection that the plaintiff did not have legal capacity to sue by failing to make his motion within 20 days after service of the complaint. Rowlee v. Durfey, 227 App. Div. 759, 236 N. Y. 

S. 430.

The demurrer has been abolished, but the objections formerly taken by demurrer are preserved and taken in accordance with §§278-283 of the Civil Practice Act. 

Rothenberg v. Metzger, 227 App. Div. 444, 238 N. Y. S. 

139; 225 Fifth Ave. Corp. v. Freeman, 120 Misc. 472, 199 N. Y. S. 519. 

8210. Defects Not on the Face of the Complaint.

The defendant has 20 days, after service of the complaint, within which to serve a notice of motion for judgment dismissing the complaint,' or one or more causes of action therein, where the following defects do not appear on the face of the complaint: 

5 See Simon v. David, p. 157. • For effect of dismissal, see p. 218. 

7 For effect of dismissal, see p. 218.

(1) Lack of jurisdiction of the person of the defendant. (2) Lack of jurisdiction of the subject of the action. (3) Plaintiff's lack of legal capacity to sue. (4) Another action pending between the same parties for the same cause. (5) An existing final judgment or decree of a court of competent jurisdiction, rendered on the merits, which determined the same cause of action between the parties. (Res adjudicata.) (6) The time during which the action might have been brought has elapsed. (Statute of Limitations.) (7) The claim or demand in the complaint has been released. (8) The contract which is the basis of the action is unenforceable under the statute of frauds. (9) The cause of action did not accrue against the defendant because of his infancy or other disability. (Rule 107.) $211. Hearing and Disposition. 

A motion under Rule 107 must be made on the complaint and on affidavits which state facts tending to show that the defect or defects actually exist. The plaintiff may present affidavits denying the facts alleged by the defendant or setting forth facts tending to obviate the defendant's objections. The court may: (1) Grant the motion; and, in its discretion, allow the plaintiff to amend the complaint on just terms. (2) Direct that questions of fact, stated in the order, be tried by a jury or referee, and that the findings be reported to the court for its action. (3) Overrule the objection; and, in its discretion, allow the defendant to plead the same facts in the answer as a defense.

PLEADINGS 

125 If the objections were directed to some of the causes of action but not to all, judgment may be entered in accordance with the provisions for consolidation and severance or with the provisions for judgment after trial of issues and determination of a motion for judgment. (Rules 107, 108; 

195; C. P. A. §96.)

Affidavits may be used to establish the defects enumerated in Rule 107, but not to show the insufficiency of all or part of the complaint. Squier v. Houghton, 131 

Misc. 129, 226 N. Y. S. 162. 

§212. Motions to Correct the Complaint. 

The defendant may apply to the court for correction of the complaint by moving to compel the plaintiff: (1) To state separately and number his causes of action.Ⓡ (2) To make the complaint more definite and certain. (3) To correct a misjoinder of parties plaintiff. (4) To correct a defect of parties plaintiff or defendant. (5) To correct a complaint wherein causes of action have been improperly united." (6) To eliminate objectionable matter. (Rules 90; 102, 103.) 

§213. Motion to Make More Definite and Certain. 

The defendant may make a motion to correct the complaint, if any matter therein is so indefinite, uncertain or obscure that the precise meaning or application thereof is not apparent. The motion should be made within 20 days after the service of the complaint. No extension of time within which to make this motion will be granted, unless application therefor is made on 2 days' notice to the plain- 

8 See p. 118. 

⁹ See p. 115.

tiff. The court may require the plaintiff to serve an amended complaint. (Rules 102; 105.)

A complaint which alleges that the defendant guaranteed a certain indebtedness by various agreements in writing without alleging the substance of any of them, is defective because it states only legal conclusions as to the effect of "various agreements," and a motion to make it more definite and certain by setting forth in full or by annexing the alleged agreements, should be granted. Should the attached agreements be ambiguous and if the complaint does not state any definite construction thereof, the court will hold that the complaint is so indefinite and uncertain that the defendant cannot safely answer it. U. S. Printing & 

Lithograph Co. v. Powers, 183 App. Div. 513, 170 N. Y. 

S. 314.

A motion to make more definite and certain should be denied if the meaning of the pleading is clear, although additional facts might be helpful to the defendant to prepare for trial but not necessary for him to plead. Smythe v. Cleary, 127 App. Div. 555, 111 

N. Y. S. 872. $214. Misjoinder or Defects of Parties.10

The time provisions in the last section also apply to a motion to correct a complaint because of: (1) A misjoinder of parties plaintiff. (2) A defect of parties plaintiff or defendant.

An amended complaint may be ordered by the court. (Rules 102; 105.)

Affidavits may be used on these motions if the defects do not appear on the face of the complaint. $215. Motion to Strike Out. 

Within 20 days after service of the complaint, or upon an extension granted on 2 days' notice, the defendant may 

10 See Chapter IV.

PLEADINGS 

127 move the court to strike out any matter in the complaint which is sham, frivolous, irrelevant, redundant, repetitious, unnecessary, impertinent or scandalous, or which may tend to prejudice, embarrass or delay the fair trial of the action. If the court order the matter to be stricken out, the pleading may be deemed amended accordingly, or the court may order the plaintiff to serve an amended complaint omitting the objectionable matter. (Rules 103; 105.)

In a complaint for an action for breach of contract of guaranty, an allegation which at most tended only to show a motive for defendant's giving the guaranty, which was immaterial, held subject to be stricken on motion. Murphy v. National City Bank, 203 App. Div. 

571, 196 N. Y. S. 859. 

Where a pleading contains irrelevant and redundant matter, mingled with admissions, denials, and defenses, so mixed up that it is impossible to separate them and strike out the bad without completely redrafting the pleading, it is not the court's duty to attempt to eliminate the parts that are bad, and all parts so mingled should be stricken out entirely. International Ry. Co. v. Jaggard, 204 App. Div. 67, 197 N. Y. S. 384. "It is unnecessary and improper to repeat or incorporate in an affirmative defense a denial made in another part of the answer, and no motion is necessary to strike out such denials where the defense is attacked under Rule 103." 276 Spring Street Corporation v. 

Forbes, 134 Misc. 537, 540, 235 N. Y. S. 257, 261. See also Blackwell v. Columbia Trust Co., 199 App. Div. 

759, 192 N. Y. S. 226; Clinckett v. Casseres, 205 App. 

Div. 710, 200 N. Y. S. 178.

The constitutionality of a statute cannot be determined before trial. A motion to strike out a defense which pleads the unconstitutionality of a statute will be denied. People v. Sterling, 132 Misc. 769, 230 N. Y.

Affidavits may not be used to prove the defects on a motion under Rule 103. H. C. King Motor Sales Corp. v. 

Allen, 209 App. Div. 281, 204 N. Y. S. 555.

§216. Motions in the Municipal Court. 

In general, the motions which may be addressed to the pleadings in a municipal court action and the procedure thereon are the same as in a supreme court action. (Mun. 

Ct. Code §15.) On or before the last day for answering, the defendant may raise a question not involving the merits of the action by filing with the clerk a notice of special appearance stating the purpose for which he appears. The objections which may be taken by special appearance are: (1) That the court does not have jurisdiction of the defendant. (2) That the court has no jurisdiction of the subject of the action. (Mun. Ct. Code §§78; 88.) 

(3) That the summons fails to contain a statement of a cause of action.

All other objections may be taken by motion. The notice of motion must state the particular grounds and the defects or objections upon which the party relies. An objection to the sufficiency of the complaint or any objection which would terminate the action without putting the plaintiff to proof if the objection were sustained, may be set forth and particularized in the answer as a defense, in lieu of a motion. On application of either party, such objection in the answer must be disposed of before trial. 

If defendant's motion is overruled, the court must allow him to answer, but may impose costs. If an objection that a pleading is insufficient, taken on motion or answer, is sustained, the court must allow an amendment, but may impose costs. On failure to amend, the pleading or part thereof may be stricken out. (Mun. Ct. Code §§89, 90.) $217. The Answer.

The defendant sets forth his side of the controversy in an answer, which is the only pleading on his part. (C. P. A. $260.)

PLEADINGS 

129 

§218. Time to Answer. 

In the supreme court, the defendant has 20 days after service of the complaint in which to serve his answer.

If the attorney for one defendant later appears for another defendant, he has 20 days after such appearance in which to answer for the second defendant. Except in a case where an order of arrest can be granted only by the court, a defendant arrested before answering in a supreme court action may answer within 20 days after the arrest. If the defendant has made certain motions¹¹ addressed to the complaint on grounds that are not frivolous, he may delay answering until 10 days after service of the order entered on the motion. An application for an extension of time to answer will not be granted except on an affidavit of merits. When the action is brought on a promissory note or other written evidence of debt for the unconditional payment of money, no extension of time to answer will be granted unless 2 days' notice is given the plaintiff. When the time has once been extended by stipulation or order, an application for a further extension must be made on affidavits and 2 days' notice to the plaintiff. Copies of the affidavits used on an application must always be served with the extension order. (C. P. A. §§263; 283; Rules 85-88.) A defendant who demands the determination of the ultimate rights of two or more defendants must serve a copy of his answer on each defendant to be affected by the determination at least 20 days before trial. (C. P. A. §264.) 

Sections 282 and 283 of the Civil Practice Act do not apply to motions under Rules 102 and 103. The making of such motion does not extend the time to answer, and such motion is not abandoned by service of the answer. 

Rothenberg v. Metzger, 227 App. Div. 444, 238 N. Y. S. 

139; Shipley v. Schnitzer, 224 App. Div. 730, 229 N. Y. S. 915. 

11 See p. 122.

A co-defendant brought into the action under subd.

2 of section 193 of the Civil Practice Act may plead only to the answer served upon him and not to the complaint. Travlos v. Commercial Union of America, 217 

App. Div. 352, 217 N. Y. S. 459. $219. In the City Court.

In the city court the defendant has 6 days after service of the complaint in which to answer; except where the summons was served by publication, when he has 10 days. 

A defendant arrested before answer has 10 days. (City Ct.

Act §§49, 50.) 

$220. In the Municipal Court.

In the municipal court the defendant may answer 5 days after service of the summons and complaint or summons with the cause of action endorsed thereon. When the last day falls on Saturday, he may answer on the next business day. If an objection taken to the complaint by motion is overruled, the court must allow the defendant to answer and may impose costs. On or before the last day to answer, the time to answer may be extended by stipulation of the parties or by order of the court obtained on good cause. (Mun. Ct. Code §§19; 78; 90.) The time to answer cannot be extended by an ex parte order for more than 3 days. 

An application for further extension may be made on notice. (Mun. Ct. Rule X.) A defendant who demands the determination of the ultimate rights of two or more defendants must serve a copy of his answer upon such defendant to be affected by the determination at least 2 days before trial or at such other time as the court directs. 

(Mun. Ct. Code §83.) 

$221. Contents of the Answer.12

As in the case of the complaint, the answer should contain the title to the action, the name of the court, the county 12 For procedure on oral answer in the municipal court, see p. 105.

PLEADINGS 

131 or borough and district, the name of the defendant's attorney or the name of the defendant if he appears in person and the address of the attorney or defendant. In addition the answer must contain: (1) A general or specific denial of each material allegation controverted by the defendant, or of any knowledge thereof sufficient to form a belief. (2) A statement of new matter constituting a defense or counterclaim. (C. P. A. §261; Mun. Ct. Code §83.) 

(3) A demand for judgment, if the defendant believes he is entitled to affirmative judgment upon a counterclaim interposed. (C. P. A. §270.) 

(4) A demand for a determination of the ultimate rights of two or more defendants, if such determination may be made in the action and the defendant requires it. (C. P. A. 

§264; Mun. Ct. Code §83.) 

An answer which uses words of the complaint so as to form a negative pregnant is improper. In his answer the defendant "Denies" and then repeats the words of the complaint. The statement is a denial pregnant with an admission of the truth of the allegation of the complaint and is called a negative pregnant. Baker v. Bailey, 16 Barb. 54. 

An allegation that defendant "has no information sufficient to form a belief" is bad. Steinback v. Diepenbrock, 52 App. Div. 437, 65 N. Y. S. 118. 

The phrase "defendant states that he denies" or "defendant says that he denies" in the answer is equivalent to "defendant denies." Kirschbaum v. Eschmann, 205 

N. Y. 127, 98 N. E. 328; Jones v. Ludlum, 74 N. Y. 61.

In an action on a bond a denial by a surety that he has "no knowledge or information sufficient to form a belief as to whether or not the principal in the bond was, at the time of the commencement of this action, indebted to the said plaintiff in the sum mentioned in the complaint, or in any other sum, and therefore he denies the same," is merely the denial of a legal con-

clusion. It puts in issue no fact alleged. Emery v. 

Baltz, 94 N. Y. 407. 

A statement in the answer that, "defendant denies upon information and belief the material allegations contained in the complaint" is insufficient. It raises no issue and there is no way of ascertaining what is the ground of the defense. N. Y. Coach & Auto Lamp 

Co. v. Brown, 82 Misc. 92, 143 N. Y. S. 100. 

Where defendant in his answer denies that he has any knowledge or information sufficient to form a belief as to the allegations of the complaint without addressing his denial to specific allegations, and where some of the allegations covered by such denial must have been within the knowledge of the defendant, some were presumptively within his knowledge and some might not be within his knowledge, the answer is bad.

However, the defendant should have been given an opportunity to perfect his pleading and a motion for judgment on the pleadings should be denied. Kirschbaum v. Eschman, 205 N. Y. 127, 98 N. E. 328.

By omitting to deny either generally or specifically the allegations of the complaint for goods sold and delivered, but answering by way of a counterclaim based on a warranty and allegations inconsistent with the complaint, the answer is not a denial and will not put the allegations of the complaint in issue. It must be dismissed. Smith v. Coe, 170 N. Y. 162, 63 N. E. 57. "The general denial in the answer puts in issue all matters which plaintiff is bound to prove, and nothing more." Norris v. McMechen, 134 Misc. 866, 868, 236 

N. Y. S. 486, 489. "Under a general denial the defendant may controvert by evidence anything which the plaintiff is bound to prove in the first instance to make out his cause of action, or anything that he is permitted to prove for the purpose under his complaint. Milbank v. Jones, 141 

N. Y. 340, 345, 36 N. E. 388. 

In an action on a note alleged to be under seal, the plaintiff must prove that the maker did in fact affix his seal. Therefore, under a general denial, the defendant may prove that the note did not contain his seal when issued. Farmers Loan & Trust Co. v. Siefke, 144 N. Y. 

354, 39 N. E. 358.

PLEADINGS 

133 An estoppel in pais, such as title in a person other than the plaintiff, may be proved under a general denial. 

Feinberg v. Allen, 208 N. Y. 215, 101 N. E. 893. 

If the answer in an action on a note denies execution, forgery may be proved under a general denial. 

Dougherty v. Salt, 227 N. Y. 200, 125 N. E. 94. 

§222. Admissions. 

Every material allegation in the complaint not controverted by the answer is taken as true for the purposes of the action. (C. P. A. §243.) §223. Several Defenses and Partial Defenses. 

The answer may set forth as many defenses, whether legal or equitable, as the defendant has. Unless the defenses are in answer to the entire complaint, they must distinctly refer to the cause of action which they do answer. A partial defense may be interposed, but it must be stated to be a partial defense to the entire complaint or to one or more causes of action therein. Matter tending to mitigate or reduce damages is a partial defense. (C. P. A. §262.)

The fact that particular matter might be proved under a general denial, does not preclude the defendant from also pleading it as a defense. Morgan Munitions 

Co. v. Studebaker Corp., 226 N. Y. 94, 123 N. E. 146; 

Staten Island Midland R. R. Co. v. Hinchliffe, 170 N. Y. 

473, 63 N. E. 545; Kelly v. National Bank of Whitehall, 190 App. Div. 760, 180 N. Y. S. 606.

Defenses either deny material allegations in the complaint or confess and avoid such allegations. Benedict v. Seymour, 6 How. Pr. 298; Staten Island M. R. R. Co. v. Hinchliffe, 170 N. Y. 473, 63 N. E. 545.

An equitable defense may be set up in an action at law. Anything which shows the plaintiff ought not to recover is available to the defendant. Susquehanna 

Steamship Co., Inc., v. Anderson & Co., Inc., 239 N. Y. 

285, 146 N. E. 381.

All defenses which admit the original cause of action, but are based on subsequent facts or transactions which tend to defeat the cause of action, must be pleaded and proved by the defendant. Farmers Loan & Trust 

Co. v. Siefke, 144 N. Y. 354, 39 N. E. 358.

Each defense must be complete in itself. It must contain every essential allegation and, unless other parts are specially incorporated by proper reference, reference cannot be had to them. Outcalt v. Bonheur, 120 App. 

Div. 168, 104 N. Y. S. 1099.

In an action for the recovery of commissions on an exchange of real estate the defendant may couple a denial of any employment of or indebtedness to the plaintiff with a plea that defendant was induced to enter into the contract by plaintiff's false representations. 

Wendling v. Pearce, 27 App. Div. 517, 50 N. Y. S. 509. 

That portion of an answer which mingles defenses and a denial should be stricken out. When the defenses which are good are so commingled with irrelevances and redundancies as to make it impossible to separate them without redrafting the entire pleading, all of the defenses should be stricken out, with leave to plead over. 

Burnham v. Hornaday, 223 App. Div. 218, 228 N. Y. S. 

246; Merchants Nat. Bank v. R. Prescott & Son, Inc., 223 App. Div. 194, 228 N. Y. S. 483. "Defendants may plead inconsistent defenses. Election of remedies applies to causes of action, not to defenses. One defense may set up a judgment as valid, and another may assert that it is null and void." Gilbert v. Burnstine, 135 Misc. 305, 310, 237 N. Y. S. 171, 179.

Where there are several distinct charges in a libel action, the "rolled-up" plea may be set up as a partial defense. "The purport of the plea is that all the facts stated in the alleged libel are true, that there remains in the libel over and above all statements of fact certain expressions of opinion, which standing alone would be libelous, but that these expressions of opinion when related to the facts proved were fair comment and that, therefore, the expression of opinion was as fully justified as the statement of fact." Foley v. Press Pub. Co., 226 App. Div. 535, 543, 235 N. Y. S. 340, 349. 

In an action for slander, circumstances in mitigation of damages must be set up in the answer in order that

PLEADINGS 

135 evidence thereof may be admissible. Willover v. Hill, 72 N. Y. 36. 

$224. Particular Defenses. 

The defendant must raise by his answer all matters which show: (a) that the action cannot be maintained; or, (b) that the transaction is void or voidable in point of law; and (c) all grounds of defense which if not raised would be likely to take the adverse party by surprise or would raise issues of fact not arising out of the preceding pleadings. Examples of matters which should be pleaded under (c) are: (1) Fraud. (2) Statute of limitations. 

(3) Release. 

(4) Payment. (5) Facts showing illegality either by statute, common law, or statute of frauds. (C. P. A. §§242; 30.)

In an action to recover damages for wrongful death, the defendant must plead and prove contributory negligence on the part of the deceased. (C. P. A. §265; Decedent Es tate Law §131.) 

The Statute of Limitations must be pleaded as an affirmative defense and cannot be proved under a general denial. Rodger v. Bliss, 130 Misc. 168, 223 N. Y. S. 

401; Richardson v. Gregory, 219 App. Div. 211, 219 N. 

Y. S. 397, aff'd, 245 N. Y. 540, 157 N. E. 849; Harmon v. Alfred Peats Co., 243 N. Y. 473, 154 N. E. 314.

A release must be pleaded as new matter constituting a defense. Boxberger v. N. Y., N. H. & H. R. R. Co., 237 N. Y. 75, 142 N. E. 357.

The rules as to pleading and proving payment are as follows: 

"(1) In an action upon contract for the payment of money only, where the complaint does not allege a balance due over and above all payments made, it is suffi-

cient for the plaintiff to allege and prove a breach of the obligation by the non-payment thereof when it matured, as the presumption of non-payment continues until met by the allegation and proof of payment. "(2) When the complaint sets forth a balance in excess of all payments, owing to the structure of the pleading, it is necessary for the plaintiff to prove the allegation as made, and this leaves the amount of the payments open to the defendant under a general denial. "(3) When the action is not upon contract for the payment of money, but is upon an obligation created by operation of law, or is for the enforcement of a lien where non-payment of the amount secured is part of the cause of action, it is necessary both to allege and prove the fact of non-payment." Conklin v. Weatherwax, 181 

N. Y. 258, 268, 73 N. E. 1028.

Although a complaint be defective because it does not state that a specified sum is due upon the note in suit, the defect is cured by an answer which does not allege payment. Cohu v. Husson, 113 N. Y. 662, 21 N. E. 703. 

When the contract sued upon is valid on the face of the complaint, the defendant may not introduce evidence to establish its illegality unless he has raised the question in his answer. Milbank v. Jones, 127 N. Y. 

370, 28 N. E. 31.

The illegality of a contract, which is valid on its face, must be pleaded as a defense and cannot be put in issue by a general denial. Norris v. McMechen, 134 Misc. 

866, 236 N. Y. S. 486.

The mere statement that the contract sued upon was illegal is not a sufficient defense. The facts upon which illegality can be predicated must be set forth in the answer. City Nat. Bank in Miami v. Lake Const. 

Co., 227 App. Div. 85; 237 N. Y. S. 58. "A license to do something that cannot be lawfully done without such license is new matter constituting a defense which must be alleged in the answer." Ramme v. Long Island R. R. Co., 226 N. Y. 327, 331, 123 N. E. 747.

In the absence of an allegation in the complaint that the contract sued upon was in writing, the defense of the

PLEADINGS 

137 Statute of Frauds must be pleaded as an affirmative defense. Bean v. Flint, 204 N. Y. 153, 97 N. E. 490;
