Bauer v. Oceanic Steam Navigation Co., 178 N. Y. 339, 70 N. E. 863; Crane v. Powell, 139 N. Y. 379, 34 N. E. 

911; Marston v. Swelt, 66 N. Y. 206; Bernham C. & M. 

Corp. v. Ship-A-Hoy, Ltd., Inc., 200 App. Div. 399, 193 

N. Y. S. 372.

The defendant is not required to plead the Statute of Frauds when it is apparent on the face of the complaint that the contract is unenforceable because not in writing. Magee v. Fish, 175 App. Div. 125, 161 N. Y. S. 1057. 

In an action for death of motorist struck by a trolley car at a crossing, the defendant has the burden of pleading and proving contributory negligence under §131 of the Decedent Estate Law. Bond v. Schenectady Ry. Co., 251 N. Y. 315, 167 N. E. 455.

The objection that the plaintiff is not the real party in interest must be pleaded in the answer. Ducosse v. 

American Yellow Taxi Operators, 224 App. Div. 516, 231 N. Y. S. 51. 

Unless the action is one in which ownership must be shown by the plaintiff, the objection that the plaintiff is not the real party in interest must be pleaded as a defense. It cannot be proved under a general denial. 

Willey v. Cameron, Michel & Co., Inc., 217 App. Div. 

651, 217 N. Y. S. 248.

In an action against the surety on a lease for rent for July and August, judgment by default was entered. 

Such judgment is res adjudicata as to the defense of constructive eviction, which cannot be raised in an action against the surety for the rent for subsequent months. 

Phipps v. Oprandy, 69 App. Div. 497, 74 N. Y. S. 985. 

Only one action may be brought on a single contract if all claims thereunder have matured. A judgment in an action on some of the items of a contract is a bar to subsequent action on other items which could have been included in the first complaint. But where there are two accounts between the parties, entirely separate and independent of each other, a recovery on one is not a bar to recovery on the second. Secor v. Sturgis, 16 

N. Y. 548.

Justification must be pleaded in the answer and cannot be proved under a general denial in an action for false arrest and imprisonment, assault and malicious prosecution. Peterson v. N. Y. Consolidated R. R. Co., 230 N. Y. 566, 130 N. E. 891. 

§225. Stating Separately and Numbering. 

Each separate defense must be stated separately and numbered, and divided into paragraphs numbered consecutively. As far as possible each paragraph should contain but a single allegation, but an allegation contained in one paragraph may be incorporated as a whole in another defense by reference without repeating it. Denials of facts alleged in the complaint must not be repeated or incorporated in a separate defense. A fact once denied is deemed denied for all purposes. (Rule 90.) 

§226. In the Municipal Court. 

The provisions of the Municipal Court Code regarding motions which may be addressed to the pleadings have already been discussed.13 There are no particular specifications for motions addressed to the answer, and, in general, the practice in the supreme court applies. $227. Counterclaims.¹4

If the defendant has an independent claim against the plaintiff, he may bring a separate action or, in a proper case set forth his cause of action as a counterclaim in the answer. The rules specified above 15 for pleading particular causes of action in the complaint apply to similar causes of action in counterclaims. $228. Requisites.16 

A counterclaim to be interposed in the answer: 

13 See p. 128. 

14 For counterclaims in matrimonial actions, see pp. 285, 289, 294. 

15 See pp. 109-113. 

16 For consolidating actions where counterclaiming is not permitted, see p. 195.

PLEADINGS 

139 (1) Must tend to diminish or defeat plaintiff's recovery. (2) Must be a claim against the plaintiff or the person whom he represents. (3) Must be in favor of the defendant, or one or more defendants. (4) Must be such that a separate judgment may be had in the action against the plaintiff or the plaintiff and another person or persons alleged to be liable. (5) Must be one of the following causes of action: (a) A cause of action arising out of the contract or transaction set forth in the complaint as the foundation of the plaintiff's claim or connected with the subject of the action. (b) In an action on contract, any other cause of action on contract existing at the time the action was commenced. (C. P. A. §266.) "The test of the propriety of a counterclaim is whether the defendant may upon the same facts maintain a separate action against the plaintiff." Kupfer v. 

Thompson, 227 App. Div. 516, 518, 238 N. Y. S. 222, 224.

In an action for conversion and breach of contract, the defendant set up a general denial. He had previously issued a summons in another court in an action arising out of the same transaction for work, labor and services. 

The defendant cannot be compelled to set up his cause of action as a counterclaim in the action for conversion and breach of contract, but may proceed in his separate action. Walkup v. Mesick, 110 App. Div. 326, 97 N. Y. S. 142. See also Brown v. Gallaudet, 80 N. Y. 413. 

A counterclaim must be alleged as such in order that it shall not be deemed a mere defense. Technical language is unnecessary, but the intention to make a personal claim against the plaintiff must be clear. It is best to state that the pleading is a counterclaim or include a prayer for relief. Bates v. Rosenkraus, 37 N. 

Y. 409.

When a defendant has an election to set up a cross claim of any kind to diminish or overcome the claim of the plaintiff, or to bring an independent action thereon, such claim, if asserted, must be set up as a counterclaim in the action. If a counterclaim is relied upon, it must be alleged in the answer and not left to inference. 

Richard Deeves & Son v. The Manhattan Life Ins. Co., 

195 N. Y. 324, 88 N. E. 395.

Where plaintiff merely seeks equitable relief and demands no personal money judgment against one of the defendants, the latter cannot set up a counterclaim and demand a money judgment against plaintiff. Lip man v. Jackson Architectural Iron-Works, 128 N. Y. 

58, 27 N. E. 975.

In an action for the taking and conversion of a quantity of wood, defendant may counterclaim that he was the mortgagee of the land and that plaintiff had wrongfully cut the wood, thereby wasting the land to defendant's damage. Here the counterclaim was connected with the subject of the action. Carpenter v. Manhattan Life Ins. Co., 93 N. Y. 552.

Generally one tort cannot be pleaded as a counterclaim to an action on another where there is no necessary or legal connection between them. However, in a suit in equity to restrain the defendant from interfering with the plaintiff's right of access to a wharf to which title is claimed by adverse user, the defendant may, as a counterclaim, allege ownership of the wharf, ask that the plaintiff be enjoined from entering thereon and claim damages for trespass. The subject-matter of the action is the respective rights of the parties in the wharf, an if defendant is successful, the plaintiff's claim would be defeated. Kelly v. Webster, 143 App. 

Div. 737, 128 N. Y. S. 58. 

A counterclaim may not be interposed in an action based on fraud and deceit unless it is alleged that the counterclaim arose out of the transaction set forth in the complaint as the foundation for the plaintiff's claim, or is connected with the subject of the action. Levan v. 

American Saftey Table Co., 222 App. Div. 110, 225 N. Y. 

S. 583. 

An equitable counterclaim for specific performance may be interposed in an action at law for the purchase

PLEADINGS 

141 price. Weinheimer Bros. v. Ross, 205 N. Y. 518, 99 N. 

E. 145.

If the defendants are severally liable, one of them may interpose a counterclaim in his favor. American 

Guild v. Damon, 186 N. Y. 360, 78 N. E. 1081. 

A joint demand cannot be counterclaimed in an action against one defendant on a several claim. Hopkins v. 

Lane, 87 N. Y. 501.

In order that the dismissal of a counterclaim may constitute a bar to a subsequent action, the subject matter of such counterclaim must have been in litigation and a final determination must have been made. Where a counterclaim on a note had been interposed in an action which resulted in a judgment by default for plaintiff, but no evidence was adduced on the counterclaim and the counterclaim was not mentioned in the judgment, an action on the note involved in the counterclaim is not barred. Voorhees v. Dennie, 131 Misc. 391, 227 N. Y. S. 607. $229. Particular Counterclaims.

A counterclaim under (b) above is subject to the following rules: (1) If the action brought by the plaintiff is upon a negotiable promissory note or bill of exchange which was assigned to the plaintiff after it became due, a claim existing against the person who so assigned or transferred the note will be allowed as a counterclaim if it might have been so allowed against the assignor while the note belonged to him. (2) If the action brought by the plaintiff is founded on an assigned 17 contract, other than a negotiable promissory note or bill of exchange, a claim existing against the party thereto or against an assignee thereof at the time of the assignment, and belonging to the defendant, in good faith, before notice of the assignment, will be allowed as a 

17 For claims which may not be assigned, see p. 59.

counterclaim to the amount of the plaintiff's demand, if it might have been so allowed against the party, or the assignee, while the contract belonged to him. (3) If the plaintiff is a trustee for another or if the plaintiff has no actual interest in the contract upon which the action is founded, a demand against the plaintiff will not be allowed as a counterclaim. But a demand existing against the person whom the trustee represents, or a demand against the person for whose benefit the action was brought, will be allowed as a counterclaim up to the amount of the plaintiff's claim if it might have been so allowed in an action brought by the person beneficially interested. (C. 

P. A. §267.) 

In order that a claim against the assignor may be set up as a counterclaim in the assignee's action, it must have been due at the time of the assignment. Michigan 

Savings Bank v. Millar, 110 App. Div. 670, 96 N. Y. S. 568. 

If the defendant is sued as an executor, administrator or other person in a representative capacity, he may counterclaim a demand belonging to the decedent or other person whom he represents, if the person so represented might have done so in an action against him. (C. P. A. §268.) 

Where the plaintiff is an executor or administrator suing in his representative capacity, a demand against the decedent, belonging to the defendant at the time of the death, may be set forth as a counterclaim as if the decedent had brought the action during his life-time. If a balance is the defendant, judgment may be entered therefor against the plaintiff in his representative capacity. 

(C. P. A. §269.) 

found to both? $230. Several Counterclaims.

The defendant may set forth in his answer as many counterclaims as he has. They may be legal or equitable.

PLEADINGS 

143 (C. P. A. §262.) But each separate counterclaim must be stated separately and numbered, and divided into paragraphs numbered consecutively. As far as possible, each paragraph should contain but a single allegation, but one counterclaim may be incorporated as a whole in another counterclaim by reference. Denials of facts alleged in the complaint must not be repeated nor incorporated in a separate counterclaim. (Rule 90.) $231. New Parties in a Counterclaim.

The defendant must set forth the names of all persons who would be defendants if he brought a separate action on the cause stated in his counterclaim. Any person named who is not already a party to the action must be served with a copy of the answer. A person so served may reply within the time limited for the service of an answer to a complaint, or he may serve a notice of appearance on the party interposing the counterclaim. (C. P. A. §271.) 

A defendant so impleaded may move to strike out the counterclaim as improperly attempting to bring him into the action. Federal Credit Bureau v. Narice Holding Corp., 130 Misc. 37, 239 N. Y. S. 244. $232. In the Municipal Court.

A counterclaim may include any claim or cause of action of which the court has jurisdiction. In a proper case the counterclaim may be against the person whom the plaintiff represents and may be in favor of one or more defendants between whom and the plaintiff a separate judgment may be had in the action. However, the court may direct that claims or causes of action joined in a counterclaim be tried separately, or may strike out a counterclaim and allow another action thereon to be brought. (Mun. Ct. Code §85.) $233. Defects Waived.

The defects appearing on the face of the answer which

are waived unless an objection is taken by motion are: (1) That the defendant lacks legal capacity to recover on the counterclaim therein. (2) That another action is pending between the same parties for the cause of action stated in a counterclaim. (3) That a counterclaim is not one which may be properly interposed in the action. (C. P. A. §278.) $234. Defects Not Waived.

The provisions stated in respect of objections to the complaint which are not waived by failure to object thereto on motion before trial apply to similar objections to the answer.

The manner of stating objections, moving as to part of the answer and pleading to the remainder, and the effect of pleading pending a motion are the same as in the case of an objection taken to the complaint.18 (C. P. A. §§279-283.) §235. Motions Addressed to the Answer. 

In general the motions which may be addressed to the answer are similar to those which may be addressed to the complaint. They may be divided into the following classes: (1) A motion to dismiss a counterclaim. (2) A motion to strike out all or part of the answer. (3) A motion to compel the defendant to correct the answer. 

§236. Defects on the Face of a Counterclaim. 

Within 10 days after the service of the answer, the plaintiff may serve a notice of motion to dismiss a counterclaim, if one or more of the following defects appear on the face thereof: (1) The court lacks jurisdiction of the subject of the counterclaim. 

18 See p. 122.

PLEADINGS 

145 (2) The defendant lacks legal capacity to recover on the counterclaim. (3) Another action is pending between the same parties for the same cause. (4) The counterclaim is not one which may be properly interposed in the action. (5) The counterclaim does not state facts sufficient to constitute a cause of action. (Rule 109.)

Affidavits may not be considered on a motion under 

Rule 109. Welch v. City of Niagara Falls, 210 App. Div. 

170, 205 N. Y. S. 454. 

8237. Defects Not on the Face of the Answer.

If the defect does not appear on the face of the answer, the plaintiff has 10 days after service of the answer in which to serve notice of motion for judgment dismissing a counterclaim where: (1) The court lacks jurisdiction of the subject of the counterclaim. (2) Another action is pending between the same parties for the same cause. (3) There is an existing final judgment or decree of a court of competent jurisdiction rendered on the merits which determined the same cause of action between the parties. (4) The claim set forth in the counterclaim has been released. (5) The counterclaim is based on a contract which is unenforceable under the statute of frauds.

The motion is made on the pleadings and affidavits tending to show the defects alleged. The motion is determined as is the similar one addressed to the complaint.10 (Rules 

110; 108.) 

19 See p. 124.

$238. Motions to Strike Out. 

Within 10 days after the service of the answer, the plaintiff may serve notice of motion to strike out a defense consisting of new matter, if it appears on the face of the answer: (1) That the defense is insufficient in law. (Rule 109.) 

Within 20 days after service of the answer, the plaintiff may serve notice of motion to strike out matter in the answer which: (2) Is sham, frivolous, irrelevant, redundant, repetitious, unnecessary, impertinent or scandalous, or may tend to prejudice, embarrass or delay a fair trial. If the motion is granted, the answer may be deemed amended accordingly or the court may order defendant to serve an amended answer omitting the objectionable matter. (Rules 103; 105.) 

Within 20 days after service of the answer the plaintiff may serve notice of motion to strike out an answer which: (3) Is sham or frivolous.

If the answer is sham or frivolous, the court may treat it as a nullity and give judgment accordingly, or may allow a new answer to be served on just terms. (Rules 104,20 105.)

An extension of time in which to serve a notice of motion under Rules 103 and 104 may not be had unless 2 days' notice of the application therefor is given the plaintiff. (Rule 105.) 

Plaintiff's motion to strike out affirmative defenses as insufficient in law admits the truth of the allegations of such defenses and brings into question the sufficiency of the complaint. Dougherty v. Equitable Life Assur. 

Soc., 135 Misc. 103, 236 N. Y. S. 673; Small v. Sullivan, 245 N. Y. 343, 157 N. E. 261.

A motion to strike out a defense is in the nature of a demurrer and searches the record, and the sufficiency of the complaint may be determined on such motion. 

20 See Horvath v. Brettschneider, p. 157.

PLEADINGS 

147 

Willey v. Cameron, Michel & Co., Inc., 217 App. Div. 

651, 217 N. Y. S. 248. But a similar motion to test a counterclaim does not involve a search to determine whether the complaint states a cause of action. Fulton 

Co. G. & E. Co. v. Hudson Riv. T. Co., 200 N. Y. 287, 93 N. E. 1052.

Affidavits cannot be considered on a motion to strike out an answer as frivolous and sham under Rule 104, and for the purpose of delay only. Reed v. Neu-Pro. 

Const. Corporation, 226 App. Div. 70, 234 N. Y. S. 400; 

Lowe v. Plainfield Trust Co., 216 App. Div. 72, 215 N. 

Y. S. 50. 

If the defense of another action pending is good on its face, it cannot be stricken out on motion, under Rules 104 and 109, since it cannot be judged in the light of facts extrinsic to the answer. Levan v. Amer ican Safety Table Co., 222 App. Div. 110, 225 N. Y. S. 

583.

A motion under Rule 109, is an application for a determination of the face value of the pleadings; and, therefore, only the pleadings may be considered. If it is impossible to determine the validity of a defense without considering the evidence, then a motion to strike the defense from the answer will be denied. First

National Bank of Palmyro v. Jacobs, 132 Misc. 840, 230 

N. Y. S. 762.

On a motion to strike out a defense as insufficient, the defendant may attack the complaint on the ground that it does not state facts sufficient to constitute a cause of action because the motion searches the record for the first fault in pleading and reaches back to condemn the first pleading that is defective in substance. Chelsea 

Exchange Bank v. Travelers Insurance Co., 173 App. 

Div. 829, 160 N. Y. S. 225. See also Maurice v. Travelers 

Ins. Co., 121 Misc. 427, 201 N. Y. S. 372. But a similar motion addressed to a counterclaim in the answer searches the record only as far as the counterclaim, and not back to the complaint. Fulton Co. G. & E. Co. v. Hudson Riv. T. Co., 200 N. Y. 287, 93 N. E. 1052.

Such motion addressed to the reply to a counterclaim, tests the sufficiency of the counterclaim. Klauck v. Federal Insurance Co., 131 App. Div. 519, 115 N. Y. S. 1049.

$239. Motions to Correct the Answer.

Motions to correct the answer are similar to motions made to correct the complaint. The plaintiff may make a motion: (1) To compel the defendant to state separately and number the defenses in the answer. (2) To compel the defendant to state separately and number the counterclaims. (Rule 90.) (3) To correct the answer with respect of matter which is so indefinite, uncertain or obscure that its meaning or application is not apparent. (4) To correct a defect of parties. (5) To correct a misjoinder of causes of action. (Rule 102.) 

A motion under Rule 102 should be noticed within 20 days after the service of the answer. An application for an extension of time must be on 2 days' notice to the defendant. If the court grants the motion, he may direct that an amended answer be served. (Rules 102; 105.) $240. The Reply.

When the answer contains a counterclaim, the plaintiff may serve a reply thereto. A reply to a counterclaim is like an answer to a complaint, except that the reply contains no counterclaims. (C. P. A. §272.) If the plaintiff fails to reply when the answer contains a counterclaim, the defendant may make a motion, on notice, for judgment on the counterclaim.21 (C. P. A. §494.) If an answer contains new matter constituting a defense by way of avoidance, the defendant may move to compel the plaintiff to reply to the new matter. 

The determination of the motion is within the discretion of the court. The proceedings on failure to reply to such new 

21 See Judgment by Default, p. 231.

PLEADINGS 

149 matter when a reply is ordered are the same as in a case of a counterclaim. (C. P. A. §274.) 

8241. Time to Reply.

The reply should be served 20 days after service of the answer. The time to reply will not be extended except on an affidavit of merits. When the time to reply has once been extended by stipulation or order, a further extension will not be granted unless 2 days' notice of the application has been given to the defendant. Copies of the affidavits used on an application must always be served with an order of extension. (C. P. A. §273; Rules 85; 87, 88.) 

8242. In the Municipal Court.

In the municipal court, a reply to a counterclaim or an affirmative defense is unnecessary. The allegations of a counterclaim or an affirmative defense are deemed denied, even though no reply is filed. However, at any time before judgment the court may order a written reply, verified or unverified. The general rules as to motions addressed to the pleadings in a municipal court action apply to motions addressed to the reply.22 (Mun. Ct. Code §87.) 

8243. Contents of the Reply. 

The reply should contain the title of the action, the name of the court, the county or district, the name of the plaintiff's attorney or plaintiff's name if he appears in person, and the address of the attorney or plaintiff as in the case of the complaint. In addition, the reply must contain a general or specific denial: (1) of every material allegation of the counterclaim controverted by the plaintiff; or (2) of any knowledge or information thereof sufficient to form a belief. 

22 See p. 128.

As in the case of the answer, the grounds of reply which would take the defendant by surprise or raise issues of fact not within the pleadings if not pleaded must be set forth in the reply.23 (C. P. A. §242.) 

The reply may contain: (1) new matter not inconsistent with the complaint constituting a defense to the counterclaim; (2) two or more distinct avoidances of the same defense or counterclaim. (C. P. A. §272.) 

$244. Admissions.

In a case where a reply is required, every material allegation of new matter in the answer not controverted by the reply is taken as true for the purposes of the action. Allegations of new matter in the answer to which a reply is not required and of new matter in the reply are considered to be controverted by traverse or avoidance as the case may be. (C. P. A. §243.) 

$245. Motions Addressed to the Reply.

In general, the motions which the defendant may make in respect to the reply are similar to those which the plaintiff may make to the answer, with the exceptions of those motions which may be directed to a counterclaim in the answer. The defendant may move: (1) To compel the plaintiff to state separately and number the defenses in the reply.24 (Rule 90.) (2) To compel the plaintiff to correct the reply if it contains any matter which is so indefinite, uncertain or obscure that its precise meaning or application is not apparent. (Rule 102.) (3) To strike out matter in the reply which is sham, frivolous, irrelevant, redundant, repetitious, unnecessary, 23 See p. 135. 

24 See p. 148.

PLEADINGS 

151 impertinent or scandalous or which may tend to prejudice, embarrass or delay the fair trial of the action. (Rule 103.) (4) To strike out a reply as sham or frivolous and for judgment thereon. The court may treat such reply as a nullity. (Rule 104.) 25 

The motions under Rules 102, 103 and 104 may be noticed within 20 days after service of the reply. The court may order or allow an amendment, as the case may be. (Rules 102-105.) (5) To strike out the reply or separate defenses therein on the ground that it is insufficient in law on the face thereof. This motion should be made within 10 days after service of the reply. (Rule 111.)

A motion to dismiss a reply on the ground that it is insufficient in law searches the record as far as the counterclaim, and the plaintiff may have a determination of the sufficiency of the counterclaim. Klauck v. 

Federal Insurance Co., 131 App. Div. 519, 115 N. Y. S. 1049. 

Setting forth a counterclaim as a defense obviates the need of a reply. Susquehanna Steamship Co., Inc. v. Anderson & Co., Inc., 239 N. Y. 285, 146 N. E. 381.

A motion for permission to file an answer to a reply is not an application for permission to file a supplementary pleading and there is no statutory authority therefore. Sleicher v. Sleicher, 228 N. Y. S. 711. $246. Amendments of Pleadings.26

The amendments ordered or allowed by the court after determination of motions addressed to the pleadings have already been discussed under the various rules. In addition, any pleading may be amended as of course within specified time limits. 

25 See Horvath v. Brettschneider, p. 157. 

26 For amendments of the pleadings on the trial, see p. 213.

$247. Amendments of Course. 

A pleading may be amended once as of course, without costs and without prejudice to the proceedings already had, as follows: 

(1) The plaintiff may amend the complaint at any time before the period for answering it expires; or within 20 days after: (a) Service of the complaint. (b) Service of the answer. (c) Service of a notice of motion addressed to the complaint. (2) The defendant may amend the answer at any time before the period for replying thereto expires; or within 20 days after: (a) Service of the answer. (b) Service of the reply. (c) Service of a notice of motion addressed to the answer. (3) The plaintiff may amend the reply within 20 days after: (a) Service of the reply. (b) Service of the answer. (c) Service of a notice of motion addressed to the reply.

However, if it is made to appear to the court that the amendment was made for the purpose of delay and that because of the delay the adverse party will lose the benefit of a term for which the case was or may be noticed, the amended pleading may be stricken out or restored to its original form on just terms. (C. P. A. §244.) A copy of the amended pleading must be served on the attorney for the adverse party. An amended complaint or an amended answer must be answered or replied to within 20 days after service. The penalties for failure to answer or reply are the same as in the case of an original pleading. (Rule 101.)

PLEADINGS 

153 $248. Supplemental Pleadings.

Upon application, a party may, and in a proper case must, be allowed to serve a supplemental pleading alleging material facts: (1) Which occurred after his former pleading was made; or (2) Of which he was ignorant when the former pleading was made.

The supplemental pleading may be in place of, or in addition to, the former pleading. (C. P. A. §245.) 

Where a cause of action survives the transference of a party's interest, the devolution of a party's liability or the death of a party,27 and a new party is substituted, the court may direct that supplemental pleadings be made. (C. P. A. §87.) "A supplemental complaint is limited to setting forth facts occurring subsequent to the commencement of the action and in aid of the original cause of action. It cannot be made the means of setting forth facts constituting an entirely new and independent cause of action." 

Rummell v. Blanchard, 173 App. Div. 695, 697, 160 N. 

Y. S. 235, 236.

The court has no power to allow a supplemental pleading which sets forth acts of the defendant subsequent to the commencement of the action and which demands judgment for damages therefor for which an independent action might have been brought. However, where the acts prior and subsequent to the commencement of the action constitute a single wrong, the plaintiff must recover all damages in one action. Park & Sons 

Co. v. Hubbard, 198 N. Y. 136, 91 N. E. 582. 

8249. Bills of Particulars.

A demand may be made requiring the adverse party to furnish an itemized and detailed statement, called a bill of particulars, of his claim. A bill of particulars is not a plead- 

27 See p. 29.

ing in itself, but an amplification thereof. If a pleading does not set forth the items of an alleged account, a bill of particulars of the account must be served within 10 days after demand. A party failing to comply with this demand is precluded from giving evidence of the account. If a defective bill of particulars is served, the court may order a further account. (C. P. A. §246.) In other cases, a bill of particulars may be demanded; and if it is not delivered, an order therefor may be obtained, upon notice. Upon default, the court may exclude evidence of the parts of affirmative allegations which should have been itemized in a bill of particulars. (C. P. A. §247; Rule 115.)

A bill of particulars is an amplification of a pleading, but it does not have the same effect as if the allegations had been inserted in the complaint before answer. A bill of particulars does not make the complaint bad or the answer good. It does not cure the defendant's omission to plead the defense of the Statute of Frauds or nullify the waiver or abandonment of that defense to be inferred from such omission. It serves merely to explain and make definite the allegations set forth in the pleading and limits the proof to be offered at the trial. Harmon v. Peats Co., 243 N. Y. 

473, 154 N. E. 314.

When a bill of particulars setting forth items of the complaint has been demanded and not served, evidence of such items should be excluded only if the demand was an order. Gebhard v. Parker, 120 N. Y. 33, 33 N. 

E. 982. $250. In the Municipal Court.

In the municipal court, the defendant may file with the clerk a written demand for a bill of particulars at the time of answering. The plaintiff may file a similar demand within 

3 days after the counterclaim is interposed. Within 3 days after demand in either case, the bill of particulars must be filed. Upon notice or motion, failure to comply may be punished by precluding evidence of the affirmative allegations

PLEADINGS 

155 of which a bill of particulars should have been filed. The court may order the service and filing of a bill of particulars of a cause of action in a complaint or counterclaim. 

(Mun. Ct. Code §78.) 

$251. Verification.28

When a sworn and signed statement as to the truth of the allegations contained in a pleading is appended thereto, the pleading is said to be verified, and the statement is said to be an affidavit of verification. In the affidavit the deponent states that the pleading is true except as to those allegations stated to be on information and belief, and as to those matters he believes it to be true. If the deponent is not the party on whose behalf the pleading is made, he must state the grounds of his belief as to all matters not stated upon his own knowledge and also the reason the affidavit is not made by the party. (C. P. A. §276; Rule 100.) 

$252. Obligatory Verification. 

In some cases a pleading or part of it must be verified; in all others it may be verified. Verification of the complaint is obligatory in the following instances: (1) When application is made for an order to serve the summons by publication. (C. P. A. §232.) 

(2) When an action is brought on a judgment against joint debtors not served with the summons in the original action.20 (C. P. A. §1186.) 

(3) Whenever a lis pendens is filed in an action affect ing real property.30 (C. P. A. §120.) 

(4) When an action to establish a mechanic's lien is brought in the municipal court. (Mun. Ct. Code §78.) 

28 For advantages of verification, see Judgment by Default, p. 232. 

29 See p. 230. 

30 See p. 274.

If a pleading is verified, all subsequent pleadings and bills of particulars must be verified, except: (1) The general answer of an infant by his guardian ad litem. (2) Where the party pleading would be privileged from testifying as a witness concerning the matter contained in the pleading. The fact that a verified complaint charges the defendant with fraud does not excuse him from verifying his answer. (C. P. A. §248; Rule 116; C. P. A. §250; Mun.

Ct. Code §78.) 

The answer of a defendant corporation in an action to recover damages for the non-payment of a promissory note or other evidence of debt for the absolute payment of money must be verified. (C. P. A. §252.) Defenses which do not involve the merits of the action are called dilatory defenses and must be verified. (C. P. A. §251.) If the complaint is not verified and the answer contains a counterclaim and defenses, the counterclaim may be verified as if it were a separate pleading. (C. P. A. §249.) §253. The Deponent. 

In general, the verification must be made by the party; or by the party acquainted with the facts, if several parties united in interest plead together. Any person acquainted with the facts may verify a pleading by the people of the state or a public officer on their behalf. If a domestic corporation is a party, the verification is made by an officer thereof. An agent or attorney may verify a pleading if: (1) The party is a foreign corporation; (2) The party is not within the county where the attorney has his office; (3) None of several parties united in interest and pleading together who is acquainted with the facts is within the county;

PLEADINGS 

157 (4) The action or defense is founded upon a written instrument for the payment of money only which is in the possession of the agent or attorney; (5) The agent or attorney has personal knowledge of all the material allegations of the pleading. (Rule 99.)

The remedy for a defective verification is to treat the pleading as unverified. If the copy served is without a copy of a sufficient verification, the adverse party may, upon due notice, treat it as a nullity. (C. P. A. §253.) 

§254. Judgment on the Pleadings. 

If warranted by the pleadings or admissions of a party or parties, the court may render judgment on the whole or a part of a cause of action at any stage of an action or appeal. (C. P. A. §476.) After issue is joined, certain motions for judgment may be made. Either party may move for judgment on the pleadings. If the plaintiff or the defendant is entitled to judgment on the pleadings, the court may give judgment accordingly, regardless of which party made the motion. (Rule 112; Mun. Ct. Code §91.) 

If an answer is frivolous on its face, a motion for judgment under Rule 112 is proper, since only the pleadings will be considered thereon. But if, upon inspection, it cannot be said that the defenses are frivolous, the proper procedure is a motion to strike them out as sham under Rule 104. On this motion affidavits may be considered. Horvath v. Brettschneider, 131 Misc. 618, 227 

N. Y. S. 109.

A motion for judgment dismissing a complaint on the ground that it does not state facts sufficient to constitute a cause of action may be properly brought under 

Rule 112 and if not barred by failure to move within 

20 days under Rule 106. Simon v. David, 241 N. Y. S. 188. 

No affidavits may be used on a motion under Rule 

112. Donnelly v. Bauder, 217 App. Div. 59, 216 N. Y.

S. 437.

§255. Summary Judgment. 

The plaintiff may make a motion to strike out the answer and for judgment thereon in any action to recover a debt or liquidated demand arising on: (1) A contract, whether express or implied, sealed or not sealed. (2) A judgment for a stated sum.

The motion must be made on the pleadings and an affidavit verifying the cause of action, stating the amount claimed and stating the plaintiff's belief that there is no defense to the action. The defendant may present affidavits or other proof showing facts which entitle him to defend. 

If the motion is decided in favor of the plaintiff, judgment is entered and the action is terminated. If the motion is decided against the plaintiff, the case must be tried in the usual way. (Rule 113.) If the defense is good as to only part of the claim, the plaintiff may have judgment for the part of his claim which is admitted or to which there is no defense, and the remainder may be severed. (Rule 114.) 

A motion for summary judgment under Rule 113 is directed to the question as to whether, in a limited class of cases, the defendant has a genuine defense. The court does not try the issues of fact. It merely determines whether there is an issue of fact to be tried. 

Hanna v. Mitchell, 235 N. Y. 534, 139 N. E. 724.

Rule 113 does not infringe on the right of trial by jury. It is a rule of procedure, which prescribes a method by which the court may determine summarily whether or not a bone fide issue exists. General Investment Co. v. Interborough Rapid Transit Co., 235 N. Y. 

133, 139 N. E. 216. 

Summary judgment under Rule 113 will not be granted in an action against a carrier for failure to deliver goods shipped, even where the damages were stipulated in the contract. A debt under Rule 113 must be founded on a contract to pay a liquidated sum on execution and is not a claim for damages. Norwich

PLEADINGS 

159 

Pharmacal Co. v. Barrett, 205 App. Div. 749, 200 N. Y. 

S. 298.

On a motion to strike out the answer and for summary judgment, the court is authorized to determine only whether there is an issue to be tried. Plaintiff's affidavit must state all facts necessary to establish a cause of action, and his belief that there is no defense. 

Defendant must show by affidavits or otherwise that he has a good defense, interposed in good faith and not for delay. Dwan v. Massarene, 199 App. Div. 872, 192 

N. Y. S. 577. 

A summary judgment should be granted only when it is clear that no real question of fact is presented. 

Gen. Investment Co. v. Interborough R. T. Co., 235 N. 

Y. 133, 139 N. E. 216. Summary judgment in an action on checks payable to "cash" should be denied where the answer alleges the only consideration for the checks was an illegal transaction under the Eighteenth Amendment and knowledge thereof on the part of the plaintiff. Weinberg v. Goldstein, 226 App. Div. 479, 235 N. Y. S. 529. 

See also Croker v. Croker, 252 N. Y. 24, 168 N. E. 450. 

Application for summary judgment should be made only if the action is to recover on a liquidated debt or demand. This judgment should not be granted if the answer sets up a counterclaim which tends to reduce the claim or counterclaims and defenses which indicate that the claim was unliquidated. Schwed v. Kennedy, Inc., 220 App. Div. 189, 221 N. Y. S. 179.

In order to obtain summary judgment under Rule 113, it must be possible to calculate the amount of the judgment to which the plaintiff is entitled on the papers presented to the court. No further proof must be required, and where the answer raises the issue of the amount due, summary judgment is improper. Roberts v. Donahue, 246 N. Y. 447, 159 N. E. 393. "Technical defects in the pleading of an adversary are not available to a plaintiff upon an application under this rule for summary judgment." Curry v. MacKenzie, 239 N. Y. 267, 272, 146 N. E. 375. 

Rule 113 does not apply to foreclosure actions. Reed v. Neu-Pro. Const. Corporation, 226 App. Div. 70, 234 

N. Y. S. 400; Securities Acceptance Corp. v. E. M. Kane

Co., Inc., 119 Misc. 354, 196 N. Y. S. 519, aff'd. 207 App. 

Div. 840, 201 N. Y. S. 945.

An appeal may be taken from an order granting or denying summary judgment in the supreme court, Lee v. Graubard, 205 App. Div. 344, 199 N. Y. S. 563 (but see, Poland Export Corpt. v. Marcus, 204 App. Div. 302, 198 N. Y. S. 5), and in the city court, State Realty Co. v. Post, 123 Misc. 925, 206 N. Y. S. 713, but not in the municipal court. Swimmer v. Silverman, 128 Misc. 444, 218 N. Y. S. 315.

When a motion for summary judgment under Rule 113 is granted, it is good practice to enter both an order and a judgment. Weinberg v. Goldstein, 226 App. Div. 

479, 235 N. Y. S. 529.

A motion for summary judgment under Rule 113 is directed to the question: Has the defendant a genuine defense? Therefore, upon plaintiff's motion for summary judgment, the defendant is not entitled, as of course, to serve an amended answer under §244 if his time to do so has otherwise expired. Security Finance 

Co. v. Stuart, 130 Misc. 538, 224 N. Y. S. 257. 

Where the answer does not deny any of the allegations of the complaint but sets up a counterclaim based on another contract, the plaintiff, on a motion for summary judgment brought on affidavits, is entitled under Rule 114 and $476 of the Civil Practice Act to enter judgment for the claim stated in the complaint. The counterclaim will be severed. Monies collected on the judgment sufficient to satisfy the counterclaim must be deposited in court until the merits of the counterclaim are determined. Little Falls Dairy Co. v. Berghorn, 130 

Misc. 454, 224 N. Y. S. 34.

CHAPTER VII  §256. Nature of Provisional Remedies. 

After serving the summons or summons and complaint in an action, the plaintiff sometimes finds himself inadequately protected because there is danger that the defendant will abscond, dispose of his property, wantonly injure it or cause irreparable injury to property of the plaintiff before a judgment in the action can be obtained. 

8257. Kinds of Provisional Remedies. 

In anticipation of the above possibilities, the law gives such plaintiff certain provisional remedies. They are of a temporary nature and continue only until final determination of the action. These provisional remedies are: 

(1) Order of arrest. 

(2) Warrant of attachment. (3) Temporary injunction. (4) Appointment of a receiver.

The action to recover a chattel, formerly called replevin, is of the same character and will be included here. (C. P. A. §§814; 974; 1092.)

An order of arrest, a temporary injunction or a warrant of attachment cannot be granted after the parties have agreed on the facts and submitted the controversy to the court. (C. P. A. §548.) The removal of an action or pro ceeding to another court does not affect the validity of a provisional remedy. (C. P. A. §95.) A defendant who interposes a counterclaim upon which he demands affirmative judgment, is treated as a plaintiff within the meaning of these sections. (C. P. A. §824.) 

1 See p. 5. 161

§258. Simultaneous Remedies. 

An order of arrest, a warrant of attachment and an injunction order, may all be granted in the same action; but, in the discretion of the court or judge, the plaintiff may be made to elect between them, if it appears that two or all of them are unnecessary to protect the plaintiff. (C. P. A. §823.) When an order of arrest has been granted in a replevin action, and the defendant has been arrested, a subsequent replevin cannot be made. If the order of arrest has been granted but the defendant has not been arrested, the subsequent replevin of the chattel supersedes the order. 

(C. P. A. §1112.) 

$259. Application for an Order or Warrant.

Application for an order or warrant must be made before final judgment on affidavit showing the plaintiff's right thereto, and security must be furnished for the protection of the defendant's right. It may be made without notice to a court or judge. The affidavits, in the case of arrest, must show facts and circumstances from which the amount of bail to be required may be determined and must be filed within 10 days after service. The security furnished in this case must be equal to one tenth of the bail required and not less than $250. Security may be dispensed with in actions under §§826, subd. 9, and 827. (C. P. A. §§815, 816, 817, 818, 819; 835, 836, 837; 907; Rules 80, 81; Mun. Ct. Code §§30; 42; 58; 67.) $260. Execution of Mandate.

The warrant or order must be executed by the sheriff in supreme court and city court actions and by a marshal in municipal court actions, except that an injunction order may be served by a private person. (C. P. A. §§840; 883; 

910; 1100; City Ct. Act §28; Mun. Ct. Code §§30; 47; 60; 

67; 151.)

$261. An Order of Arrest. 

An order of arrest may be obtained: (1) in certain actions enumerated in the statute; (2) in equity actions where certain extrinsic facts appear. 

The nature of the cause of action gives the plaintiff the right to an order of arrest, when the action is brought to recover: (1) A fine or penalty. (2) Damages for a personal injury. (See Gen. Const. 

Law §37-a.)² (3) Damages for an injury to property, including the wrongful taking, detention, or conversion of personal property. (4) Damages for a breach of promise to marry. (5) Damages for misconduct or neglect in office or in a professional employment. (6) Damages for fraud or deceit. (7) A chattel where it is alleged in the complaint that the chattel or a part thereof has been concealed, removed, or disposed of. (8) Money received, or to recover damages for the conversion or misappropriation of property where it is alleged in the complaint that the money was received or the property embezzled, or fraudulently misapplied by a public officer or an attorney, or by an agent or officer of a corporation or bank in the course of his employment, or by a factor, agent, broker or other person in a fiduciary capacity. (9) Money, funds, or property owned or held by the state or subdivision or portion thereof, or municipal or public corporation, etc., which the defendant, without right, has obtained, received, converted or disposed of, or to recover damages for same. 

2 See p. 30.

(10) In an action on contract, other than a contract to marry, where the complaint alleges that the defendant was guilty of fraud in contracting or incurring the liability, or that he has or is about to remove his property with intent to defraud his creditors. (C. P. A. §826; Mun. Ct. Code §29.) 

The defendant may be arrested, if his servant committed a tort in the scope of his employment. Davids v. Brooklyn Heights R. R. Co., 104 App. Div. 23, 93 

N. Y. S. 285; Ossman v. Crowley, 101 App. Div. 597, 92 N. Y. S. 29.

In contract, the defendant can be arrested on his servant's fraud, only when personally guilty. Hathaway v. Johnson, 55 N. Y. 93.

The provisional remedy arrest is a drastic one and should be granted only upon clear proof that plaintiff has brought himself within the provisions of §826 of the Civil Practice Act. Todd-Buick v. Smith, 118 Misc. 

102, 192 N. Y. S. 459; affirmed 202 App. Div. 774, 194 

N. Y. S. 985. 

§262. Equity Actions. 

In an action wherein the judgment demanded requires the performance of an act, the neglect or refusal to perform which would be punishable as a contempt, where the defendant is a nonresident, or, being a resident, is about to depart from the state, by reason of which nonresidence there is danger that an order or judgment will be rendered ineffectual, the defendant may be arrested on order of the court, granted in its discretion, and served either before or after judgment. (C. P. A. §827; Mun. Ct. Code §29.)

Where more than one cause of action is stated in the complaint, an order of arrest cannot be granted unless sustained by authority of each of them. Boyle v. Semenoff, 201 App. Div. 426, 194 N. Y. S. 309. 

3 See p. 256.

PROVISIONAL remedies and repLEVIN 

165 

§263. Persons Subject to Arrest. 

A woman cannot be arrested except in a case where the order must be granted by the court or where it appears that the action is to recover damages for a wilful injury to person, character or property. (C. P. A. §829.) A person prosecuted in a representative capacity, as heir, executor, administrator, assignee, trustee, etc., cannot be arrested except for his personal act. (C. P. A. §831.) An officer of an unincorporated association against which the action is brought cannot be arrested therein. (Gen'l. Ass'ns. L. §15.) 

In the discretion of the court or judge, a lunatic, an idiot, or an infant under the age of 14 years, may be discharged from arrest as a privileged person. (C. P. A. §830.) 

§264. Privileged Persons. 

Certain persons are privileged from civil arrest while exercising public duties: (1) Foreign ministers, representatives and their domestic servants. (2) Members of Congress and the state legislature. (3) Officers of courts of record; attorneys employed in a case to be heard during the term. (4) Electors on election day. (5) Enlisted men of the army and navy, (6) Militiamen. (7) Policemen and firemen of second class cities. (8) Post office officials of New York City. (9) Resident witnesses subpoenaed or ordered to attend. (10) Nonresident witnesses or parties on the way to and from court. (11) Prisoners and the officers in charge while passing through another county. (C. P. A. §841; Holbrook v. Henderson, 4 Sandf. 619; Valarino v. Thompson, 7 N. Y. 576;

U. S. Const. Art. I, §6; Legislative L. §2; Civil Rights L. 

§24; Petrie v. Fitzgerald, 1 Daly 401; R. Fed. S. §1237;

Barnes Federal Code §1707; Military L. §235; Second Class Cities L. §139; N. Y. C. Charter §341; Civil Rights L. §25; 

Person v. Grier, 66 N. Y. 124; Civil Rights L. §22.)

Persons enticed into the jurisdiction or brought in by subterfuge are privileged. (Higgins v. Dewey, 14 N. Y. S. 

894, affirming 13 N. Y. S. 570; Smith v. Meyers, 1 Thomp. & C. 665.) The proper method of protest when a privileged person is arrested but not in custody, is by motion under 

C. P. A. §841, not habeas corpus. (Matter of Lampert, 21 

Hun 154.) §265. Bail.

The defendant, at any time before he is in contempt or before execution against his person, may be discharged from arrest at any hour, either upon giving bail (sureties who will answer for his complying with the judgment) or upon depositing the sum specified in the order of arrest. 

If the defendant is actually confined and judgment in the action has been rendered against him, but an execution against his person has not been issued, he may elect to give bail, make deposit or give a bond for liberties of the jail. A person in custody by virtue of an order of arrest, an execution, or in consequence of a surrender in exoneration of his bail, may obtain jail liberties on delivering to the sheriff an approved undertaking. Upon delivery of the undertaking, all previous bail or deposit must be discharged.

But a person guilty of contempt cannot obtain jail liber ties. (C. P. A. §§847, 848; Prison L. §§361, 362; 364, 364-a; 

Mun. Ct. Code §§32; 34, 35.) §266. Liberty under Bail and Undertaking. 

When a person gives bail, he has complete liberty, subject to his appearance when final judgment is rendered against him or at the direction of the court. The under-

taking giving a person liberties of the jail, restricts the person to a particular area. In New York, Bronx or Kings 

County he must remain within the particular county; in other counties, the areas are smaller. (Prison L. §§357, 358.) $267. Default.

Upon default on the undertaking the plaintiff must first exhaust his remedies against the defendant, but may then proceed against the bail by action. (C. P. A. §§870, 871.) 

$268. Surrender.

Except in an action to recover a chattel, the bail may surrender the defendant in their own exoneration, or the defendant may surrender himself. (C. P. A. §865.) $269. Vacating an Order of Arrest.

An order of arrest may be vacated on the ground that: 

(1) The person arrested is privileged. (C. P. A. §841.) (2) The complaint is insufficient. (C. P. A. §843.) 

(3) The papers upon which the order was granted are 

(4) Except in a case where the order can be granted only by the court, the plaintiff without excuse or with collusion unreasonably delays trial, entry of judgment, or issue of execution. (C. P. A. §846.) 

The court may allow an amendment which will make the complaint or papers sufficient. (C. P. A. §§843, 844, 

845.) The defendant may also apply for reduction of bail or for an increase in the security given by the plaintiff. (Rule 83; Mun. Ct. Code §38.) $270. Duration of Commitment.

The time during which a person may be confined, before final judgment, is unlimited, except in municipal court actions.

The provision of the Civil Rights Law Sec. 72, lim iting the time of imprisonment "upon any mandate against the person," refers only to a final process or mandate after an adjudication fixing the amount due.

It does not include orders of arrest issued at the time of the commencement of an action or before any recovery. Levy v. Salomon, 105 N. Y. 529, 12 N. É. 53. $271. In the Municipal Court.

The detention of the defendant arrested in a municipal court action is limited to 2 days, exclusive of Sundays and legal holidays, from the time he is first taken to court; unless the trial of the action has commenced and continued without unnecessary delay or is adjourned at defendant's request. (Mun. Ct. Code §32.) §272. Temporary Injunction by Order. 

The right to this order sometimes depends on the nature of the action. Actions in which it may be granted are chiefly actions in equity. Where it appears from the complaint that the plaintiff demands and is entitled to a judgment against the defendant restraining the commission or the continuance of an act, the commission or continuance of which during the pendency of the action would result in injury to the plaintiff, an injunction order may be granted to restrain it. (C. P. A. §877.) The order may be granted only upon notice. (C. P. A. §882, in effect Sept. 1, 1930.)

A court of equity has no inherent absolute power to grant temporary injunctions; that authority must be found in the Civil Practice Act. Where the complaint presents a case showing that affirmative action by the defendant of a temporary character is necessary to preserve the status of the parties, then a mandatory injunction may be granted; but if there be neither proof nor allegation to that effect, and the act sought to be enforced is not continuous in its character, but solely the one sought to be decreed by final judgment, then the issuing of a preliminary mandatory injunction is

without authority. Bachman v. Harrington, 184 N. Y. 

458, 77 N. E. 657.

The burden is on the plaintiff to show that there was no default on his part in the performance of the contract and unless he can show a clear legal right to the relief sought, a preliminary injunction should not issue. If the contract is ambiguous, it must be construed against the plaintiff if it was drawn by him. 

Shubert Theatrical Co. v. Gallagher, 200 App. Div. 596, 

193 N. Y. S. 401. 

§273. Uses of Temporary Injunctions. 

The following are examples of cases in which an injunction order may be obtained: (1) To restrain public officers from committing illegal 

(2) To prevent acts which are illegal under the zoning laws. (3) To restrain a person who breaches his agreement not to engage in a certain business. (4) To restrain a person from using a trade name or his own name in unfair competition. (5) In labor disputes, where there is an attempt on either side to effectuate an unlawful purpose or a lawful purpose by unlawful means. (6) By members of associations to restrain the officers thereof from doing acts contrary to the rules of the association. (7) To enjoin nuisances. (8) To restrain unauthorized production of a play. (9) To restrain an actor of special merit or reputation from performing for others during the existence of an exclusive contract.

A temporary injunction will be granted to restrain a city commissioner from awarding a contract without public competitive bidding, in violation of §51 of the

General Municipal Law and §419 of the Greater New 

York Charter. Shewan & Sons, Inc. v. Mills, 211 App. 

Div. 687, 208 N. Y. S. 381.

It is proper to grant an injunction to restrain defendant from conducting a quarrying and stone-crushing business in a restricted residential neighborhood. 

MacNeish v. Tuckahoe Stone Corp., 241 N. Y. 503, 150 

N. E. 530. 

Where defendant agreed in a contract of sale that he would not conduct the same business within five blocks of the old store, he can be restrained upon violation of such agreement. Bernfeld v. Freedenberg, 125 

Misc. 645, 211 N. Y. S. 692.

A person who sold the name and good-will, as well as his stock in trade and property, in a business bearing his name will be enjoined from engaging in the same business under his own name and from giving another person the license or authority to use such name in a competing business. Merritt Burial & Cremation Co. v. 

Merritt Co., 155 App. Div. 565, 140 N. Y. S. 895.

In labor disputes between employers and employees, the court will grant an injunction only when there is an attempt to effectuate an unlawful purpose or to effectuate a lawful purpose by unlawful means. Interborough Rapid Transit Co. v. Lavin, 247 N. Y. 65, 159 

N. E. 863. 

An injunction to restrain actors under contract with plaintiff from performing for any other management will not be granted unless the talents of such actors were so unusual as to make the loss of their services a matter of serious consequence to the plaintiff. Shu bert Theatrical Co. v. Gallagher, 200 App. Div. 596, 193 

N. Y. S. 401.

The publishing of a libel cannot be enjoined. Old 

Investors' & Traders' Corp. v. Jenkins, 133 Misc. 213, 232 N. Y. S. 245; Marlin Firearms Co. v. Shields, 171 

N. Y. 384, 64 N. E. 163. 

8274. Right Dependent on Extrinsic Facts. 

Where the right to an injunction order depends on extrinsic facts, the order may be granted:

(1) Where it appears that the defendant, during the pendency of the action, is doing or procuring or suffering to be done, or threatens, or is about to do, or to procure or suffer to be done, an act in violation of the plaintiff's rights respecting the subject of the action and tending to render the judgment ineffectual. (2) Where it appears that the defendant during the pendency of the action threatens or is about to remove or dispose of his property, with intent to defraud the plaintiff. 

(C. P. A. §§878; 981.) 

In general a person not a party to the action can not be restrained. The exceptions are attorneys, agents, servants and successors in office. Dixon v. Telerico, 217 

App. Div. 191, 217 N. Y. S. 482. $275. State Officers.

An injunction restraining a state officer or board may be granted only by the court on notice. (C. P. A. §879.) $276. Service.

An injunction order granted by the court must be served by delivering a certified copy thereof. Where it is granted by a judge, it must be served by showing the original order and delivering a copy. Copies of the papers upon which the order was granted must be delivered with the copy of the order. (C. P. A. §883.) §277. Vacating. 

A motion may be made to vacate an injunction order on the original papers or upon proof, or both. (C. P. A. §§897, 898.) $278. Warrant of Attachment.45

While arrest and injunction are proceedings in per- 4 For attachment before publication of the summons, see p. 95. 

5 For satisfaction of judgment out of attached property, see p. 243.

sonam, attachment is a proceeding in rem. Application may be made without notice. (C. P. A. §815.) $279. Right to Warrant.

In the following cases a warrant of attachment may be granted when the action is to recover a sum of money only as a tax or as damages: (1) A breach of contract, express or implied, other than a contract to marry. (2) A wrongful conversion of personal property. (3) An injury to person or property in consequence of negligence, fraud or other wrongful act. (4) A wrongful act, neglect or default by which the decedent's death was caused, where the cause of action arose in this state and the action is brought by an executor or administrator against a defendant who would have been liable to an action in favor of the decedent by reason thereof if death had not ensued. (C. P. A. §902; City Ct. 

Act §34; Mun. Ct. Code §§39, 40.) §280. Application for Warrant. 

The plaintiff must show that a cause of action specified above exists against the defendant, and, if the action is to recover damages for breach of contract, that the plaintiff is entitled to recover a stated sum over and above all counterclaims. He must also show that the defendant: (1) Is a foreign corporation or a nonresident. (2) If a natural person and a resident, has departed therefrom with intent to defraud his creditors or to avoid service of a summons, or keeps himself concealed therein with like intent. (3) If a natural person or a domestic corporation, has removed or is about to remove property from the state with intent to defraud creditors, or is about to, or has assigned,

disposed of or secreted, property with like intent, or if the defendant is a domestic corporation that no person can be found within the state after diligent effort, upon whom a summons can be served. (4) Has made, under his own hand and signature, or that of a duly authorized agent with his knowledge and acquiescence, a false statement in writing as to his financial responsibility or standing, for the purpose of procuring credit or the extension of credit. (5) If an adult and a resident of the state, has been continuously without the state for more than six months next before the granting of the order of publication of the summons against him and has not designated a person upon whom service may be made, or such designation no longer remains in force, or service cannot be made upon such person within the state after diligent effort. (C. P. A. §903; 

City Ct. Act §34; Mun. Ct. Code §40.) §281. Peculation and Deceit. 

A warrant of attachment may also be granted where the complaint demands judgment for a sum of money only in an action for peculation and deceit. (C. P. A. §904; City 

Ct. Act §34.) §282. Service of Summons. 

6 In any case where the warrant is granted before service of the summons, personal service of the summons must be made or service by publication must be commenced within 30 days after the granting of the warrant. (C. P. A. $905.)

In so far as §48 of the Municipal Court Code authorizes service of the summons on a foreign corporate defendant by delivering it to the person in possession of • See p. 95.

defendant's attached property, it is unconstitutional. 

Drew v. Northwestern Corporation, 133 Misc. 706, 233 

N. Y. S. 292; Nerenberg v. Keith, 101 Misc. 551, 167 

N. Y. S. 612. 

$283. Personal Property Subject to Attachment. 

The personal property subject to attachment includes: (1) Unpaid subscriptions to the defendant foreign corporation (if it is not a corporation created by federal law). (2) Stocks and bonds and the income and profits thereon. (3) A cause of action arising upon contract, including notes or bonds found within the county. (4) A right to or interest, present or future, in any property or estate of a deceased person, which could be legally assigned. (C. P. A. §§912; 914, 915, 916.)

Rights to profits which may be made in the future cannot be attached. There can be no attachment or levy upon a contingent right, which may or may not become a cause of action according to the occurrence or nonoccurrence of a future event. Frederick v. Chicago Bearing Metal Co., 221 App. Div. 588, 224 N. Y. S. 629.

A debt due a foreign corporation not doing business in the state from another foreign corporation doing business in the state cannot be attached by a resident. The situs of the debt is the domicile of the debtor, which is the state of its incorporation. Cohn v. Enterprise 

Distributing Corporation, 214 App. Div. 238, 212 Ñ. Y. 

S. 39. 

8284. Real Property Subject to Attachment. 

The real property which may be levied upon under this warrant includes any interest in real property, either vested or not vested, which is capable of being alienated by the defendant. (C. P. A. §913.) Real property and interests therein cannot be levied upon in a municipal court action. 

(Mun. Ct. Code §44.)

§285. Certificate of Defendant's Interest. 

Upon application by a sheriff holding a warrant of attachment, the president of an association or corporation must furnish a certificate of the defendant's interest therein. 

A person refusing to give such certificate, or giving one which appears untrue, may be ordered to submit to an examination under oath. The sheriff may maintain any action or special proceeding necessary to obtain possession of or to discover property of the attachment debtor. (C. P. 

A. §§918, 919; 922; Mun. Ct. Code §46.) 

Equitable assets and choses in action may be attached and the sheriff may maintain an action to compel transference of the judgment debtor's interest. Castriotis v. 

Guaranty Trust Co., 229 N. Y. 74, 127 N. E. 900. 

§286. Levy. 

Levy is made: (1) Upon personal property capable of manual delivery by taking the same into the sheriff's custody. (2) Upon other personal property by leaving a certified copy of the warrant and a notice showing the property attached, with the person holding same; or if it consists of a demand other than a bond or note, with the person against whom it exists, or the officer of the corporation or the executor, etc. (3) Upon property discovered in a sheriff's action, by entering in the proper clerk's office the judgment rendered and thereafter levying as usual. (4) Upon real property, by filing a notice of attachment with the clerk of the county where it is situated. (C. 

P. A. §917; Mun. Ct. Code §45.) §287. Inventory. 

Inventory must be made by the sheriff and two disinterested freeholders immediately after levy is made. (C. P.

A. §921.

) In municipal court actions the inventory is made by the marshal. (Mun. Ct. Code §43.) Perishable property may be sold by the sheriff on order of the court. (C. P. A. §923; City Ct. Act §25.) Exceptions exist as to attached goods in transit on a vessel. (C. P. A. §920.) 

§288. Motions. 

A motion to vacate the attachment, modify it, or to increase the security given by the plaintiff may be made by the defendant or a person having an interest in the at tached property. (C. P. A. §948; Mun. Ct. Code §§54; 56.) 

A defendant who has appeared in the action may apply for a discharge of the attachment on furnishing an under taking. (C. P. A. §§952, 953; Mun. Ct. Code §49.) If a third party claim the property attached by the sheriff, he may file notice with the sheriff, who may then impanel a jury to try the validity of the claim. (C. P. A. §§924-926; 

103.) In the municipal court such third party may obtain the property if the defendant has not reclaimed it, by filing a bond with the clerk. (Mun. Ct. Code §50.) $289. A Receiver.

A receiver of property which is the subject of an action in the supreme or county court may be appointed by the court before final judgment on the application of a party who establishes an apparent right to, or interest in, the property, where it is in the possession of an adverse party and there is danger that it will be removed beyond the jurisdiction of the court, or lost, materially injured, or de stroyed. (C. P. A. §974.) Notice of the application must be given to the adverse party, unless he has failed to appear in the action. (C. P. A. §975.) 

Ordinarily the court will not appoint a receiver pendente lite for a corporation unless the corporation is in- 

7 See p. 254 for receiver after judgment.

solvent or threatened with insolvency. Lichtenstadter v. Lichtenstadter Bros., 130 Misc. 772, 224 N. Y. S. 459.

A receiver of this kind requires no title but only the custody of the property as the representative of the court. 

He can take no step without an order from the court. 

A receiver pendente lite is a mere temporary officer of the court and does not possess the powers of a permanent receiver. His functions are limited to the care and preservation of the property committed to his charge, and he possesses no authority except such as the orders of the court confer. Decker v. Gardner, 124 

N. Y. 334, 26 N. E. 814. $290. Security.

Security must be furnished by the receiver. (C. P. A. §976.) 

§291. Replevin. 

Replevin is now called an action to recover a chattel. 

A person bringing the action must have the right to the possession of the chattel wrongfully taken or wrongfully detained. $292. Right to Maintain.

The action may not be maintained in the following cases: (1) Where the chattel was taken by virtue of a warrant against the plaintiff for the collection of a tax, assessment or fine issued in pursuance of a state or federal statute, unless the taking was, or the detention is, unlawful. (2) Where it was seized by virtue of an execution or warrant of attachment against the property of the plaintiff or of any person from or through whom the plaintiff has derived title since the seizure thereof, unless it was legally exempt from seizure or is unlawfully detained.

(3) Where it was seized by virtue of an execution or a warrant of attachment against the property of a person other than the plaintiff and at the time of the commencement of the action the plaintiff had not the right to reduce it to his possession. (C. P. A. §1089; Mun. Ct. Code §57.) §293. Pleading. 

Title is sufficiently pleaded by an allegation that the party pleading or a third person was the owner of the chattel or that it was his property at the time the action was commenced, unless the right of action or defense depends on a right of possession by virtue of a special property. In the latter case, the facts as to the special property must be pleaded so as to show right of possession at the time the action was commenced. (Rule 270.) If the title is stated sufficiently, a general allegation that the defendant wrongfully took the chattel is sufficient. When the action is based on wrongful detention only, the facts showing the wrongful detention must be set forth. (Rule 271.)

A complaint in proceedings for claim and delivery of personal property must show a right of property and of possession in plaintiff. An allegation of wrongful detention is not sufficient. And where the plaintiff's case depends upon a wrongful detention without a wrongful taking, an averment in the complaint of a demand and refusal is necessary. Scofield v. Whiteledge, 49 N. Y. 259.

In an action to replevin, if the complaint does not allege wrongful taking, it must set forth facts showing wrongful detention. It is sufficient to allege ownership and right to possession and, to show wrongful detention, a demand that the chattel be delivered and defendant's refusal to comply therewith. Wagman v. Raynor, 163 App. Div. 68, 148 N. Y. S. 471. $294. Time of Replevy.

The plaintiff may cause the sheriff to replevy the chattel when the summons is served, or any time afterward

and before the defendant answers; or, where the judgment is taken by default, before entry of the final judgment. (C. P. A. §1094.) The plaintiff may proceed in the action and recover the chattel or its value, although he has not required the sheriff to replevy it or the sheriff has not been able to do so. (C. P. A. §1116; Mun. Ct. Code §§58; 60.) "In commencing an action to recover a chattel, it is optional with the plaintiff to merely commence an action. 

He may couple it with an application for a writ of replevin, an incidental and concurrent remedy, and preliminary relief." Triangle Mint Corp. v. Horgan, 133 

Misc. 802, 803, 233 N. Y. S. 570, 573; Coler v. Judd, 124 

Misc. 22, 207 N. Y. S. 248. 

§295. Requisition and Undertaking. 

A requisition and an undertaking must be delivered to the sheriff and an affidavit stating facts showing: (1) Ownership or special right to possession. (2) Wrongful detention and cause thereof. (3) That the case does not fall within those in §1089 above. (4) The actual value of the chattel. 

The requisition must be directed to the sheriff of a particular county or generally to the sheriff of any county where the chattel is found. The undertaking must be executed by two sureties approved by the sheriff and the amount must be at least twice the value of the chattel. 

(C. P. A. §§1095, 1096; 1099; Mun. Ct. Code §§58, 59.) The affidavit may be made by the plaintiff's agent or attorney if the facts are within his personal knowledge, or if the plaintiff is not within the county where the attorney resides or has his office or is not capable of making the affi davit. (C. P. A. §1110.) 

The affidavit must set forth the alleged cause of detention of the property according to the best knowledge,

information and belief of the affiant. The failure of the affiant to allege a demand for the return of the property and refusal makes the affidavit void, and the omission cannot be supplied by amendment. Northwest Engineering Co. v. Rappl, 132 Misc. 497, 230 N. Y. S. 177. 

§296. Making the Levy. 

The sheriff must take the chattel into his possession and, if it is not delivered to him in pursuance of a demand, he may break into the building or enclosure and seize it. 

He must serve the defendant with copies of the requisition, affidavit and undertaking. (C. P. A. §1110.) Perishable goods may be sold at public auction on a court or judge's order. (C. P. A. §1101-a.) 

$297. Reclamation by Defendant.

The defendant may reclaim the replevied chattel, if he has not objected to the plaintiff's sureties. Within three days after the chattel is replevied, the defendant may serve a notice upon the sheriff that he requires the return of the chattel replevied. With the notice, he must deliver an affidavit and an undertaking similar to those of the plaintiff. (C. P. A. §1102, 1103; Mun. Ct. Code §63.) If the defendant reclaims and justifies his sureties, the sheriff must return the chattel to him. If the defendant does not reclaim, justify his sureties, or except to the sureties of the plaintiff, the sheriff must deliver the chattel to the plaintiff. (C. P. A. 

§1105; Mun. Ct. Code §67.) 

$298. Rights of a Third Person.

A third person, not a party to the action, who claims the chattel as against the defendant may serve the sheriff with an affidavit stating his right, any time before delivery is made to either party. The sheriff may serve the plaintiff's attorney with notice that he requires indemnity against such claim. If the indemnity is not furnished, the sheriff

PROVISIONal remedies and rEPLEVIN 

181 may deliver the chattel to the claimant. If the sheriff delivers the chattel to the plaintiff after receiving such notice from a third person, the latter may maintain an action against the sheriff. The summons must be issued within three months after delivery of the chattel and served within three months after issuance. (C. P. A. §§1107, 1108; Mun. 

Ct. Code §§67, 68.) 

$299. Warrant of Seizure.

In the municipal court, a writ of replevin cannot be used in an action to foreclose a lien on a chattel under a chattel mortgage or conditional sale contract where title is not vested in the person in possession. A warrant of seizure is issued by the court or a justice thereof upon application made on an undertaking and affidavits showing the lien and a description of the chattel. The warrant requires the marshal to seize the chattel and keep it until judgment is ren dered. (Mun. Ct. Code §§70, 71, 72.) 

"A warrant of seizure" cannot be used in a city court action to regain possession of a chattel sold under a conditional sale contract. Replevin must be used. 

Grossman v. Weiss, 129 Misc. 234, 221 N. Y. S. 266.

However, in an action to foreclose a chattel mortgage in the supreme court, county court or city court, a warrant of seizure or a receiver is the proper remedy. Marcus v. Sherr, 132 Misc. 734, 230 N. Y. S. 425.

CHAPTER VIII  $300. Preparing for Trial. Examinations. 

In addition to the motions discussed in Chapter VI, certain other steps may be taken to clarify the issues and to prepare for the trial of an action. These include examinations to take testimony, discovery and inspection, consolidating and severing causes of action, and subpoenaing witnesses and documents.

While preparing pleadings or collecting evidence to be produced on the trial, a party often finds he lacks information necessary to his cause. He is allowed to make certain examinations to enable him to obtain this information. Such examination is had by questioning the witness under oath and obtaining his answers. Ordinarily the questions and answers are written down and constitute a deposition. The practice in regard to the allowance of these examinations is not uniform throughout the various departments of the supreme court. Indeed, there is no subject of civil practice on which there is such a wide divergence of opinion. The main difficulty of the courts is to prevent such examinations from becoming "fishing expeditions." The court of appeals has stated a very liberal rule as to the allowance of examinations, but it refuses to determine the scope of a particular examination, because this question is one for the discretion of the court to which application is made. 

A party may, in the discretion of the judge, have a general examination of his adversary, and it is not as of course to be limited to an affirmative cause of action, or the affirmative defense set forth in favor of the party desiring the examination. Herbage v. City of 

Utica, 109 N. Y. 81, 16 N. E. 62. 182

PREPARATION FOR trial 

183 The court of appeals will not determine the limits to be placed on an examination before trial, since the matter rests in the discretion of the supreme court, and its decision, therefore, cannot be reviewed by the court of appeals. Middleton v. Boardman, 240 N. Y. 552, 148 

N. E. 701. 

§301. Before Commencing Action. 

An expected party, on order and affidavit, may take by deposition material and necessary testimony before commencing action. (C. P. A. §295; Rule 123.) It has been held that such examination may be had for the purpose of identifying the defendant. (Lauffer v. Eastern Star 

Temple, 210 App. Div. 619, 207 N. Y. S. 292, Fourth Dept.)

In the First Department, it has been held that such examination may be had only to perpetuate testimony.¹ (Matter of Hufstutler, 220 App. Div. 587, 222 N. Y. S. 43.) 

§302. To Frame the Complaint. 

In some cases an examination may be had, on order, to enable the plaintiff to frame his complaint. (C. P. A. §288; Rule 122.)

When the plaintiff has a cause of action against the defendant, but lacks information as to its character, he is entitled to examine the defendant, in order to enable him to frame his complaint. Teall v. Roeser, 206 

App. Div. 371, 201 N. Y. S. 280, Fourth Dept.; Heye v. 

American Chemical Education Co., 185 App. Div. 13, 172 N. Y. S. 622, First Dept., aff'd. 230 N. Y. 547, 130 

N. E. 886. 

§303. For Use on Motion. 

On one day's notice to the adversary's attorneys, a party may apply for an order to take the testimony before a referee of a person not a party, where the applicant intends to use such testimony on a motion. (C. P. A. §307; Rule 120.) 

1 See pp. 190, 191.

$304. Examination before Trial.
