On 5 days' notice, on stipulation or on order, any party to an action may cause to be taken by deposition before trial, his own testimony or that of an adverse party, which is material and necessary in the prosecution or defense of the action on matters set forth in the notice, stipulation or order. A motion may be made to vacate or modify the notice when this method is used. (C. P. A. §§288; 290, 291, 292; 298; Rule 124.) On order, an examination of books and papers also may be had. (C. P. A. §296; Rule 121.)

The provisions apply to special proceedings as well as to actions. (C. P. A. §308.) 

The statute does not authorize an examination for the purpose of obtaining information which could be had in a bill of particulars. Sands v. Comerford, 211 

App. Div. 406, 207 N. Y. S. 398; Cameron v. Rochester & Syracuse R. Co., Inc., 125 Misc. 140, 210 N. Y. S. 241.

When the taking of a deposition of a party is only on the ground that it is material and necessary on the trial, it is not permitted until after issue has been joined; because, until he has answered the defendant is not an adverse party. Diefendorf v. Fenn, 125 App. Div. 

651, 110 N. Y. S. 68; Mackay, Lovell & Co. v. Dillon, 215 

App. Div. 842, 213 N. Y. S. 681. See also St. John v. 

Putnam, 128 Misc. 707, 220 N. Y. S. 146.

An order may require an examination before trial and the production of books and papers at the same time or they may be had separately. Fey v. Wisser, 206 

App. Div. 520, 202 N. Y. S. 30. §305. Original Owner.

A party may cause to be taken the testimony, which is material and necessary, of the original owner of a claim which constitutes, or from which arose, a cause of action acquired by the adverse party by grant, conveyance, transfer, assignment or endorsement and which is set forth 2 See p. 204.

in his pleading as a cause of action or counterclaim. (C. P.

A. §288.) 

When the adverse party or original owner is a corporation, joint stock association or other unincorporated association, the testimony, specified in C. P. A. §288, of one or more of its officers, directors, managing agents or employees, may be taken. (C. P. A. §289.) There is some conflict as to the interpretation of the words "agents or employees." 

The person to be examined under this section must in some degree be a representative or manager of the corporation. Friedman v. N. Y. Central R. R. Co., 206 

App. Div. 169, 200 N. Y. S. 337.

Motormen and conductors are managing agents within the purview of $289. Enequist v. B'klyn City R. Co., 216 App. Div. 730, 214 N. Y. S. 450. 

An elevator operator is an "agent" or "employee" under $289. Bergman v. Edbro Realty Co., 135 Misc. 

87, 236 N. Y. S. 409. 

§306. Testimony of a Prisoner. 

The testimony of a person imprisoned under a sentence for felony may be taken only on order and in the discretion of the court. (C. P. A. §297.) §307. Right to Examination. 

The decisions governing allowance of examination vary in the different departments. 

The general rule in the Second Department is that the applicant may have the examination to prove his own case only. The exceptions cannot be classified, but they include (1) litigations presenting a fiduciary relation between the parties and when the facts, therefore, are peculiarly within the knowledge of the adverse party; (2) litigations presenting the relation of principal and agent, or the like, and the facts peculiarly within the knowledge of the adverse party; (3) litigations in which a defense, unanswered and established, would destroy

the plaintiff's cause of action, but in such cases the examination is limited to avoidance and not extended to disclosure. Oshinsky v. Gumberg, 188 App. Div. 23, 176 

N. Y. S. 406, Second Dept.

It must be shown that the testimony of such witness is material and necessary for the prosecution or defense of the action. Goldmark v. U. S. Electro-Gal vanizing Co., 111 App. Div. 526, 97 N. Y. S. 1078, First Dept. 

§308. Examination in Certain Actions. 

Such examinations have been had in contract, accounting, divorce, libel, and negligence actions. 

Where the plaintiff cannot bring a regular action for accounting, but must bring an action at law, and the defendant is the only party having records, the plaintiff is allowed to have the examination. Oppenheimer v. 

Van Raalte, 151 App. Div. 601, 136 N. Y. S. 197, First Dept.

Where an examination involves an accounting it will not be allowed in the Second Department. Pome rantz v. Naomi Realty Corporation, 228 App. Div. 837, 

240 N. Y. S. 480. "This court has frequently had before it the question of examination before trial in negligence cases, and in certain other action for torts, and has consistently held that, except in unusual circumstances, as a matter of public policy, in the exercise of its judicial discretion, a general examination before trial would not be allowed. We have allowed specific or limited examinations where ownership or control has been denied." 

Shaw v. Samley Realty Co., Inc., 201 App. Div. 433, 435, 436, 194 N. Y. S. 531, 532, 533, First Dept. ...

The testimony sought to be elicited must be necessary for use of the party seeking it in establishing some fact which he must affirmatively prove. The plaintiff is entitled to examine the defendant as to notice of the defective condition of a stairway in a case brought for injuries caused thereby. Kessler v. North River 

Realty Co., 169 App. Div. 814, 155 N. Y. S. 799, First Dept.

The Second Department does not agree with the ruling in Shaw v. Samley Realty Co. (supra), in the limitation in negligence actions. However, the examination in this case was limited to adducing evidence on behalf of the plaintiff to enable him to sustain certain of the allegations in his complaint. Samols v. Mayer, 120 

Misc. 516, 199 N. Y. S. 754.

In the Fourth Department, the plaintiff in a negligence action was permitted to examine the defendant as to his previous experience in driving automobiles, his authority to drive an automobile in Connecticut, the facts regarding the time of the accident, the manner in which it occurred, the speed at which the defendant was driving, the character of the road and defendant's familiarity therewith, the cause and reasons for the accident, and as to all facts and circumstances producing it. National Fire Ins. Co. v. Shearman, 209 App. Div. 538, 204 N. Y. S. 673.

As a matter of public policy, an examination before trial will not be permitted in an action for alienation of affections, criminal conversation, seduction or breach of promise of marriage. Gullette v. Field, 96 Misc. 81, 159 N. Y. S. 646. 

§309. Before Whom Testimony Is Taken. 

The testimony is taken before any judicial officer, notary public, commissioner of deeds, or attorney. The person before whom testimony is taken must not be attorney or counsel for a party thereto, unless the parties mutually agree. It is taken orally unless the parties otherwise stipulate. The officer must take down or cause to be taken down every question and answer, unless the parties consent or an order directs that only the substance of the testimony be inserted. The examination is conducted in the same manner as on a trial, except that an objection need not be noted and may be raised on the trial, unless it is an objection to form. When complete, the testimony must be read to the person examined and subscribed by him. If a witness refuses to answer a question in an examination before a person who is not a judge, the refusal may be

reported to the court or judge, who shall determine if the witness should answer. (C. P. A. §§301, 302; Rule 129.) 

§310. Attendance. 

If the examination is to be had on notice, a person may be compelled to attend and testify by a subpoena; but a resident can be compelled to attend only in a county in which he resides or has a place of business; and a nonresident only in the county where the subpoena was served. 

(C. P. A. §§299, 300.) The penalty for failure to obey is punishment for contempt.3

The court may not strike out the answer because of defendant's failure to obey a notice for examination before trial served on his attorney. Levine v. Moskowitz, 206 App. Div. 194, 200 N. Y. S. 597; Roseberg Holding 

Co., Inc., v. Berman, 214 App. Div. 146, 211 N. Y. S. 900; 

Ungar v. Payne, 217 App. Div. 768, 216 N. Y. S. 929. 

§311. Notice. 

The notice of taking testimony by deposition which must be given the adversary or his attorney must specify: (1) The person before whom the testimony is to be taken. (2) The time and place. (3) The names of the persons to be examined. (4) The matters upon which such persons are to be examined. (C. P. A. §290.) 

The examination is limited to the particulars mentioned in the notice. Sands v. Comerford, 211 App. Div. 

406, 207 N. Y. S. 398. 

§312. Examination on Order. 

An order to take testimony by deposition may be obtained in the first instance, on affidavits showing right to 

3 See pp. 215-217.

examine and on notice to the other parties. The order must be obtained when the examination is held to enable the plaintiff to frame his complaint or before action is commenced. (C. P. A. §§292; 294, 295; Rule 122.) §313. Testimony Taken without the State.

The foregoing provisions apply to the taking of testimony within the state for use within the state. On order, testimony can be taken outside the state for use here. 

The deponent must take an oath or affirmation before a proper officer and the testimony must be on written inter rogatories if the court so ordered. (C. P. A. §302.) The court may order the issuance of a commission. (C. P. A. $294.) Rule 130 outlines special provisions which must be observed when depositions are taken without the state.

An order for the suppression of such deposition may be obtained if it was improperly taken or returned. (Rule 133.) 

§314. Filing. 

Within 10 days all depositions and required papers must be filed in the office of the clerk of the court. (Rules 125; 

131, 132.) 

§315. Letters Rogatory. 

If it appears that the ends of justice will be better promoted by the issuance of letters rogatory than by the granting of an order to take testimony, such letters may be issued by the court. (C. P. A. §309.)

When a witness is in a foreign country, beyond the jurisdiction of the court, a commission would have no force or effect, except by permission of the foreign government; therefore letters rogatory are directed to a judicial tribunal or officer of a foreign country with a request to summon the witness and take the testimony. 

In such a case the matter must be referred to the judicial tribunals of that country to proceed, if they will, as a matter of courtesy. Anonymous, 59 N. Y. 313.

§316. Physical Examinations. 

A physical examination of the plaintiff by a physician designated by the court and the deposition of the plaintiff may be had on order obtained on notice in a personal injury action, if the defendant presents to the court satisfactory evidence that he is ignorant of the nature and extent of the injuries complained of. If the plaintiff be a female, she is entitled to an examination by a physician of her own sex. 

(C. P. A. §306; Mun. Ct. Code §117.) 

In the Third Department it has been held that the court may, in a proper case, order an x-ray photograph to be taken as a part of the physical examination. Hollister v. Robertson, 208 App. Div. 449, 203 N. Y. S. 514.

In the Second Department it has been held that §306 does not authorize the taking of an x-ray photograph. 

Laqua v. General Linen Supply & Laundry Co., 227 App. 

Div 794, 237 N. Y. S. 197; Van Orden v. Madow, 207 

App. Div. 827.

A sample of the plaintiff's blood may be taken, if permission is granted by the court. Hayt v. Brewster, Gordon & Co., Inc., 199 App. Div. 68, 191 N. Y. S. 176,

Fourth Dept. 

§317. Depositions to Perpetuate Testimony. 

Any party to an action may cause to be taken, on notice or order, material and necessary testimony of any person who: (1) Is about to depart from the state. (2) Resides more than 100 miles from the place of trial. (3) Is so sick or infirm as to afford reasonable grounds of belief that he will be unable to attend the trial. 

(4) Or other special circumstances. (C. P. A. §§288; 

290; 292; Rule 123.) Rule 138 specifies the contents of the petition.

An order for examining a witness before trial on the ground that the witness is so sick that he will not be able to attend the trial, will be vacated where the only

allegation as to the materiality of the witness is contained in the affidavit of the moving party who states what he expects to prove by the testimony of the witness, but does not disclose any circumstances tending to show that he has any reasonable ground for his expectation, and also where the moving papers simply show the serious illness of the witness many months prior to the making of the motion, and fails to show its continuance. Johnson v. New Home Sewing Mach. Co., 62 App. Div. 157, 70 N. Y. S. 875. $318. In Regard to Real Property.

Where a person has been or he and those under whom he claims have been, for one year in possession of real property or of an undivided interest therein, claiming it in fee, or for life, or for a term of years not less than ten, he may apply to the supreme court by petition, or notice, to take the deposition and perpetuate the testimony of any person. Such testimony is taken before a referee designated by the court. Before such evidence will be received on the trial or special proceeding, the court must be satisfied that the deponent is deceased, or is unable personally to attend by reason of insanity, sickness or other infirmity, or is confined in a prison or jail, or is absent from the state, and his attendance cannot be compelled by subpoena or his testimony taken by commission. (C. P. A. §§313; 316, 317320.)

The pendency of an action or intention to commence one is not a necessary prerequisite to the taking of a deposition for the perpetuation of testimony respecting title to property under these sections. Matter of Durey, 223 App. Div. 70, 227 N. Y. S. 580, aff'd. 248 N. Y. 

538, 162 N. E. 516. 

§319. In the City and Municipal Courts. 

All provisions for taking depositions in the supreme court apply to the city court. (City Ct. Act §31.) In the municipal court, a party or a person who expects to be a party may cause to be taken his own deposition or that

of any other party in the same manner as in the supreme court. (Mun. Ct. Code §§27; 110; 112; 114.) A commission may be issued to take the testimony of a witness who is without the city of New York. (Mun. Ct. Code §§101-106.) 

On order and 2 days' notice, a deposition to perpetuate testimony may be taken. (Mun. Ct. Code §§107-114.) §320. Discovery and Inspection. 

On order, the court may compel a party to produce and discover, or to give to the other party, an inspection and copy, or permission to take a copy or photograph of a book, document, or other paper, or to make discovery of any article or property in his possession or under his control, relating to the merits of the action or the defense. 

Punishment for refusal to comply may be suspended by paying applicant's expenses and submitting. Upon the final hearing of an application to punish, the court may direct that the complaint or answer of the party in default be stricken out and judgment be rendered accordingly; or that portions of the pleadings be stricken out; or that he be debarred from maintaining a claim or defense in relation to which the discovery was sought. Such books, documents, etc., may be excluded from evidence; or the party may be punished for contempt, or both. (C. P. A. §§324, 325; Rules 

140, 141, 142.) On order, a party may be compelled to state his knowledge of the whereabouts of specified documents. 

(C. P. A. §328.) 

The power to punish for failure to comply with an order for inspection is limited by the presumption which attaches to the suppression of the evidence withheld. 

In addition, the penalty may be inflicted only on the adverse party who has disobeyed the order. Feingold v. Walworth Bros., Inc., 238 N. Y. 446, 144 N. E. 675. 

§321. Notice to Produce. 

Such inspection may also be had on notice. Failure to comply may be punished by excluding the suppressed evi-

dence on the trial, unless good excuse is shown; or the moving party may introduce secondary evidence. (C. P. A. §327; Mather v. Eureka Mower Co., 118 N. Y. 629, 23 N. E. 993.) 

An aggrieved party may move for vacation, modification or regulation of the notice to produce. Wile v. 

Nassau Smelting and Refining Works, Ltd., 205 App. 

Div. 657, 200 N. Y. S. 154. 

§322. Admission of Facts. 

Any party, by written notice given not later than 10 days before the trial, may call on any other party to admit, for the purpose of the cause, matter or issue only, any specific fact or facts mentioned in such notice. If compliance is not had within 6 days, the person neglecting or refusing may be compelled to pay the expenses incurred in proving such fact or facts, in the discretion of the court. (C. P. A. §323.) 

§323. Admission of Genuineness of Paper. 

At any time before trial, an attorney may exhibit to his opponent a paper material to the action and request a written admission of its genuineness. If the admission is not given 4 days after request, in the discretion of the court, the party refusing may be compelled to pay the expenses incurred in proving the genuineness of the paper. (C. P. A. $322.) 

§324. Genuineness of Signature. 

In the municipal court a signature to a written instrument which is pleaded is admitted as genuine unless, within 8 days after joinder of issue the party sought to be charged files with the clerk a denial and demand that it be proved. 

The denial may be included in the answer. (Mun. Ct. 

Code §92.)

§325. Notice of Trial. 

An action is brought to trial by the service of a notice of trial upon the opposing party and the filing of a note of issue with the clerk of the court. A notice of trial in the supreme court may be served at any time after joinder of issue and at least 14 days before the commencement of the term. The note of issue, stating the title of the action, the names of the attorneys, the time when the last pleading was served, the nature of the issue, whether trial is to be had with or without a jury, and the nature and object of the action, must be filed with the calendar clerk at least 12 days before the commencement of the term. The cause is then put on the calendar of the court which is ready 5 days before the opening day of the term. (Rules 150, 151.)

The amendment of the answer destroys the notice of trial, and a new notice must be given. Coler v. Lamb, 19 App. Div. 236, 46 N. Y. S. 117. 

§326. Service by Either Party. 

Usually the plaintiff serves the notice of trial. However, the defendant may serve a cross-notice and then he is in a position to have the complaint dismissed if the plaintiff default on the trial. (C. P. A. §433.) 

§327. In the City and Municipal Courts. 

A notice of trial in the city court, except in marine cases, may be given for any day of any term. A note of issue must be filed at least 3 days before the day set for trial. The time for personal service of a notice of trial is not less than 

5 days. (City Ct. Act §§29, 30.) In the municipal court, either party, when both parties appear by attorney, may serve a notice on the other fixing the date of trial not less than 5 days nor more than 8 days after service of such notice. The notice and proof of service must be filed with the clerk, who then places the case on the calendar. When

either party appears in person, the notice is given by the clerk, on application of either party. (Mun. Ct. Code §95.) 

§328. Waiver of Trial by Jury. 

4 A jury trial is waived in actions in the supreme court in the counties of New York City and in the city court where the complaint demands judgment for a sum of money only or to recover chattels, by failing to serve, with the notice of trial, a written notice demanding trial by jury and failing to file a copy of the latter notice with the county clerk (court clerk in city court), within 10 days after service thereof. If the plaintiff fails to serve and file such notice, any party may, within 20 days (10 in the city court) after the service of the notice of trial on him, or with his own notice of trial, serve upon the attorneys for all other parties a notice demanding a jury trial and file a copy with the county clerk (court clerk in city court) within 10 days after service. The party first demanding a jury trial must pay the fee therefor. (C. P. A. §426, subd. 5; City Ct. 

Act §16-a; Rule 157.) In the municipal court of the city of 

New York, the demand for a jury trial must be made not later than 3 days after issue is joined, otherwise a trial by jury is waived unless ordered by the court. The demand may be made in writing and filed with the clerk, or orally and endorsed on the summons. Either party may make the demand, and must, at the time of making the demand, pay the clerk six dollars. (Mun. Ct. Code §§118; 173.) 

§329. Consolidation and Severance. 

Actions may be consolidated or severed if a substantial right is not prejudiced thereby. It often happens that actions which could not be joined in one complaint may be consolidated and tried together. If the same plaintiff is suing several defendants in separate actions for sim- 

4 See also p. 205.

ilar libels, the defendants may apply for a consolidation of the actions. Although a certain counterclaim may not be interposed in a given action, the defendant may sometimes succeed in having the matters in the complaint and the matters in the counterclaim tried together by bringing a separate action on the cause of action in the proposed counterclaim and then obtaining an order consolidating the two actions. Thus it is not permissible to counterclaim annulment in an action for separation, and yet, if two separate actions are brought, they may be tried together after consolidation. The supreme court may order the removal of an action pending in another court and its consolidation with an action already pending in the supreme court. (C. P. A. §§96, 97, 97-a.) The city court may remove a case pending in the municipal court and consolidate it with an action arising out of the same transaction which is pending in the city court. (City Ct. Act §18-a. In effect Sept. 1, 1930.)

In the municipal court, the plaintiff may join all his causes of action in one complaint, but, if the court determines that the causes so joined should not be tried or disposed of together, it may order a severance. (Mun. Ct. Code §79.) 

Where common questions of law and fact are involved and it can be done without prejudice to a substantial right, consolidation under §96 will be granted. 

Lamborn v. Czarnikow-Pionda Co., 227 App. Div. 72, 237 N. Y. S. 69.

Consolidation under §96 depends on whether or not a substantial right will be prejudiced by the joinder; the fact that §209, relating to joinder of parties, or §258, relating to joinder of causes of action is not satisfied, may be considered but is not conclusive on the question. Crandall v. A. B. Leach & Co., 222 App. Div. 

292, 225 N. Y. S. 649.

Section 96 applies only when the complaint contains more than one cause of action, and does not apply to the severance and separate trial of one of several issues constituting a single cause of action upon which only one judgment can be entered. However, under §443,

subd. 3, a single issue or certain issues may be separated from the others and tried first. Such severance is granted when the determination of one or more issues in a particular way would end the entire litigation. City of N. Y. v. Interborough Rapid Transit Co., 134 Misc. 827, 236 N. Y. S. 449.

Consolidation of actions pending in different districts of the municipal court may be had. Melker v. 

Guarino, 135 Misc. 548, 238 N. Y. S. 569 (App. Term,

Second Dept.). Contra. Levey Co. v. Fox, 121 Misc. 

113, 200 N. Y. S. 274 (App. Term, First Dept.). 

§330. Tender and Offer of Judgment. 

Before trial, the defendant in an action where the complaint demands judgment for a sum of money only, may tender the plaintiff a sum which he believes adequate to satisfy the claim. To avail the defendant, the money must be accepted or paid into court. If the plaintiff does not accept, and his judgment exclusive of costs does not exceed the tender, he is not entitled to costs but must pay defendant costs. (C. P. A. §§171-173; 179.) A similar tender may be made on a counterclaim. (C. P. A. §§174; 179.)

Unless the defendant gives the statutory notice required by C. P. A. §§171-173, he is not entitled to dismissal; and the plaintiff may treat the amount deposited with the city chamberlain as common law tender, which has not been affected by statutes, and sue for the balance. Stadler & Stadler v. Long, 241 N. Y. S. 637. 

In an action to recover damages for breach of contract, the defendant's attorney may serve, with the answer, a written offer that if the defendant fails in his defense, the damages may be assessed at a specified sum. If the plaintiff serves notice before or with the notice of trial that he accepts, and damages are awarded to him on the trial, they must be assessed accordingly. If the plaintiff does not accept the offer, he cannot prove it on the trial. If the amount awarded the plaintiff does not exceed the offer, the defend-

ant is entitled to recover expenses necessarily incurred by him in preparing for the trial of the question of damages, the amount to be determined by the court. (C. P. A. §§175, 176.) 

§331. Offer of Judgment. 

Before trial the defendant may serve upon the plaintiff's attorney a written offer to allow judgment to be taken against him for a sum, or property, or to a specified affect. 

Within 10 days, the plaintiff may accept by written notice and file the summons, complaint, offer and acceptance. The clerk must then enter judgment. If the notice of acceptance is not given, the offer cannot be given in evidence on the trial; but if the plaintiff fail to obtain a more favorable judgment, he cannot recover costs from the time of the offer, but must pay costs from that time. (C. P. A. §177.)

In the municipal court the offer is filed or a deposit made with the clerk. (Mun. Ct. Code §81.) When the defendant sets up a counterclaim to an amount greater than the plaintiff's claim, or sufficient to reduce the plaintiff's recovery below $50.00, the plaintiff may make a similar offer with like effect. (C. P. A. §178.) (See also C. P. A. §§171-176.) §332. Preferences on the Calendar. 

Certain causes need not wait their turn on the calendar, but may secure an earlier trial on notice. The order of the preferences and the method of obtaining one are given in the Civil Practice Act, §§138, 139 and 141; City Court Rules, III; and Municipal Court Rules, IV. 

§333. Compelling Attendance of Witnesses and the Produc tion of Documents.

Probably the last thing an attorney does before trial is to make certain that the witnesses, by whose testimony he expects to win his case, will be present at the trial. If he

is not sure the witness will appear voluntarily, he compels the witness to attend by serving him with a subpoena. A subpoena is a process in the name of the court, referee, etc., requiring the attendance of the person named therein, if found within the jurisdiction of the court, to testify in a cause pending in that court where proof or the production of books, papers or other documents is required. (C. P. A. 

§403; Mun. Ct. Code §97.) §334. Service of Subpoenas. 

A subpoena is served by exhibiting the original and delivering a copy or a ticket containing its substance, and paying or tendering him the fees allowed by law for traveling to and returning from the place of trial (8¢ per mile if farther than 3 miles) and one day's attendance (50¢). No mileage fee is necessary in a municipal court case. (C. P. A. §§404; 1539; Mun. Ct. Code §98.) A witness who fails to appear may be punished for contempt and is liable for all damages sustained by reason of his nonappearance and $50.00 in addition thereto. If he is a party to the action, the court may also strike out his pleadings. (C. P. A. §405; 

Jud. Law §§753; 772; Mun. Ct. Code §§98-100.) §335. A Prisoner as a Witness. 

An order is necessary to obtain the presence of a prisoner as a witness. (C. P. A. §§415-420; Rule 163.) 

§336. A Subpoena Duces Tecum. 

A subpoena duces tecum is the name given to the process to compel the production of books, documents, etc. Five days' notice must be given in order to obtain the produc tion of a book of account. (C. P. A. §411.) Original hospital records need not be produced except on court order; but copies certified by the superintendent of the hospital or his 

See Chapter III.

assistant may be obtained under a subpoena duces tecum on 24 hours' notice. (C. P. A. §412.) A court order is necessary to procure books, documents or records from a library, public officer or public department and one day's notice must be given the person served and the adversary or his attorney. (C. P. A. §410; Rule 162.) In order to compel the attendance of a particular public officer or an officer of a corporation, in addition to the production of his records, an ordinary subpoena must be served on him as well as a subpoena duces tecum.

CHAPTER IX TRIAL §337. Calling the Calendar. 

Court is opened by the call of the calendar. The judge or clerk calls the titles of the actions listed for trial that day. Both parties, by their attorneys (usually the clerks of the attorneys), answer "ready" when their case is called, if they are ready to proceed to trial. 

§338. Answering the Calendar. 

The attorneys may answer "settled" or "settled and discontinued" when their case is called. This means the parties have agreed to settle their differences outside of court and without a trial. Such a settlement is usually a compromise agreement, and is perfectly valid and binding. (C. P. A. §476; Rule 4.) An infant's case may not be settled nor compromised, except on order of the court.

Where a settlement was made by authorized persons and the plaintiff, relying thereon, discontinued the action, the settlement will be upheld even though it is oral. Lloyd v. R. S. M. Corp., 225 App. Div. 85, 232 

N. Y. S. 290. 

Where the parties orally agreed, before a judge in chambers, upon a sum in settlement and that if defendant defaulted thereon judgment should be entered for the full amount claimed, and the case then being tried before the same judge was marked "settled" as a result of such agreement, the settlement will be upheld, and, under §476, the plaintiff may enter judgment for the full amount. Gass v. Arons, 131 Misc. 502, 227 

N. Y. S. 282.

A contract of settlement, if valid in itself, is final, and must be sustained by the court without regard to the validity of the original claim. Therefore when the parties, after entering into a settlement agreement, 201

marked the case "settled and discontinued" on the call of the calendar, a subsequent motion to set aside the settlement agreement will be denied. However, it might be that in an independent suit the settlement could be upset for reasons that would invalidate a contract, such as fraud or over-reaching. Yonkers Fur Dressing Co. v. Royal Ins. Co., 247 N. Y. 435, 160 N. E. 778. "The only source of power, for the guardian ad litem, is in the Court. The appointment is made not by the infant but by the Court, or a judge thereof. He is appointed to prosecute the action, and he may employ the ordinary and customary means to bring it, if possible, to a successful termination. The authority conferred upon him is to prosecute, not to settle, to obtain for the infant an adjudication as to his rights-not to barter away those rights in such a manner as the guardian may choose. Certainly no such settlement could be binding on the infant, except if made with the express sanction of the court." Edsall v. Vandemark, 39 Barb. 589. 

8339. Adjournments by Consent. 

If the attorneys have agreed to postpone the trial, one of them will answer by giving a date and then saying "by consent." This means both sides agree to adjourn the trial until the date mentioned. Adjournment is usually granted under these conditions. 

§340. Adjournment by Permission. 

One side may be ready and the other not. The one desiring the adjournment applies for it by presenting to the court affidavits to the effect: (1) That the attorney who was to have tried the case is actually engaged in the trial or argument of a case in another branch of the same court or a higher court. (2) That the attorney is a member of the state legislature, which is in session. (3) That the attorney is ill. (4) That a party or witness is too ill to attend and testify.

TRIAL 

203 (5) Other proper excuse.

The judge then examines the application and grants the adjournment or orders both parties to be ready for trial. (See Rules of the various courts; C. P. A. §433-a; Mun.

Ct. Code §96.) 

8341. When Only the Defendant Appears.

If only the defendant appears and he has served a notice of trial, he may have the complaint dismissed by moving for dismissal when the case is called. (C. P. A. §433.) See next case. 

§342. When Only the Plaintiff Appears. 

If only the plaintiff appears and he has served a notice of trial, he may take judgment by default.¹ (C. P. A. §433.) "With due regard to the zeal which an attorney should properly display in his client's behalf, and quite irrespective of what he believes to be the merits of the pending litigation, ordinary professional courtesy demands that he should not ask for judgment by default against his opponent unless he is satisfied, after communicating with him, that the default is willful or intentional. In any event the court should not countenance the taking of so-called snap defaults. With the large volume of pending litigation, mistakes in answering calendars are bound to occur, due sometimes to our unscientific method of numbering cases on the calendar and more often to errors on the part of office subordinates. "So-called snap defaults accomplish no useful purpose whatever; they often do a great deal of harm. 

Attorneys are injured in their relations with clients, litigants become resentful, and justly so, when without warning they find judgments entered against them by default; the time of the court is taken up with motions to open the defaults; additional clerical work is involved, and, after all is said and done, the only effect is delay, because practically all of these cases of unintentional defaults find their way back to the calendar 

1 See p. 231.

sooner or later on some terms. A lawsuit is something more than a game which is to be won by the player who is shrewder and more observant of the rules." 

Marcus v. Simotone & Combined Sound & Color Films, 

Inc., 135 Misc. 228, 230, 237 N. Y. S. 509, 510. 

§343. Disposition after Call of the Calendar. 

When the calendar has been called, the court holds as many "ready" cases as he thinks can be tried that day, and puts the others over to the following day. A reserve calendar is sometimes published from which cases are added to the day calendar from time to time. 

If more than one trial part is sitting, the judge who has presided at the call of the calendar sends cases to the other parts for trial. 

§344. The Issues. 

An issue of fact in a case arises upon an allegation of fact by one side and its denial by the other. If no facts are in dispute but the parties differ as to the law, there is only an issue of law. The issues of fact are tried by: a judge and jury, a judge, or a referee. Where a defendant demands an affirmative judgment on a counterclaim against the plaintiff, an issue of fact thereon is tried as if the defendant were a plaintiff, and the counterclaim a complaint. Issues of law are determined by the court. (C. P. A. §§421-432.) §345. Qualifications of the Judge. 

A judge is disqualified from hearing a case in which he is a party, in which he has been attorney or counsel, or in which he is interested; or if he is related by consanguinity or affinity to any party within the sixth degree. (Jud. L. §15.) 

§346. Jury and Non-Jury Cases. 

In the supreme and city courts cases to be tried before a judge and jury are heard at trial term; and cases to be

TRIAL 

205 tried before a judge alone are heard at special term. (Rule 

158; City. Ct. Act §5.) In the municipal court, the terms are called jury or non-jury terms. (Mun. Ct. Code §121.) 

§347. Trial by Jury.2 The New York Constitution, Art. I, §2, provides for trial by jury "in all cases in which it has been heretofore used.” 

These cases are the ones which were triable by jury at common law. The statutes enumerate certain cases in which an issue of fact must be tried by a jury (unless the same is waived): (1) Where the complaint demands judgment for a sum of money only. (2) An action of ejectment; action for dower; for waste; for a nuisance (abatement and damages, but not where an injunction is requested); or to recover a chattel. 

(C. P. A. §425.) 

A trial by jury may be had on application of a party, or direction of the court: (1) In an action for partition. (2) In an action to annul a marriage, unless it is based on physical incapacity. (3) On the issue of adultery in an action for divorce. (4) Other issues of fact in the discretion of the court. (C. P. A. §§1023; 1142; 1149; 427; 430.) 

§348. Waiver of Trial by Jury. 

A jury trial may be waived in any civil case. (Const.

Art. I, §2.) A party may waive his rights to a trial by jury: 

(1) By failing to appear at the trial. (2) By filing with the clerk a written waiver signed by the attorney for the party. 

2 For trial of issues by jury in Special Proceedings, see Chapter XIV.

(3) By an oral consent in open court entered in the minutes. (4) By moving the trial of the action, without a jury, or neglecting to object when the opposing side so moves. (5) (Applicable only to the supreme court in the counties of New York City and to the city court) actions for money only or to recover chattels, by failing to serve with his notice of trial a written notice demanding trial by jury and failing to file a copy with the county clerk (court clerk in city court) within 10 days.³ (C. P. A. §426.) (6) By failing to make a motion therefor within 20 days after issue joined, in a case where a jury trial is not given as a matter of right. (Rule 157; City Ct. Act §16-a.) 

The fee must be paid at the time of demand. (C. P. A. 

§1557-a; City Ct. Act §16-a.) 

An action for nuisance in which equitable relief is demanded as well as an equitable action to restrain a nuisance, the plaintiff waives the right of trial by jury and submits to the course of trial in equity cases. Cogswell v. N. Y., N. H. & H. R. R. Co., 105 N. Y. 319, 11 

N. E. 518.

When an action by a lessee for an injunction to restrain a nuisance, to which has been joined, to avoid multiplication of suits, a legal claim for damages, is, by the expiration of the lease and the vacation of the premises prior to the trial, shorn of all its equitable features, leaving nothing but the claim for damages, the defendant is entitled to a trial by jury, unless it has been waived. McNulty v. Mt. Morris Elec. Light 

Co., 172 N. Y. 410, 65 N. E. 196. But an action for a perpetual injunction to restrain the defendant from using its property in such a way as to continue a nuisance should be tried as an action in equity and only specific issues may be sent to a jury. Miller v. Edison 

Elec. Illuminating Co., 78 App. Div. 390, 80 N. Y. S. 319.

In an action to foreclose a mortgage, the answer admitted the allegations of the complaint and set up 

3 See p. 195.

TRIAL 

207 new matter in the way of a counterclaim, and demanded a judgment for a sum alleged to be due and damages for breach of contract. Plaintiff served a reply. Both parties noticed the issues for trial, and when it was reached defendant demanded a trial by jury, which was denied. By noticing the cause for trial in the equity side of the court, defendant waived his right to trial by jury. Mackellar v. Rogers, 109 N. Y. 468, 17 N. E. 350.

A plaintiff who seeks legal and equitable relief in respect of the same wrong thereby waives his right to trial by jury. But he cannot by so doing deprive defendant of his right to a jury trial. DiMenna v. Cooper & Evans Co., 220 N. Y. 391, 115 N. E. 993.

In a proceeding to have premises on which intoxicating liquor is alleged to have been manufactured declared a nuisance and closed for one year, owner of premises is not entitled to a jury trial, as the proceeding is in rem and not in personam. In re Love, 205 App. 

Div. 363, 199 N. Y. S. 530.

A jury trial may be waived by not objecting to an order of reference. Dotsko v. Szymaniak, 133 Misc. 657, 233 N. Y. S. 167.

The statute specifies ways in which a jury trial may be waived, but the provisions are not exclusive, and the same effect may be given to conduct or acquiescence by a party, which in other cases would require a conclusion that a right designed for his benefit had been waived. Mackellar v. Rogers, 109 N. Y. 468, 17 N. E. 350. 

§349. In the Municipal Court. 

In the municipal court, the demand for a trial by jury may be endorsed on the summons. Either party at the time of pleading or within 3 days after joinder of issue may demand a jury trial. A written demand, unless endorsement is made on the summons, must be filed with the clerk; and six dollars must be paid to the clerk upon making the demand. Unless such demand is made and the fee paid, a trial by jury is waived; but the court, in its discretion, may direct that a trial by jury be had, in which case the plaintiff

must pay the fee. (Mun. Ct. Code §§118; 173.) If one party has demanded a jury of six, the other party may, at least 2 days before trial, file a written demand for a jury of twelve. At the beginning of the trial he must pay the clerk six dollars. (Mun. Ct. Code §§120; 173.) §350. Number of Jurors. 

In the supreme, county and city courts, the jury is always composed of twelve men. In the municipal court, the jury is usually composed of six men, but in an action or special proceeding where the claim for damages or the value of chattels exceeds two hundred and fifty dollars, a jury of twelve may be had. In the latter case the fee is twelve dollars instead of six. (Mun. Ct. Code §§120; 173.) 

§351. Qualifications for Jury Duty. 

The general qualifications for a juror are given in §§598; 

600; 686, 687; 502 and 503 of the Judiciary Law. The general provisions for excusing a juror from service are given in §544 thereof. The qualifications and exemptions apply to the supreme, city and municipal courts. (Jud. Law, supra;

Mun. Ct. Code §121.) 

The objection that a juror does not have the qualifications required of every juryman before he can be put on the jury panel must be made at the time such juror is drawn and cannot be made after trial. People v. Cosmo, 205 N. Y. 91, 98 N. E. 408. 

§352. Pooling the Jurors. 

If the trial term of a court consists of more than one part, the jurors may be drawn for one part or several parts and be assigned therefrom to any part by the presiding judges. (Jud. L. §618.) This "pooling" of the jurors expedites jury trials and saves the time of the jurors. 

§353. Central Jury Part. 

In the municipal court for the Borough of Manhattan, all jury cases are tried at the court located at 30 West 35th

TRIAL 

209 Street. There is one calendar for tort cases; all others are placed on the "commercial" calendar. An effort to conciliate all cases on the commercial calendar must be made in Part VIII of the Central Jury Part before they are put on the calendar for trial. All actions are instituted in the proper districts as heretofore and remain there until a jury is demanded, the fee paid and notice of trial served and filed with proof of service with the clerk in the district. (Mun. 

Ct. Code §§7, subd. 3; 17.) §354. Selecting the Jury. 

According to the usual practice, slips of paper, each bearing the name of a juror on the term panel, are put in a box and drawn, one at a time, by the clerk. A struck jury (special panel) may be had, in the discretion of the court. (C. P. A. §449.) As their names are called, the jurors take their places in the jury box. When the required number has been called, counsel examine them as to their competency to sit on the jury in the case to be tried. 

The possible challenges are: (1) To the panel or array. (2) For principal cause. (3) For favor. (4) Peremptory (without giving reason). Each party has the right to six peremptory challenges in a court of record and three in a court not of record. (C. P. A. §§448; 450-453.) "The challenge for principal cause, asserts that there are facts, from which the law will say that the person proposed to sit as a juror, is not indifferent between the parties. If it is seen that a certain state of facts does exist, the law does ipso facto, declare that result." Greenfield v. People, 74 N. Y. 277, 281.

Where more than one defendant have a substantially common defense, the court will treat them as one defendant and allow only six peremptory challenges,

but if defendants are antagonistic, the court may allow six peremptory challenges for each defendant. Downey v. Finucane, 205 N. Y. 251, 98 N. E. 391. 

§355. Disqualification. 

A juror is disqualified from sitting on the jury in a case if he is related by consanguinity or affinity to any party within the sixth degree. The party related to the juror must raise the objection before the case is opened. Any other party may raise the objection six months after the verdict. 

(C. P. A. §455; Jud. L. §15.) §356. A Challenge to the Favor. 

A challenge to the favor may be based on the fact that a juror is an employee of a party, that he is a stockholder of a corporation which is a party; or, in actions for damages for injury to person or property, that he is a stockholder, director, officer or employee or interested in a casu alty insurance company. (C. P. A. §452.) 

Where a juror whose wife is related to a stockholder of a corporation defendant is challenged for favor, and held disqualified, it will be considered to have been for actual bias and within the sound discretion of the trial court. Butler v. Glens Falls R. R. Co., 121 N. Y. 112, 24 N. E. 187. 

§357. Copies of Papers. 

The duty is imposed on the plaintiff or the party moving the cause for trial to furnish the court with copies of the summons and pleadings and the offer of judgment, if any has been made. He must plainly designate on each pleading the parts thereof claimed to be admitted or controverted by the succeeding pleading. (Rule 160.) 

$358. The Opening Addresses. 

The party having the affirmative issues to maintain has the right to open and close. (Rule 161.) Usually the plain-

TRIAL 

211 tiff has the right, but if the defendant admits all the allegations in the complaint necessary to make out a cause of action and sets up an affirmative defense or counterclaim, he has the right to open and close. However, an oral admission at the trial does not give the right to the defendant to open and close; he "must frame his pleading with that view and so as to present no issue upon any allegation of the complaint essential to the plaintiff's alleged cause of action. If the defendant fail to do that, no matter how little proof the remaining issue may require, or how easily, or in what manner it may be established by evidence, the right of the plaintiff to open and close the case is not denied to him." (Lake 

Ontario National Bank v. Judson, 122 N. Y. 278, 25 N. E. 367.) 

§359. Motion to Dismiss. 

A motion to dismiss may be made after the plaintiff's opening address, but such dismissal is not on the merits. 

(C. P. A. §482.) $360. The Introduction of Evidence. 

After the opening addresses, the evidence is introduced. 

The evidence given at the trial may consist of (1) the oral testimony of witnesses; (2) the depositions of absent witnesses; and (3) documentary or other evidence. When depositions are read in evidence they have the same effect and are subject to the same objections as is oral testimony. 

(C. P. A. §§303-305; 366-402; Mun. Ct. Code §§115, 116.) 

Testimony in equity cases is taken as in actions at law.

(Const. Art. VI, §20.) 

The term evidence includes all the means by which any alleged matter of fact, the truth of which is submitted to investigation, is established or disproved. 

Dibble v. Dimick, 143 N. Y. 549, 38 N. E. 724. 

4 See Chapter VIII.

§361. Swearing the Witnesses. 

If the testimony of a witness is taken orally, an oath is usually administered before the testimony is taken. If the witness declares he has conscientious objections to taking the oath, he may make an affirmation. If the witness is not a Christian, he may be sworn according to the ceremony of his religion. The court may examine an infant witness or a witness of weak intellect to ascertain his capacity and knowledge, and may inquire of such witness what manner of swearing he deems most obligatory. (C. P. A. §§360-365.) §362. Examining the Witnesses. 

Each witness for the plaintiff is first examined by the plaintiff's attorney. This is called the direct examination. 

The defendant's attorney then cross-examines such witness, confining his questions to matters brought out on the direct examination. One counsel only on each side may examine or cross-examine a particular witness. (Rule 161.) 

§363. Exceptions. 

If at any time during the examination of a witness by an opponent, counsel wishes to object to a question, he should make his objection before the witness answers. If the witness does answer, counsel may move to strike out the answer. If the court does not sustain the objection or grant the motion to strike out, counsel should take an exception, otherwise any error in the court's ruling may not be preserved for purposes of an appeal. For the same reason, an exception should be taken to every ruling of the court, e. g., on a motion or on a request to instruct the jury, to which an objection may be made. An exception should be written or entered on the minutes. (C. P. A. §§444; 446.) The jury, in the discretion of the court, may 

See pp. 267, 268, 273.

TRIAL 

213 be excluded from the courtroom during the argument of an objection. (Rule 164.) $364. Depositions as Evidence.

A deposition read in evidence has the same effect as oral testimony. A deposition taken within the state, except that of a party taken at the instance of an adverse party or a deposition taken on stipulation, may not be read in evidence unless it appears to the satisfaction of the court that the deponent is: (1) dead; (2) without the state; (3) more than one hundred miles from the place of the trial; or that (4) by reason of insanity, sickness, or other infirmity, or imprisonment, he is unable to travel and appear at the court; or that (5) for some reason his attendance cannot be compelled by subpoena, with the exercise of reasonable diligence. A deposition taken without the state may be read in evidence unless an order for its suppression has been granted. (C. P. A. §§304; 314, 315; 321.)

Either party may offer such deposition in evidence but it can be used only against a party who was duly notified of or who consented by stipulation to the taking of the deposition, or against his predecessor in interest, or against a party who has not appeared or answered. (C. P. A. §§303; 321.) 

Where the right appears, testimony by deposition may be taken on notice during trial. (C. P. A. §293.) 

§365. Variance. 

If a party has been mistaken in the relief appropriate to the facts as established, and the proper relief is within the jurisdiction of the court, the pleadings may be amended on trial. (C. P. A. §111.) If the facts have not been pleaded properly an amendment may be granted. (Rule 166.) If it appears that the proof is defective, but can be produced, the judge may adjourn the trial or direct a new trial, in his

discretion. (Rule 166.) If variance between proof and pleading has actually misled the adversary to his prejudice, amendment cannot be allowed. (C. P. A. §434.) 

Where conspiracy to deprive plaintiff of his commissions, as alleged in the complaint, was not proved, but there was sufficient evidence to go to the jury on the theory of a breach of a contract to pay commissions, the court should have permitted an amendment to conform to the proof. Hornstein v. Podwitz, 226 

App. Div. 53, 234 N. Y. S. 429. 

§366. Judgment during Trial. 

Judgment or partial judgment may be rendered at any stage of an action, if warranted by the pleadings or admis sions of any party. (C. P. A. §476.) 

§367. Withdrawing a Juror. 

If counsel finds on trial that he is surprised and his cause prejudiced by the testimony or conduct of a witness, the conduct or remarks of his adversary, or by some other occurrence at the trial, he may submit to a nonsuit, which is not on the merits, and try the case on a future date. Since the jury in the supreme court must be composed of twelve men, the trial cannot proceed with fewer and the nonsuit desired may be obtained by moving to withdraw a juror. 

The motion is to the discretion of the court and, if he is satisfied that the reason offered is a proper one, he will grant the motion. If the motion is granted, a juror is asked to step out of the jury box, a mistrial is declared and the effect is the same as if the trial had never been started. 

The application must be made before the case is submitted to the jury. (C. P. A. §457; Pirrung v. Supreme Council, 104 App. Div. 571, 93 N. Y. S. 575.)

Where a party relied upon a stipulation as to the facts in the case, and was surprised by the introduction of oral testimony, he could move to withdraw a juror. 

Dillon v. Cockcroft, 90 N. Y. 649.

TRIAL 

215 Improper remarks of counsel tending to prejudice the jury may be made the basis of a motion to withdraw a juror. Walsh v. Frankenthaler, 186 App. Div. 

62, 173 N. Y. S. 764; Bloodgood v. Whitney, 200 App. 

Div. 56, 192 N. Y. S. 383. "Evidence that the defendant in an action for negligence was insured in a casualty company, or that the defence was conducted by an insurance company, is incompetent and so dangerous as to require a reversal, even when the court strikes it from the record and directs the jury to disregard it, unless it clearly appears that it could not have influenced the verdict." Simpson v. Foundation Co., 201 N. Y. 479, 490, 95 N. E. 10. 

However, counsel is not precluded from asking a juror on the voir dire examination if he is interested in such casualty company. Blair v. McCormack Const. 

Co., 123 App. Div. 30, 107 N. Y. S. 750, aff'd. 195 N. Y. 

520, 88 N. E. 1114.

In an action on a burglary insurance policy, remarks by plaintiff's attorney to the jury, tending to prejudice the jury against defendant's case, and using arguments not supported by proof, held sufficient grounds upon which to grant a motion for a mistrial. Cherry 

Creek Nat. Bank v. Fidelity & Casualty Co., 207 App. 

Div. 787, 202 N. Y. S. 611. 

§368. Contempt of Court. 

The dignity and orderliness of the trial is safeguarded by provisions of the Judiciary Law providing for the punishment of civil and criminal contempt. $369. Civil Contempts.

Civil contempts include: 

(1) Misbehavior in office or wilful neglect or violation of a duty, or disobedience to a lawful mandate of the court or judge by an attorney or court officer. (2) Putting in fictitious bail or surety or for deceit or abuse of a mandate by a party. (3) For disobedience to a mandate by any person.

(4) For interfering with officers of the court or the attendance, etc., of witnesses. (5) Refusal to attend, be sworn, or answer, by a witness. (6) Improper conversing concerning the merits of the action or proceeding by a juror without immediately disclosing the same to the court. (7) Proceeding contrary to law in a case removed from his jurisdiction, by a judge of an inferior court. (Jud. L. §753.)

The contempt may be punished summarily when committed in the view and presence of the court. Otherwise an order to show cause or a warrant of attachment commanding the sheriff to arrest the person is necessary. After hearing, if the accused is found guilty, he may be punished by fine equal to the loss sustained by a party; or, if it cannot be ascertained, a maximum of $250 plus costs and expenses to the aggrieved party; or imprisonment or both. (Jud. L. §§755, 756, 757; 770; 772; C. P. A. §406.) The provisions of §753, except subd. 7, apply to the municipal court. (Mun. Ct. Code §13.) 

"Any person who interferes with the process, control or action of the court in a pending litigation, unlawfully and without authority, is guilty of civil contempt if his act defeats, impairs, impedes or prejudices the right or remedy of a party to such action or proceeding." King v. Barnes, 113 N. Y. 476, 479, 21 N. E. 

182. And such person must pay, to the injured party, the fine imposed by the court or submit to imprisonment. In re Cornblum, 133 Misc. 357, 232 N. Y. S. 22. 

§370. Criminal Contempts. 

Criminal contempts are: (1) Disorderly, contemptuous, or insolent behavior, committed during the sitting of the court, in its immediate view and presence, and directly tending to interrupt its proceedings, or to impair the respect due to its authority.

TRIAL 

217 (2) Breach of the peace, noise or other disturbance, directly tending to interrupt its proceedings. (3) Wilful disobedience to its lawful mandate. (4) Resistance wilfully offered to its lawful mandate. (5) Contumacious and unlawful refusal to be sworn as a witness; or, after being sworn, to answer any legal and proper interrogatory. (6) Publication of a false, or grossly inaccurate report of its proceedings. But the court cannot punish as a contempt, the publication of a true, full and fair report of a trial, argument, decision or other proceeding therein. (Jud. 

L. §750.) These contempts may be punished by a fine not exceeding $250, or by imprisonment, not exceeding thirty days, or both. When the contempt is committed in the view and presence of the court, punishment may be summary. 

Otherwise notice and a reasonable time to defend are re quired. (Jud. L. §751; Mun. Ct. Code §13.) §371. Plaintiff's Case. 

When the plaintiff has introduced all his evidence, he rests his case. If he has alleged two inconsistent causes of action in his complaint, he may be compelled to elect between them at the end of his case. 

The plaintiffs can be compelled to elect, at the close of their case, between rescission of the contract and damages for breach of the contract. Richard v. American Union Bank, 222 App. Div. 115, 225 N. Y. S. 696.

If a cause of action in negligence and a cause of action upon breach of contract are merged in a complaint and not separately stated and numbered, the plaintiff may be compelled to elect between them before going to the jury. If he elects the action in contract, the allegations of negligence may be treated as surplusage. Jersey Silk & Lace Stores v. Best Silk Shops, 134 Misc. 315, 235 N. Y. S. 277.

Plaintiff is not required to elect between inconsistent causes of action before the close of the evidence. Irving

Trust Co. v. Reikes, 228 App. Div. 510, 240 N. Y. S. 232. 

§372. Motion to Dismiss. 

At the close of the plaintiff's case, the defendant moves to dismiss the action on the ground that the plaintiff has not proved a cause of action. A dismissal of the action before the end of plaintiff's evidence does not bar a new action for the same cause unless the judgment specifically declares that it is rendered on the merits. A dismissal of a complaint at the end of the plaintiff's evidence is a final determination of the merits and bars a new action for the same cause unless the court dismisses without prejudice. 

(C. P. A. §482.) 

If, after moving to dismiss at the close of plaintiff's evidence and excepting to a denial of such motion, the defendant puts in evidence on his part, he thereby waives the exception and the denial cannot be assigned as error. The motion to dismiss should be made again at the close of all the evidence. Hopkins v. Clark, 158 

N. Y. 299, 53 N. E. 27. 

§373. Defendant's Case. 

If the motion to dismiss is not granted, the defendant preserves his exception to the denial by resting his case, or he waives it and introduces evidence on his side. The same rules apply as in the plaintiff's case. 

§374. Motions at the Close of the Evidence. 

When all the evidence is in, the defendant moves to dismiss the complaint or for a direction of a verdict in his favor. The motion raises the question as to whether, admitting all facts presented and giving the plaintiff the advantage of every inference that can properly be drawn from them, an issue of fact is presented for determination by the jury. The plaintiff may make the same motions on a cause of action in a counterclaim. A dismissal of a counterclaim

TRIAL 

219 at the close of the defendant's evidence, or a dismissal of the complaint or counterclaim at the close of the whole evidence, is a final determination on the merits and bars a new action for the same cause, unless the court dismisses without prejudice. (C. P. A. §482.)

By failing to move for the dismissal of the complaint at the close of the entire case, the defendants concede that there is a question of fact for the jury. 

Atkinson v. Sanders and Dotin, 250 N. Y. 170, 164 N. 

E. 894. §375. Motion for a Directed Verdict.

The judge may direct a verdict when he would set aside a contrary verdict as against the weight of evidence. (C. P. 

A. §457-a.) If he does direct a verdict, the jury finds as he directs and the trial is at an end. When only questions of law are presented, the judge may direct that the jury render a verdict subject to the opinion of the court. The judge may then accept the verdict or set it aside and direct that judgment be entered for either party. (C. P. A. §461.) 

§376. When Both Parties Move for a Directed Verdict. 

When both parties move for a directed verdict, and neither motion is withdrawn, the case is taken out of the jury's hands and submitted to the court for determination. 

When this occurs, the court must give counsel opportunity to withdraw his motion and substitute a motion that the case be submitted to the jury.

The effect of a request by each party for a directed verdict in his favor is to transfer the functions of the jury to the court. If the party whose request is denied does not thereupon request to go to the jury on the facts, a verdict directed for the adversary stands as would the finding of a jury. Thompson v. Simpson, 128 

N. Y. 270, 28 N. E. 627. "Although there was a verdict in form, in reality the finding was by the court. 

The verdict was not di-

rected by the court within the true meaning of the Constitution, but by the consent of both parties the court decided the question as one of fact, and the verdict simply expressed the result, which was a finding both of fact and also of law, by the court. 

The jury was merely the mouthpiece of the court to announce its decision." Westervelt v. Phelps, 171 N. Y. 

212, 219, 63 N. E. 962. Defendant's motion to dismiss and plaintiff's request for a directed verdict operate as a submission to the court. Dillon v. Cockcroft, 90 N. Y. 649. .Where both parties ask for a directed verdict, and before the court directs a verdict in plaintiff's favor, the defendant may request that the issues of fact be submitted to the jury. Koehler v. Adler, 78 N. Y. 287.

The action of a court in directing a verdict for plaintiff, without first ruling on defendant's motion for directed verdict and affording him opportunity to ask to go to jury, was reversible error in view of the verdict rendered, notwithstanding that the case was submitted to the jury later. Happel v. Lehigh Valley R. Co., 210 

App. Div. 461, 206 N. Y. S. 726; Brown Paint Co. v. 

Reinhardt, 210 N. Y. 162, 104 N. E. 124; O'Connor v. 

Weber, 239 N. Y. 191, 146 N. E. 200. 

§377. Summing Up. 

If the case is not disposed of by the court, counsel make their final addresses to the jury, the one having the affirmative addressing them last. Only one counsel on each side may sum up. (Rule 161.)

The summation is a material part of the trial. The absence of the judge during the summation requires a new trial. Ciccolini v. Vocafilm Corporation, 227 App. 

Div. 736, 236 N. Y. S. 577. 

§378. Instructing the Jury. 

The judge then "charges" or "instructs" the jury as to the law applicable to the case at bar. Counsel may make requests to charge on particular points.

TRIAL 

221 

§379. Deliberation of the Jury. 

After being instructed, the jury retires to a private room to deliberate. If the jury cannot agree upon a verdict after a reasonable length of time, it may be discharged by the court and a new jury may be impaneled. The same proceedings must then be had as if the new jury were the first impaneled. (C. P. A. §463.) 

The right of a party to have the trial of his case in open court with an opportunity to be present is a substantial right; and where the jury, after they left the courtroom to consider their verdict, sent a written question to the judge and he sent back a written answer, without the knowledge and consent of the party, such right was violated and the judgment will be set aside and a new trial granted. Watertown Bank & Loan Co. v. Mix, 51 N. Y. 558.

Where the court's instruction to the jury, upon their return for further instructions and in the absence of the plaintiffs, was erroneous and adverse to the plaintiffs, the error is available on appeal without exception, since the plaintiffs had no opportunity to interpose a formal exception. Wheeler v. Sweet, 137 N. Y. 435, 33 N. E. 483. 

§380. The Verdict." 

The jury usually renders a general verdict which is defined as one by which it "pronounces generally upon all or any of the issues in favor either of the plaintiff or of the defendant." By a special verdict "the jury finds the facts only, leaving the court to determine which party is entitled to judgment thereon." (C. P. A. §458.) In actions to recover a sum of money only or real property or a chattel, the jury has discretion to render a special verdict. (C. P. A. $459.) When double, treble, or other increased damages are given by statute, the verdict must specify a sum as single damages and judgment will be entered for the increased • See Chapter X for making up the judgment roll and entering judgment.

damages. (C. P. A. §435.) The plaintiff in a civil action in a court of record need not be present when the jury delivers its verdict. (C. P. A. §462.) 

When, by mistake the verdict as announced is different from that agreed upon by the jury, the error may be corrected and affidavits of the jurors as to their true finding may be received. Such affidavits do not impeach or reverse the verdict, but establish it. Dalrymple v. 

Williams, 63 N. Y. 361. 

§381. Motions after Verdict. 

The judge may now entertain a motion, made on his minutes, to set aside the verdict and for a new trial upon the exceptions previously taken or because the verdict is excessive or insufficient, or otherwise contrary to the evi dence or contrary to law. (C. P. A. §549.) The stenog rapher's minutes, written out in full, may be treated as the judge's minutes, in his discretion. (Rule 223.) "The right to move for a nonsuit does not exist after verdict, but when a motion is made before the verdict, and the decision thereof expressly reserved by the court, with the consent of the parties until after the verdict, the court may then, notwithstanding the verdict, render its decision upon the motion and cause judgment to be entered thereon." Bail v. N. Y., N. H. & H. R. R. Co., 201 N. Y. 355, 357, 94 N. E. 863. 

The trial judge may not dismiss the complaint after verdict unless he reserved decision on the defendant's motion to dismiss or for direction of a verdict. He may only set aside the verdict and grant a new trial. Dougherty v. Salt, 227 N. Y. 200, 125 N. E. 94. 

§382. Motions for a New Trial Generally. 

If the motion for a new trial is not made immediately after reception of the verdict, it may be made before the judge before whom the case was tried at any time during the term. (C. P. A. §549.) On permission of such judge, and if exceptions were taken by the moving party, the

TRIAL 

223 motion may be heard in the proper appellate court. (C. P.

A. §550.) In a case not specified in §§549 and 550 (see next paragraph), a motion for a new trial should be heard at special term in the first instance. If it is founded on an allegation of error made by the judge on the trial in a finding of fact or conclusion of law, it must be noticed within the time given to appeal and heard before the same judge, unless he is incapacitated or disqualified. (C. P. A. §552.) 

§383. Other Grounds for a New Trial. 

A motion for a new trial may be based on: (1) Surprise as to a question of fact which appeared on the trial. (2) Disqualification of a juror.' 

(3) Misconduct of the jury. 

(4) Newly discovered evidence.

When the ground is that a juror was related to another party, the objection must be raised within six months of the date of the verdict. In other cases the motion must be made within a reasonable length of time. (C. P. A. §455.)

The granting of a motion for a new trial on the ground of newly discovered evidence is largely in the discretion of the court. Evidence that merely discredits the opponent's witness is insufficient for this purpose. 

It must appear that the evidence could not have been obtained with due diligence, before the first trial; and such evidence must be of such a character and be so positive and convincing that it will probably produce a different result, if a new trial is granted. Collins v. 

Central Trust Company, 226 App. Div. 486, 235 N. Y. 

S. 511, and cases cited.

The affidavits to be used on a motion for a new trial because of newly discovered evidence must be made by the proposed witnesses and not by the party or his attorney. Collins v. Central Trust Co., 135 Misc. 

465, 238 N. Y. S. 226. 

7 See pp. 208, 210.

§384. In the Municipal Court. 

In the municipal court, a motion to set aside a verdict and to vacate or modify a judgment, and for a new trial, must be made at the close of the trial or within 20 days after entry of judgment. A motion for a new trial on the ground of fraud or newly discovered evidence must be made with due diligence and, in any event, not later than one year after entry of judgment. (Mun. Ct. Code §129.) 

§385. Trial without a Jury. 

Trial by court without a jury is had in equity causes or when a jury trial has been waived. There can be no motion to direct a verdict, charge, or request to charge, nor are the rules of evidence applied as strictly as in jury trials.

An exception to a ruling on a question of law, taken after submission of the case in an action tried by a referee or the court without a jury, must be taken by filing notice of the exception in the clerk's office and serving a copy thereof on the attorney for the adverse party. Such exception may be taken within 10 days of the service upon the exceptant's attorney of a copy of the report or decision and notice of entry of judgment. (C. P. A. §445.) Where an interlocutory judgment is entered, the time within which such exception may be taken for use on motion for a new trial, is 10 days after service of notice of such entry. (C. P. 

A. §447.) 

§386. Statement of the Case. 

Before the case is submitted to the court, or within a time thereafter fixed by the court, an attorney for a party may submit a written statement of facts which he deems established and rulings upon questions of law which he desires the court to find. Each proposition must be stated separately and numbered. The court must note in the margin of such statement the disposal of each proposition and

TRIAL 

225 return or file the statement, but an omission to do so does not affect the validity of the decision. A refusal to find is the basis for an exception. (C. P. A. §439.) 

§387. The Decision. 

In entering a judgment, the decision in the supreme court "must state separately the facts found and the conclusions of law and direct a judgment to be entered thereon." (C. P. A. §440.) In the city court, it is sufficient if the decision directs judgment to be entered, unless a party requires that it state separately the facts found and the con clusions of law. (City Ct. Act §24.) 

Where a trial is before a court, no judgment can be entered until the court has made and filed, as required by $440, a decision disposing of the issues and directing the entry of judgment. Neither an opinion of the court directing a judgment in defendant's favor, nor an entry in the clerk's minutes to the effect that a verdict was directed for the defendant can take the place of the formal decision required by the Civil Prac tice Act. Electric Boat Co. v. Howey, 96 App. Div. 410, 

89 N. Y. S. 210.

Courts are required to pass on requests to find when submitted by either party, and the statement must be in the form of distinct propositions of law or fact, separately stated and numbered, and it is insufficient to merely mark the proposed findings as "found" and others as "refused" without making a decision as to what facts are found and the conclusion of law therefrom. Minner v. Minner, 238 N. Y. 529, 144 N. E. 781.

A decision is essential when dismissal is on the merits. Murphy v. Murphy, 223 App. Div. 701, 226 N. 

Y. S. 523. "A decision of a court upon the trial of an issue of fact or of law must be in writing, signed by the trial judge, and must state separately the facts found and the conclusions of law, or must state concisely the grounds upon which the issues have been decided and direct judgment to be entered thereon." If such decision is not made, a judgment of dismissal is a nonsuit.

Ware v. Dos Passos, 162 N. Y. 281, 283, 56 N. E. 742. 

See also Nelson v. Ryan, 222 App. Div. 754, 225 N. Y.

S. 338. 

Facts found by the court upon request are for the consideration of the appellate courts, even though they are not incorporated in the formal decision. Elterman v. Hyman, 192 N. Y. 113, 84 N. E. 937; Bremer v. Manhattan Ry. Co., 191 N. Y. 333, 84 N. E. 59; Barcalo Mfg. 

Co. v. Maldonado & Co., 221 N. Y. 499, 116 N. E. 345. 

§388. A Motion for a New Trial. 

The grounds for a new trial are the same as in a trial by jury, except for those which are inherently peculiar to a jury trial. An additional ground is given, i. e., the failure of the court to file its decision within 60 days. This motion is made at special term. (C. P. A. §442.) If a decision of the municipal court is not rendered within 14 days, the case is put on the general calendar. (Mun. Ct. Code §119.) 

A motion for a new trial may also be made in the appellate court after an interlocutory judgment entered after trial or an issue of fact by a court without a jury or by a referee. 

(C. P. A. §551.) §389. Trial by Referee. 

A trial is had before a referee as of right where the parties consent thereto, except in matrimonial actions, actions against a corporation for dissolution, and actions where the defendant is an infant. In these cases where the parties consent, the court may grant or refuse a reference; and when granted, it must designate the referee. (C. P. A. 

§§464, 465.) $390. Compulsory Reference. 

Compulsory reference to hear and determine the issues may be ordered where the trial will require the examination 

8 For reference of undefended matrimonial actions, see p. 296.

TRIAL 

227 of a long account on either side and will not require the decision of difficult questions of law. (C. P. A. §466; City

Ct. Act §23.) The court may also direct a reference to take an account and report after interlocutory or final judgment or where necessary for the court's information, or to determine and report upon a question of fact arising upon a motion or otherwise, except upon the pleadings. (C. P. A. 

§467; City Ct. Act §22.) 

In order to uphold a compulsory order of reference on the ground that the trial will require the examination of a long account, the facts must be disclosed by affidavit or upon the face of the pleadings, from which the conclusion can fairly be drawn that so many separate and distinct items of account will be litigated on the trial that a jury cannot keep the evidence in mind in regard to each of the items and give it the proper weight and application when they retire to deliberate upon their verdict. Spence v. Simis, 137 N. Y. 616, 33 

N. E. 554. 

§391. Qualifications of the Referee. 

The referee must be above objection by either party, and, in the case of a sole referee, must be a lawyer. (Rule 

172.) The powers of a referee are similar to those of a judge during a trial. He may be appointed to take testimony only or to hear testimony and render an opinion. 

(C. P. A. §469.) §392. Notice of Trial by Referee. 

The trial of an issue of fact before a referee is brought on by notice of trial and filing note of issue. (C. P. A. §469.) 

Incidental references under §467 are brought on by summons to attend.

Since there are no special statutory rules for the incidental reference, the referee in these cases is a substitute for the master in chancery, and the old chancery practice applies. Palmer v. Palmer, 13 How. Pr. 363.

$393. Report of Referee. 

Unless the referee renders his report within 60 days, either party may give notice that he elects to end the ref erence. (C. P. A. §470.) §394. In the Municipal Court. 

The president justice of the municipal court of the city of New York may direct an official referee of such court to take testimony by deposition, to determine and report upon a question of fact arising on a motion, and also to try the issues on a long account; and upon consent of all parties in an action triable by the court without a jury to decide the whole issue or any of the issues, or to report such referee's finding upon specific questions of fact involved in the issue. (Mun. Ct. Code §7, subd. 10.)

CHAPTER X JUDGMENT §395. Nature of a Judgment. 

1 A judgment is the termination of the rights of the parties in an action and may be either interlocutory or final. (C. P. A. §472.) An interlocutory judgment is a temporary determination of the rights and liabilities of the parties and remains in force only until final judgment is entered in the action.

A judgment in action to foreclose the equity of redemption in mortgaged premises, directing the sale of the premises for the satisfaction of the debt, and that defendant pay any deficiency appearing after such sale, is final, and not interlocutory merely. It leaves nothing further to be done, except ministerial acts. 

Morris v. Morange, 38 N. Y. 172.

Where the party obtaining an interlocutory judgment is entitled to a final judgment, he may obtain the latter on motion; or, in case of the default of the defendant, he may be directed to have the judgment settled 2 by a judge or referee. (Rules 187, 188.) 

§396. Parties to the Judgment. 

Judgment may be given for or against one or more plaintiffs and for or against one or more defendants. It may determine the ultimate rights of the parties on the same side, as between themselves; and it may grant affirmative relief to a defendant.
